The Lipschitz Spinor-Higher Horosphere Correspondence
Orion Zymaris

Abstract
. In a paper of Mathews [ 23 ] , an isomorphism is constructed between two-component complex spinors and horospheres in ℍ 3 \mathbb{H}^{3} carrying ‘spin decorations’. A recent arXiv preprint of Mathews and Varsha [ 21 ] extends this result to the case of ‘quaternionic spinors’ and spin decorated horospheres in ℍ 4 \mathbb{H}^{4} . The following work generalises these results to an equivariant correspondence between two-component ‘Lipschitz spinors’ with entries drawn from the Lipschitz group of a Clifford algebra, null multiflags in generalised Minkowski space, and higher-dimensional horospheres that carry an extension of the Mathews spin decoration. This correspondence allows spinors to be applied to horospheres in any dimension of hyperbolic space. A generalisation of Mathews’ complex lambda lengths to arbitrary dimension is also given, along with some relations on these lambda lengths. In particular, the noncommutative hyperbolic Ptolemy relation of [ 21 ] is extended to arbitrary dimension. This paper is adapted from the author’s thesis, available at https://doi.org/10.26180/31361224 .



0.
Introduction

0.1.
Background

Though a thorough treatment of the groups underlying spinors and spin structures was carried out in 1913 by Élie Cartan
[
7
]
in his work on projective groups (which would now be called projective representations of groups), the significance of the spinor was best understood by the early pioneers of quantum mechanics.
1
1

1

The term ‘spinor’ first appears in a paper of the physicist van der Waerden
[
34
]
, who attributes it to Paul Ehrenfest.
Groundbreaking work of Pauli
[
24
]
and Dirac
[
9
]
in the late 1920’s made use of spinors and matrix representations of certain spin groups to describe the quantum phenomenon of ‘spin’ and introduce relativistic effects into the bourgeoning field of quantum mechanics. In the realm of pure mathematics, spinors and spinor bundles would become an important tool in geometry and topology, helping to answer questions about the existence or non-existence of certain structures on manifolds or the index of elliptic operators (
[
16
]
is a standard reference). And, across both physics and mathematics, maybe the most important variety of spinor is the two-component complex spinor
(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

Since its introduction the two-component complex spinor has been thoroughly explored, its properties mapped and collated. These spinors carry representations of Spin
(3)
and Spin
(1,3)
as the groups
SU(2)
and
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
respectively, two of the low-dimensional exceptional isomorphisms between spin groups and certain matrix groups. There are many other representations of the spin groups (including other representations of Spin
(3)
and Spin
(1,3)
), but these
2\times 2
matrix groups and associated two-component complex spinors continue to be among the most fruitful. This raises a question; can this two-component form be extended to represent other spin groups?

It can indeed. To do this, we make a shift; by considering two-component complex spinors as projective coordinates on the Riemann sphere
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
, the action of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
becomes an action by Möbius transformations on
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
.
2
2

2

This perspective is well-known; see, for example,
[
27
]
.
Results about generalised Möbius transformations in higher dimensions are already known; the machinery for generalised Möbius transformations was introduced by Vahlen
[
31
]
, forgotten, rediscovered by Fueter
[
11
]
,
[
12
]
, forgotten again, and rediscovered by Maass
[
20
]
. Here we make use of the more refined formulation found in work of Ahlfors (
[
1
]
,
[
3
]
), and built upon by others such as Cao and Waterman
[
6
]
, Waterman
[
35
]
, Lounesto and Latvamaa
[
18
]
, and Wada
[
33
]
.

In this formulation, the vector space
\mathbb{C}
and its compactification
\widehat{\mathbb{C}}
are generalised to the paravector spaces
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
(with
1
real component and
n
complex components) and their compactifications
\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
. Two-component objects (the
Lipschitz spinors
) that provide a form of ‘projective coordinates’ for
\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
are defined; these Lipschitz spinors draw their entries from the
Lipschitz groups

\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, which in turn are drawn from the Clifford algebras
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
that govern the isometries of
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

Generalisations
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
of the special linear groups are also defined, again with entries drawn from the Lipschitz groups
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. These matrices act on Lipschitz spinors by matrix multiplication, or they can equivalently be considered as the components of Möbius transformations over
\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
. This results in generalised two-component spinors that carry representations of the higher spin groups Spin
(1,n+2)
as matrix subgroups of
\mathcal{M}(2,\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n})
.

A key property of spinors is the subtle nature of their geometric interpretation; they carry representations not just of the rotation groups
SO(p,q)
but of their double covers
Spin(p,q)
. In many cases
3
3

3

In the case of indefinite signature the covering maps are more complicated.
this leads to an action that is locally a rotation, but is globally well defined up to ‘rotation’ by
4\pi
. The standard geometric construction, a way to present the spinor as coordinates of some geometric object, is the null flag due to Penrose and Rindler
[
27
]
; in this picture, a flag structure is constructed on the light-cone in
\mathbb{R}^{1,3}
. They also offer an interpretation of a spinor as a tangent vector to the sphere at infinity, which is modelled as the space of rays of the light-cone.

A recent paper of Mathews
[
23
]
composes this null flag picture with some work of Penner
4
4

4

Penner’s construction is in
\mathbb{R}^{1,2}
, but it generalises neatly to higher dimensions.

[
25
]
,
[
26
]
. Penner provides a map from points of the light-cone in
\mathbb{R}^{1,3}
to horospheres in the hyperboloid model of hyperbolic space that sits within the light-cone; combined with Penrose and Rindler’s picture, the result is an equivariant and bijective correspondence between complex two-component spinors and
spin decorated
horospheres in 3-dimensional hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{3}
. A
spin decoration
is an extra structure defined in the tangent space of a horosphere, essentially a choice of orthonormal basis for the tangent space which is then lifted to the spin double cover of the orthonormal frame bundle.

The space of complex spinors has a natural symplectic form, and Mathews shows this form has a geometric interpretation as a generalisation of Penner’s
lambda length
between horocycles. A hyperbolic form of the Ptolemy relation between lambda lengths in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
is also given. In a later paper
[
21
]
, Mathews and Varsha extend this correspondence to one between spin decorated horospheres in
\mathbb{H}^{4}
and two-component spinors with quaternionic entries.

0.2.
Key Results

This work extends the Mathews spinor-horosphere correspondence to the case of arbitrary dimensional hyperbolic space. To be precise, an equivariant correspondence between Lipschitz spinors
(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
and decorated horospheres
5
5

5

Extension to include spin is discussed in Section
4
.
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
is given explicitly in the upper half-space model. Generalisations to
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
of the symplectic and Hermitian forms on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
are also defined.

Theorem

(Explicit Spinor-Horosphere Correspondence)
.

The spinor
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
maps under
\Phi
to a horosphere decorated with
n
parallel oriented line fields (labelled
i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{n}
) and centred at
\xi\eta^{-1}
in the upper half-space model (taken to be
\infty
if
\eta=0
).

•

If
\eta\neq 0
the horosphere has Euclidean diameter
|\eta|^{-2}
, and the north pole specification for the
i_{j}
direction field is
\eta^{{}^{\prime}}i_{j}\overline{\eta}
.

•

If
\eta=0
then the horosphere has Euclidean height
|\xi|^{2}
and the
i_{j}
-direction field is specified by
\xi i_{j}\xi^{*}
.

In Section
4
, we lift our objects to their spin covers and recover the full correspondence. As we use the generalised Minkowski space to mediate between the space of Lipschitzz spinors and hyperbolic space, we also have a certain null flag structure
\widetilde{\mathcal{MF}}^{n}
appearing in the correspondence.

Theorem

(Generalised Spinor-Horosphere Correspondence)
.

There is an explicit, smooth, bijective,

SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
equivariant correspondence between the following:

i)

Lipschitz spinors
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
,

ii)

Spin multiflags
\widetilde{\mathcal{MF}}^{n}
,

iii)

The space
S
Hor
n+2
of spin decorated horospheres in
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
.

The real and complex lambda lengths of Penner
[
25
]
and Mathews
[
23
]
are extended to a generalised Clifford-algebra-valued lambda length. These generalised lambda lengths relate to a generalisation of the symplectic form on complex spinors (we name this generalised form
the bracket
).

Theorem

(Algebra of Lambda Lengths)
.

The lambda length between two spin decorated horospheres with spinor coordinates
\kappa_{1}=(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})
and
\kappa_{2}=(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})
is given by taking their bracket. Properly,

\lambda_{12}=\{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\}=\xi_{1}^{*}\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}^{*}\xi_{2}.

These lambda lengths also satisfy the noncommutative Ptolemy relation of
[
21
]
, alongside other interesting properties.

Theorem

(Noncommutative Ptolemy Relations)
.

Given four spin decorated horospheres
(H_{j},W_{j})
,
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
with lambda lengths
\lambda_{jk}
between
(H_{j},W_{j})
,
(H_{k},W_{k})
, their lambda lengths satisfy

\lambda_{31}^{-1}\lambda_{23}^{*}\lambda_{42}^{-1}\lambda_{14}^{*}+\lambda_{31}^{-1}\lambda_{43}^{*}\lambda_{24}^{-1}\lambda_{12}^{*}=1.

1.
Clifford Algebras, Lipschitz Spinors, and Special Linear Groups

In
[
1
]
, Ahlfors reintroduces a method due to Vahlen
[
31
]
, Maass
[
20
]
and others to construct the isometry groups of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
as
2\times 2
matrix groups with entries drawn from the Lipschitz group
6
6

6

Also referred to as the Clifford group; we’ll exclusively use the term Lipschitz group
[
17
]
.
of a particular Clifford algebra (introduced in
[
8
]
). These matrices act on the boundary of the upper half-space model as the components of Möbius transformations which extend to isometries of the interior in the standard way, by considering their action on the endpoints of geodesics. The actions of these groups are precisely what we need to generalise the spinor-horosphere correspondence of
[
23
]
and
[
21
]
.

1.1.
Clifford Algebras

The conventions and definitions for Clifford algebras in this work are drawn from Lawson and Michelson’s ‘Spin Geometry’
[
16
]
, and Lounesto’s ‘Clifford Algebras and Spinors’
[
19
]
. Some other helpful resources are
[
5
]
and
[
29
]
.

There are several ways to define a Clifford algebra, but for our purposes it is best defined explicitly in terms of a concrete basis. Consider the vector spaces
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
with the associated quadratic form
Q_{p,q}=x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+...+x_{p}^{2}-x_{p+1}^{2}-...-x_{p+q}^{2}
.

Definition 1.1

(Clifford Algebra)
.

Take the real vector space
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
with basis
i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{p+q}
and norm
N_{p,q}
(with the standard polarisation
N_{p,q}(\cdot,\cdot)
) such that
i_{1},...,i_{p}
are of norm
1
and
i_{p+1},...,i_{p+q}
are of norm
-1
. The Clifford algebra
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
over
\mathbb{R}
is the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra generated by monomials in the
i_{j}
subject to the relations

\displaystyle i_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=1,\ 1\leq j\leq p,

\displaystyle i_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=-1,\ p+1\leq j\leq p+q,

\lrcorner

\displaystyle i_{j}i_{k}

\displaystyle=-i_{k}i_{j},\ k\neq j.

There is a standard basis of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
consisting of all
2^{p+q}
possible products
i_{j_{1}}i_{j_{2}}...i_{j_{k}}
for
k\leq p+q
and
j_{1}<j_{2}...<j_{k}
. As we might have products of many
i_{j}
, in general we write
i_{I}
for a multi-index
I=\{j_{1},j_{2},...,j_{k}\}
to indicate the product
i_{j_{1}}i_{j_{2}}...i_{j_{k}}
. Elements
x\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
can then be written explicitly in terms of the standard basis as
x=\sum_{I\subseteq\{1,2,...,n\}}x_{I}i_{I}
, where
x_{I}\in\mathbb{R}
. Elements of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
will generally be notated with a lowercase Latin character.

An element
x\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
is
reduced
if it is written in terms of the standard basis; that is, if its monomials have indices in increasing order and it does not contain monomials with repeated indices, for example
i_{1}i_{2}i_{1}
. When we refer to monomials in
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
from now on, we will assume they are reduced.

The subspaces of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
generated by reduced monomials
i_{I}
with
|I|=d
are the subspaces of degree
d
; the elements of the subspace of degree
d
are then said to have degree
d
. As all monomials live in exactly one such subspace, all monomials have a well-defined degree. The degree is not additive on multiplication of elements (for example,
i_{j}\cdot i_{j}
is of degree 0, not degree 2), but it is subadditive, and additive when taken in
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
; this gives Clifford algebras a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
grading structure.
7
7

7

see
[
16
]
, Chapter 1 for a full introduction to Clifford algebras and their grading structure.

There are several important involutions on the Clifford algebra, generalising the complex conjugate structure of
\mathbb{C}
.

Definition 1.2

(The Involutions)
.

We have the following involutions on the Clifford algebra
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
:

i)

The
grade involution

\cdot^{\prime}:\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}\to\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
is the algebra automorphism that extends the map
V^{\prime}=-V
on
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
to the entire Clifford algebra. Given an explicit element
x=\sum_{I}x_{I}i_{I}\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
for some multi-indexes
I
,
x^{\prime}
is obtained from
x
by sending every
i_{j}
to
-i_{j}
.

ii)

Given an element
x=\sum_{I}x_{I}i_{I}\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
, the
reversal (or reverse) involution

\cdot^{*}:\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}\to\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
is the algebra map that reverses each monomial of
x
(say,
i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}\to i_{3}i_{2}i_{1}
). This is an antiautomorphism; that is,
(xy)^{*}=y^{*}x^{*}
.

iii)

Doing both (in either order) gives the third morphism, the
Clifford conjugation

\overline{\cdot}=\cdot^{\prime*}=\cdot^{*\prime}
, also an antiautomorphism.

\lrcorner

The Clifford algebra
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{0,1}}
is the complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
, and there the reversal involution becomes trivial and
\cdot^{\prime}=\overline{\cdot}
is just complex conjugation. Clifford conjugation is the natural extension of the complex conjugate, and it plays the same role in defining the norm and Hermitian product (see Definitions
1.5
,
1.7
).

In
\mathbb{C}
, the reals form an invariant subspace under complex conjugation. The three involutions
\cdot^{\prime},\cdot^{*},\overline{\cdot}
also have invariant subspaces that help determine the structure of the algebra.

Lemma 1.3
.

Monomials of degree
0,2\mod 4
are invariant under
\cdot^{\prime}
, while monomials of degree
0,1\mod 4
are invariant under
\cdot^{*}
. Monomials of degree
0,3\mod 4
are invariant under
\overline{\cdot}
. Monomials that are not invariant under an involution are sent to their negative by the involution instead.

Proof.
The involution
\cdot^{\prime}
sends
i_{j}\to-i_{j}
, and so a monomial
m=i_{j_{1}}i_{j_{2}}...i_{j_{k}}
of degree
k
is sent to
(-1)^{k}i_{j_{1}}i_{j_{2}}...i_{j_{k}}=(-1)^{k}m
. If
k
is even, the monomial is invariant.

The reversal involution
\cdot^{*}
for
m
can also be achieved through
\frac{k(k-1)}{2}
transpositions of neighbouring
i_{j}
. Each transposition introduces a minus sign, so
m^{*}=(-1)^{\frac{k(k-1)}{2}}m
. Invariance requires
2|k(k-1)/2
, and since
k,k-1
are coprime either
4|k
and
k=0\mod 4
or
4|(k-1)
and
k=1\mod 4
.

Combining the above two involutions, for a monomial
m
of degree
k
we find
\overline{m}=(-1)^{\frac{k(k+1)}{2}}m
, which by a similar argument implies invariance when
k=0\mod 4
or
k=3\mod 4
. It is clear that these involutions negate monomials that are not otherwise invariant.
\hfill\square

Using the grade involution, the Clifford algebra decomposes with a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
grading as
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}=\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}^{+}\oplus\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}^{-}
, where
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}^{\pm}=\{s\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}\ |\ s^{\prime}=\pm s\}
. The even component
\mathcal{C}\ell^{+}_{p,q}
is a subalgebra.

Though Clifford algebra multiplication does not commute in general, on restriction to the
real part
(elements of degree zero) the Clifford product commutes.

Lemma 1.4
.

Given
a,b\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{p,q}
,
\text{Re}(ab)=\text{Re}(ba)
.

Proof.
We expand
a
as
a=\sum_{I\subset\{1,...,p+q\}}a_{I}i_{I}
, where the
a_{I}
are real coefficients. Similarly,
b=\sum_{J}b_{J}i_{J}
. When we take the product
ab
the pairs of terms that multiply to a real number must share their multi-index.

\text{Re}(ab)=\sum_{I\subseteq\{1,2,...,p+q\}}a_{I}b_{I}(i_{I})^{2}=\sum_{I}b_{I}a_{I}(i_{I})^{2}=\text{Re}(ba).

1.1.1.
Vectors and Paravectors

The degree zero elements of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
are the
scalars
; degree one elements are precisely the
vectors
of
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
, the underlying vector space of the Clifford algebra. We can add scalars to vectors to get the subset of
paravectors
8
8

8

The altered position of the indices in
\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
is justified by (
1.6
) and Proposition
1.17
. The dollar sign on any object indicates a general ‘paravector-ness’; For more discussion of paravectors see
[
28
]
page 254,
[
19
]
, and
[
32
]
.

\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{p,q}\subseteq\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
. Paravectors are generally denoted by boldface uppercase letters, for example
\mathbf{V}
.

While it is more common to use a Clifford algebra to describe isometries of the underlying vector space
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
, following the work of Ahlfors (see e.g.
[
1
]
) we will be taking the point of view where paravectors are the more important subset. There are various reasons to take this approach, which we will touch on, but a key reason is that the paravector point of view provides a natural continuation of the language of the complex spinor under the action of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
.

When
(p,q)=(0,1)
, this paravector space is just the complex numbers, and when
(p,q)=(0,2)
we get a subspace of the quaternions which can be identified with the span of
\{1,i,j\}
. In general,
\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
is a vector space with one real part and
p+q
‘imaginary parts’, with unit generators of
N_{p,q}
-norm
\pm 1
depending on their signature.

Paravectors are invariant under reversal, so given
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
we have
\mathbf{V}^{*}=\mathbf{V},\quad\mathbf{V}^{\prime}=\overline{\mathbf{V}}
. For
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
, consider the product
\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{V}}=\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
. It is invariant under Clifford conjugation, so
\overline{\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}=\overline{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\overline{\mathbf{V}}=\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
.

By Lemma
1.3
, this invariance implies the product is the sum of monomials of degree
0,3\mod 4
; but as a product of two paravectors the degree is bounded to
\leq 2
, implying it is purely of degree zero and is thus a scalar, so
\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{V}}=\mathbf{V}\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R},\ \forall\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
.

Definition 1.5
.

The
norm
of the paravector
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
is the map

N:\ \$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}\to\mathbb{R},\ N(\mathbf{V})=\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{V}}=\overline{\mathbf{V}}\mathbf{V}.

This norm will not in general be positive when
p>0
. We may also refer to the norm as the
magnitude squared

|\mathbf{V}|^{2}=N(\mathbf{V})
, and when
p=0
we define the
magnitude

|\mathbf{V}|=\sqrt{|\mathbf{V}|^{2}}
of
\mathbf{V}
. The terms ‘magnitude squared’ and ‘norm’ may be used interchangeably.

\lrcorner

The product
\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{V}}
can be computed explicitly in terms of the standard basis. Given:

\mathbf{V}=V_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{p+q}V_{j}i_{j},\ V_{0},V_{j}\in\mathbb{R}

we find

(1.6)

N(\mathbf{V})=V_{0}^{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{p+q}V_{j}^{2}i_{j}^{2}=V_{0}^{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{p}V_{j}^{2}+\sum_{j=p+1}^{p+q}V_{j}^{2}.

Remark
.

As
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}\subset\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
, it inherits the norm
N
of Definition
1.5
. But it already has a norm
N_{p,q}
via Definition
1.1
. These are simply the negative of one another; if we take
V=\sum_{j=1}^{p+q}V_{j}i_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
, then

N_{p,q}(V)=\sum_{j=1}^{p}V_{j}^{2}-\sum_{j=p+1}^{p+q}V_{j}^{2},

which is
-N(V)
, comparing to (
1.6
).

The norm can be polarised to give an inner product in the standard way.

Definition 1.7
.

Given elements
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
, we define the inner product (or
dot product
)

(1.8)

\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{W}=\frac{1}{2}\left(N(\mathbf{V}+\mathbf{W})-N(\mathbf{V})-N(\mathbf{W})\right).

It can equivalently be defined as

\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{W}=\text{Re}(\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{W}})=\frac{\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{W}}+\mathbf{W}\overline{\mathbf{V}}}{2}=V_{0}W_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{p}V_{j}W_{j}+\sum_{j=p+1}^{p+q}V_{j}W_{j},

where
\mathbf{V}=V_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{p+q}V_{j}i_{j}
and
\mathbf{W}
is defined similarly.

\lrcorner

Clifford multiplication of vectors in the underlying vector space
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
satisfies a
polarisation identity
.
9
9

9

See
[
16
]
(1.4); note the sign difference, due to a different choice for the ideal that defines the Clifford algebra.
For
V,W\in\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
,

(1.9)

VW+WV=2N_{p,q}(V,W).

1.1.2.
The Norm on
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}

The norm on paravectors is a real-valued map, and this holds when extended to products of paravectors; given
a\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
satisfying

a=\mathbf{V}_{1}\mathbf{V}_{2}...\mathbf{V}_{k},\text{ where }\mathbf{V}_{1},\mathbf{V}_{2},...,\mathbf{V}_{k}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p},

we find

a\overline{a}=\mathbf{V}_{1}\mathbf{V}_{2}...\mathbf{V}_{k}\overline{\mathbf{V}_{k}}...\overline{\mathbf{V}_{2}}\overline{\mathbf{V}_{1}}=N(\mathbf{V}_{1})N(\mathbf{V}_{2})...N(\mathbf{V}_{k})\in\mathbb{R}.

In fact a norm can be defined over the whole Clifford algebra by taking the real component of
a\overline{a}
, though it isn’t necessarily useful for arbitrary elements.

Definition 1.10
.

The norm of an element
a\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
is defined by

N:\ \mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}\to\mathbb{R},\ N(a)=\text{Re}(a\overline{a}).

This can also be polarised to give a form in the standard way.

Definition 1.11
.

Given elements
a,b\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
, we define the inner product (or dot product)

a\cdot b=\frac{1}{2}(N(a+b)-N(a)-N(b)).

Equivalently,

a\cdot b=\text{Re}(a\overline{b})=\text{Re}\left(\frac{a\overline{b}+b\overline{a}}{2}\right).

Elements
a\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
that have a left or right inverse
a^{-1}
are
invertible
(and
a^{-1}
is the two-sided inverse), because Clifford algebras can be represented as matrix algebras and this property holds for square matrices. Notate the subgroup of invertible elements in the Clifford algebra as
\mathcal{C}\ell^{\times}_{p,q}
. The inverse of an element
a\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
can always be written as
a^{-1}=\overline{a}(a\overline{a})^{-1}
, and if you restrict focus to products of paravectors then
N(a)=a\overline{a}
is a real number and finding its multiplicative inverse is trivial.

1.1.3.
Paravectors as Exponentiated Vectors

One way to think about the significance of paravectors is by exponentiating vectors; the exponential of the underlying vector space for the Clifford algebra will coordinatise the unit paravector sphere.

The exponential map is defined
10
10

10

The exponential is actually convergent over the whole Clifford algebra; see
[
16
]
, Chapter 1, Section 2.
by the standard Taylor series
e^{V}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j!}V^{j}
. In the complex case covered in
[
23
]
this is also the standard Lie group exponential from
\mathbb{R}
to the unit circle in
\mathbb{C}
(Euler’s map
\theta\to e^{i\theta}
), but the image of
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
under exponentiation doesn’t admit a Lie group
11
11

11

The soon-to-be-defined Lipschitz and paravector Lipschitz groups in which the vectors and paravectors reside are Lie groups, however, so this can be seen as a restriction of a Lie group-Lie algebra correspondence to a subspace.
structure in general. For the remainder of Section
1.1.3
we will take
(p,q)=(0,n)
to reduce to the case
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1,0}=\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, which is positive definite.

Proposition 1.12
.

Given a vector
V\in\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
with
N(V)=1
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
:

e^{V\theta}=\cos(\theta)+V\sin(\theta)\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

Proof.
For a vector
V\in\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
,
V^{\prime}=\overline{V}=-V
. We can compute powers of V as

V^{m}=(-|V|^{2})^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}V^{m\mod 2}

where
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
is the floor function and
m\mod 2
is the standard modulus function. Substituting this into the exponential gives us

e^{V}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-|V|^{2})^{\lfloor m/2\rfloor}}{m!}V^{m\mod 2}=\left(\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{m}|V|^{2m}}{(2m)!}+\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{m}|V|^{2m}}{(2m+1)!}V\right).

We recognise the Taylor series for
\cos|V|
and
\sin|V|
:

e^{V}=\left(\cos|V|+\frac{V}{|V|}\sin|V|\right).

Introducing
\theta
gives us

e^{V\theta}=\cos(|V\theta|)+\frac{V\theta}{|V\theta|}\sin(|V\theta|)=\cos(|V|\theta)+\frac{V}{|V|}\sin(|V|\theta).

By normalising so
|V|=1
, we find the desired expression.
\hfill\square

Remark
.

Exponentiating degree
2
forms
B\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
of unit norm will result in the same trigonometric identity:

e^{B\theta}=\cos(\theta)+B\sin(\theta),

as these satisfy

B^{2}=B(-\overline{B})=-|B|^{2}=-1.

However, for forms
F\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
of degree
3
or
4
with unit norm we instead find
F^{2}=|F|^{2}
, giving the identity

e^{F\theta}=\cosh(\theta)+F\sinh(\theta).

Generally, given any homogeneous element
Y\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
of degree
d
and unit norm, if
d
is congruent to
0,3\mod 4
we find

e^{Y\theta}=\cosh(\theta)+Y\sinh(\theta).

If, instead,
d\cong 1,2\mod 4
, we find

e^{Y\theta}=\cos(\theta)+Y\sin(\theta).

We can exploit this exponential map to parameterise the unit
n
-sphere, just as the unit circle can be parameterised by
e^{i\theta}
. Take the Clifford algebras
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
; their paravector subspaces
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
have a positive-definite norm via Definition
1.5
. Consider the unit
n
-sphere
S^{n}
in
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\subset\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
under this norm, and consider the subset
S^{n-1}\subset S^{n}
formed by the unit
(n-1)
-sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\subset\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Take a unit vector
V\in S^{n-1}
and an angle
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. By the proposition above,
e^{V\theta}
has unit length, and so lies in
S^{n}
.

Geometrically, to construct
e^{V\theta}
we pick the point
V
in
S^{n-1}
, take the plane spanned by
1
and
V
, and rotate in this plane by angle
\theta
from
1
towards
V
(see Figure
1
).

1
i_{1},i_{2},...
i_{j},i_{j+1}...
e^{V\theta}
V
\theta

Figure 1.
Parameterisation of the sphere.

1.2.
The Lipschitz Group

\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
can act on itself in various ways, commonly by some variant of the adjoint action. To ensure the action is reversible (what we’re really interested in down the line are isometries), we’ll restrict to the action of invertible elements.

Definition 1.13

(Adjoint Actions)
.

Given an element
x\in\mathcal{C}\ell^{\times}_{p,q}
, we define an action

\sigma:\mathcal{C}\ell^{\times}_{p,q}\to End(\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}),\ \sigma(x)(y)=xyx^{*}.

This action is multiplicative; that is,

\sigma(x)\sigma(y)=\sigma(xy).

This definition is a bit unusual; the more common
12
12

12

See, for example,
[
16
]
.

twisted adjoint action
is defined by

\widetilde{Ad}_{x}y=x^{\prime}yx^{-1},

which is essentially
\sigma
up to a scaling factor. The standard
adjoint action
is

Ad_{x}y=xyx^{-1}.

The adjoint action, when restricted appropriately, gives isometries of
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
, while the twisted adjoint resolves a subtlety where the standard adjoint action misses some orientation reversing isometries. However, we want to maintain the scaling factor; it appears naturally in discussion of the lambda length defined in Section
4.3
. So the action
\sigma(x)
is by isometries, plus a dilation.

Clifford algebras essentially exist to describe isometries, but in general they contain many elements that do not act via an isometry. To combat this, we focus our attention on elements whose action under
\sigma
restricts to an automorphism on some subspace of the Clifford algebra. The obvious choice is to consider the subgroup of the Clifford algebra whose action fixes the underlying vector space
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
; this then defines a map
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}\to\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
. This is called a
Lipschitz group
.

Definition 1.14

(Lipschitz Groups)
.

The
Lipschitz group

\Gamma_{p,q}
is the group generated by products of invertible vectors
V\in\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
. We can also define the even Lipschitz group:

\Gamma_{p,q}^{+}=\Gamma_{p,q}\cap\mathcal{C}\ell^{+}_{p,q}.

For all such objects if
q=0
we drop it from the notation, writing
\Gamma_{p,0}=\Gamma_{p}
and so on.

\lrcorner

There are alternative characterisations of the Lipschitz group, which can prove useful. Lounesto (
[
19
]
, p. 220) tells us the Lipschitz group
\Gamma_{p,q}
has the two following alternative characterisations:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\{s\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}\ |\ \forall V\in\mathbb{R}^{p,q},\ s^{\prime}Vs^{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p,q}\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\{s\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}\ |\ \forall V\in\mathbb{R}^{p,q},\ sVs^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{p,q}\text{ and }N(s)\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}\}.

In the second characterisation we seem to have an extra condition, that the norm of
s
is real; but this is implied by
s^{\prime}Vs^{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
in the first characterisation. Within the Lipschitz groups, we find the spin groups.

Definition 1.15

(Spin Group)
.

The spin group Spin
(p,q)
is the ‘unit’ subgroup of
\Gamma^{+}_{p,q}
.

\text{Spin}(p,q)=\{s\ |\ s\in\Gamma^{+}_{p,q}\text{ and }N(s)=\pm 1\}.

When
q=0
, we instead define

\text{Spin}(n)=\{s\ |\ s\in\Gamma^{+}_{n}\text{ and }N(s)=1\}.

It is a standard result that the twisted adjoint action of the Lipschitz group is isomorphic to the action of the orthogonal group
O(p,q)
on
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
; that is, it gives the isometries
13
13

13

see, for example,
[
16
]
Corollary 2.6.
of
\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
.

However, to utilise Ahlfors’ work
[
1
]
,
[
3
]
(which builds its objects from paravectors rather than vectors), we consider the isometries of the paravectors
\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
instead.

Definition 1.16

(Paravector Lipschitz Group)
.

The paravector Lipschitz group
14
14

14

A good discussion is in the book by Porteous in
[
28
]
, page 254.

\$\Gamma_{q+1,p}
is the subgroup of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
generated by products of invertible paravectors.

\lrcorner

As with the more standard Lipschitz group, there are multiple ways to define the paravector Lipschitz group; these are equivalent, just different characterisations, and sometimes these alternate definitions prove useful. From Lounesto (
[
19
]
p. 225), we have that the paravector Lipschitz group
\$\Gamma_{q+1,p}
is alternatively characterised by

\displaystyle\$\Gamma_{q+1,p}

\displaystyle=\{s\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}\ |\ \forall\ \mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p},\ s^{\prime}\mathbf{V}s^{-1}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}\},

\displaystyle\$\Gamma_{q+1,p}

\displaystyle=\{s\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}\ |\ \forall\ \mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p},\ s\mathbf{V}s^{*}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}\text{ and }N(s)\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}\}.

Following
[
10
]
, we also define the
Lipschitz monoid

\$\Gamma_{q+1,p}^{\triangleright}=\$\Gamma_{q+1,p}\cup\{0\}
for later convenience.

The notation of the group
\$\Gamma_{q+1,p}
is suggestive; although it’s drawn from the Clifford algebra with signature
(p,q)
, we’ve moved around the indices. Indeed, this group is isomorphic to one of the more standard Lipschitz groups.

Proposition 1.17
.

With respect to their group structure,
\$\Gamma_{q+1,p}\cong\Gamma^{+}_{q+1,p}
.

Proof.
See p.225 of
[
19
]
, and p.3 of
[
1
]
; the identification follows because the norm on paravectors has signature
(q+1,p)
(see (
1.6
)).
\hfill\square

This also relates the paravector spin groups to the more usually defined ones.

Corollary 1.18
.

The subgroup

\$\text{pin}(q+1,p)=\{s\in\$\Gamma_{q+1,p}\ |\ N(s)=\pm 1\}

is isomorphic to Spin
(q+1,p)
. The subgroup

\$\text{pin}(n+1)=\{s\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}\ |\ N(s)=1\}

is isomorphic to Spin
(n+1)
.
\hfill\square

Taken together, Proposition
1.17
and Corollary
1.18
allow us to write the standard spin and Lipschitz groups in the language of paravectors. Note that this isomorphism doesn’t respect the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
grading of the Clifford algebra.

Though Lipschitz groups are not in general commutative, the following lemma allows for some flexibility when rearranging terms.

Lemma 1.19
.

For any
a\in\$\Gamma_{q+1,p}
and paravector
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
, there exists
\mathbf{U}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
such that
a\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{U}a^{\prime}
, as well as
\mathbf{W}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
such that
a\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{W}a
.

Proof.
We can rearrange the equation to find
\mathbf{U}=a\mathbf{V}a^{\prime-1}
, which is the twisted adjoint action
\widetilde{Ad}_{a^{\prime}}\mathbf{V}
. This action preserves
\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
by definition, implying
\mathbf{U}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
as claimed. Similarly, we can rearrange the second equation as
\mathbf{W}=a\mathbf{V}a^{-1}=Ad_{a}(\mathbf{V})
,
which is the ordinary adjoint action; this also preserves
\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
, so
\mathbf{W}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}
.
\hfill\square

1.2.1.
The Action of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}

From here onwards we will consider only the Lipschitz groups
\$\Gamma_{n+1}=\$\Gamma_{n+1,0}
associated with the negative-definite Clifford algebras
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
. The following lemma gives a concrete geometric interpretation of the action of paravectors.

Lemma 1.20
.

Consider a unit paravector
\mathbf{U}=e^{V\theta}\notin\mathbb{R}
,
V\in\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
,
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. The set of fixed paravectors under the action
\sigma(\mathbf{U})
on
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is the subspace of
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
consisting of all vectors
W\in\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
satisfying
\mathbf{U}\cdot W=0
. The action
\sigma(\mathbf{U})
is a rotation in the plane spanned by
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{U}
, a rotation of
2\theta
from
\mathbb{R}
towards
\mathbf{U}
.

Proof.
We want fixed directions, which is equivalent to finding fixed vectors as
|\mathbf{U}|=1
and
\sigma(\mathbf{U})
is an isometry. Let
\mathbf{W}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be fixed by
\sigma(\mathbf{U})
.

\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{U}^{*}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{U}=\mathbf{W}\implies\mathbf{U}\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{W}\mathbf{U}^{\prime},

as
\mathbf{U}^{*}=\mathbf{U}
on paravectors and
\mathbf{U}^{-1}=\overline{\mathbf{U}}|\mathbf{U}|^{-2}=\overline{\mathbf{U}}=\mathbf{U}^{\prime}
. We expand
\mathbf{U}
and
\mathbf{W}
in terms of coordinates, rearrange, and apply (
1.9
) to find the following:

\sum_{j=1}^{n}U_{j}W_{0}i_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}U_{j}W_{j}.

As the left side is strictly imaginary and the right is strictly real, they must both be zero. The condition
\sum_{j=1}^{n}U_{j}W_{0}i_{j}=0
implies either
W_{0}=0
or
\mathbf{U}
is real, but we’ve already assumed
\mathbf{U}\notin\mathbb{R}
so we conclude
W_{0}=0
and
\mathbf{W}=W\in\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
. The condition
\sum_{j=1}^{n}U_{j}W_{j}=0
then implies
\mathbf{W}
is orthogonal under the dot product to the purely vector component of
\mathbf{U}
, implying the first claim.

So our fixed subspace under
\sigma(\mathbf{U})
is the orthogonal subspace to the vector component of
\mathbf{U}
, implying the action (which we know is an isometry) must be an orientation-preserving linear isometry in the plane spanned by
\mathbb{R}
and the vector component of
\mathbf{U}
(which is equivalent to the span of
\{\mathbb{R},\mathbf{U}\}
). The only orientation-preserving linear isometries of a vector space are rotations, implying the second part of the lemma.

To see precisely which rotation, let
\mathbf{U}=e^{\theta V}
for
V\in\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
act on
1\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Then
\sigma(\mathbf{U})(1)=e^{\theta V}(1)e^{\theta V}=e^{2\theta V}
, where the last equality follows from expanding as in Proposition
1.12
. The paravector
1
is rotated towards
\mathbf{U}
by an angle of
2\theta
.
\hfill\square

Corollary 1.21
.

All orientation-preserving linear isometries of the normed vector space
(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1},N)
can be generated by a product of rotations in planes spanned by
1
and a paravector
\not\in\mathbb{R}
.
\hfill\square

1.2.2.
Lie Algebras and Tangent Spaces

There is a standard description
15
15

15

[
19
]
pp. 221-224.
of the Lie algebras of spin groups
Spin(p,q)
as the space of bivectors
{\bigwedge}^{\!2}\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
drawn from the exterior algebra
\bigwedge\mathbb{R}^{p,q}
. We can find a similar description for the Lie algebra of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
.

Theorem 1.22
.

The Lie algebra
\$\mathfrak{g}_{n+1}
of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
consists of elements of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
whose terms have degree
\leq 2
. That is,

\$\mathfrak{g}_{n+1}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\oplus{\bigwedge}^{\!2}\mathbb{R}^{0,n},

where
{\bigwedge}^{\!2}\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
is the space of degree two elements of the Clifford algebra. The Lie bracket operation is the commutator.

We write
{\bigwedge}^{\!2}\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
for the degree two elements of the Clifford algebra because there is a canonical vector space isomorphism between the exterior algebra over a vector space
V
and the Clifford algebra over
V
for any given quadratic form
Q
(see
[
16
]
, Prop. 1.3). Notate this Clifford algebra by
\mathcal{C}\ell(V,Q)
. By an abuse of notation, we may then speak of the embeddings
{\bigwedge}^{\!r}V\subset\mathcal{C}\ell(V,Q)
. Note that this is only an isomorphism of vector spaces,
not
of algebras.

Proof of
1.22
.
Let
a\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
and
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. We can differentiate the relations defining
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
:

a\mathbf{V}a^{*}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\text{ and }N(a)\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\},

and evaluate at the identity to describe the Lie algebra.

Consider a smooth path
a_{t}\in\$\Gamma_{n+1},\ t\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
for some
\varepsilon>0
satisfying
a_{0}=1
. Then
a_{t}=1+tH+O(t^{2})
for some element
H\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
. We substitute this into our first defining relation, expand, differentiate, then evaluate at t=0:

H\mathbf{V}+(H\mathbf{V})^{*}=\dot{\mathbf{W}}_{0}\in T_{\mathbf{W}_{0}}\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\cong\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

We expand this relation in terms of the components
\mathbf{V}=V_{0}+\sum V_{j}i_{j}
to find:

\displaystyle V_{0}(H+H^{*})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}V_{j}(Hi_{j}+(Hi_{j})^{*})

\displaystyle\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

By setting
V_{0}=1
and
V_{j}=0,\ 1\leq j\leq n
we see that
H+H^{*}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Consulting Lemma
1.3
, this implies it only has terms of degrees
0,1,2+4m,3+4m
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
as other terms of degree
0+4m,1+4m
wouldn’t cancel appropriately. Similarly, by setting all
V_{j}
to zero except for one
j=\widehat{j}
, we see that
Hi_{\widehat{j}}+(Hi_{\widehat{j}})^{*}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. This implies
Hi_{\widehat{j}}
also only has terms of degrees
0,1,2+4m,3+4m
for all
i_{j}
. If
H
contained any terms of degree
2+4m
(say a term
i_{j}i_{k}...
) then in the product
Hi_{j}
that term would become degree
1+4m
as the
i_{j}
’s cancel; this conflicts with our above conclusion, so
H
cannot contain terms of degree
2+4m
outside of the case
m=0
, since we allow degree 1 terms.

Now bring in our second condition:

N(1+tH+...)=(1+tH+...)\overline{(1+tH+...)}\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\},

which implies to first order
1+t(H+\overline{H})\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
, which implies
H+\overline{H}\in\mathbb{R}
. Lemma
1.3
tells us terms of degree 3 or 0 mod 4 are invariant under
\overline{\cdot}
, implying
H
only has terms of degree
0,1+4m,2+4m
. Combining all these restrictions, the Lie algebra only allows terms of degree
0,1
, and
2
.

For the converse, we know from Corollary 2.6 (and the discussion of Theorem 2.7) of
[
16
]
that
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
has a surjective homomorphism onto
SO(n+1)
with kernel
\mathbb{R}^{+}
. This implies it is of dimension
n(n+1)/2+1
, which is therefore the dimension of its Lie algebra. By the arguments above, the Lie algebra must be contained in the vector space
\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\oplus{\bigwedge}^{\!2}\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
, and because this space is also of dimension
n(n+1)/2+1
, they are isomorphic.
\hfill\square

As is standard (see, for example,
[
30
]
§16), we can describe tangent spaces at other points of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
by left translation. First, a definition:

Definition 1.23
.

The set of elements

\mathcal{B}^{n}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\oplus{\bigwedge}^{\!2}\mathbb{R}^{0,n}

of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{0,n}}
are called the
bi-paravectors
.

\lrcorner

Corollary 1.24
.

The tangent space
T_{s}\$\Gamma_{n+1}
at
s\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
is described by the subset of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
given by products
sB
for bi-paravectors
B\in\mathcal{B}^{n}
.
\hfill\square

Applying the isomorphism of Proposition
1.17
, “bivectors” of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{n+1}}
with basis elements
1,i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{n}
are precisely the bi-paravectors.

It is a standard fact of Lie groups that inverses in the group correspond to negation in the Lie algebra:

(e^{G})^{-1}=e^{-G}.

What about the other involutions?

Lemma 1.25
.

Each of the involutions
\cdot^{\prime}
,
\cdot^{*}
, and
\overline{\cdot}
on
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
corresponds to taking that same involution in the Lie algebra, for example:

\overline{(e^{G})}=e^{\overline{G}}.

Proof.
Consider the involution
\cdot^{\prime}
. If we take the expansion of the exponential:

e^{tG}=1+tG+t^{2}G^{2}/2+...

and we apply the involution to both sides:

(e^{tG})^{\prime}=1+tG^{\prime}+t^{2}(G^{2})^{\prime}/2+...,

we find that, on restriction to first order,
(e^{G})^{\prime}
should represented in the tangent space by
G^{\prime}
. As the bi-paravectors are closed under
\cdot^{\prime}
,
G^{\prime}
is a well-defined element of the Lie algebra. A similar argument holds for the other involutions.

\hfill\square

1.3.
Special Linear Groups

In
[
1
]
, matrix groups are constructed that generalise the special and general linear groups of matrices over commutative rings. For our purposes, we’re interested in the groups
16
16

16

In Ahlfors’ definition the groups
SL(2,\cdot)
are the subgroups of
GL(2,\cdot)
with pseudo-determinant
\pm 1
, and
SL_{+}
this is the connected component with identity. As we aren’t interested in anything but definite and orientation-preserving maps, our naming is simplified.

SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
as they generalise the role of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
in providing a double cover of the isometry group of
\mathbb{H}^{3}
.

Definition 1.26

(Special Linear Group)
.

The matrix
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}
is an element of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
if

i)

a,b,c,d\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
,

ii)

a^{*}d-c^{*}b=1
,

iii)

ab^{*},cd^{*},c^{*}a,d^{*}b\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

We refer to an element of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
as a
Clifford matrix
. We similarly define the general linear group
GL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
by relaxing condition ii) to
a^{*}d-c^{*}b\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
.

\lrcorner

Condition i) is necessary to ensure the matrix is invertible, while condition ii) enforces the ‘special’ part of ‘special linear group’. The quantity
a^{*}d-c^{*}b
is a noncommutative extension
17
17

17

There are multiple ways to generalise the determinant to the noncommutative setting; we use another notion, the quasideterminant (introduced in work of Gelfand and Retakh
[
15
]
with a good overview given in
[
14
]
), in the discussion of Ptolemy relations (see Section
4.4
).
of the determinant; we refer to it as the
pseudo-determinant
, or pdet. The pseudo-determinant is multiplicative when restricted to
GL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
(see
[
1
]
), which of course extends to
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
.

An action (Möbius transformation) is defined on the one-point compactification of the paravectors
\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}=\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\cup\{\infty\}
(also called the
paravector sphere
, or
generalised Riemann sphere
) according to the rule

(1.27)

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{V}=(a\mathbf{V}+b)(c\mathbf{V}+d)^{-1}.

This is a bijective mapping
\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\to\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
(see
[
1
]
); condition iii) is required to ensure this mapping is a genuine map
\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\to\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
.

1.3.1.
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
and the Isometries of
\mathbb{H}^{n}

Clifford algebras describe the isometries of a vector space. How does one translate this into the isometries of hyperbolic space?

The action (
1.27
) is a bijective map of the ‘generalised Riemann sphere’
\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
onto itself; consider
\$\mathbb{R}^{n}\subset\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
in the obvious way, by fixing the coefficient of
i_{n}
at zero.
18
18

18

Recall that
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is generated by
1
and
i_{1}
through
i_{n}
.
The subspace
\$\mathbb{R}^{n}
is then acted on by
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n})
, but
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n})\subset SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
; we can thus consider
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n})
as a subgroup of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
, one that fixes
\$\mathbb{R}^{n}\subset\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. In particular, this implies the upper half-space
\mathbb{U}^{n+1}\subset\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is preserved,
19
19

19

To be precise, we must also note that
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
is orientation-preserving so the upper and lower half-spaces aren’t interchanged.
so this subgroup naturally acts on a model of hyperbolic space.

Lemma 1.28
.

Every
g\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n})
maps the upper half-space
\mathbb{U}^{n+1}\subset\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
onto itself, and

\left|Dg(x)\right|=(gx)_{n}/x_{n}

for all
x\in\mathbb{U}^{n+1}
, where the
Dg
is the Jacobian matrix and
x_{n}
is the coefficient of
i_{n}
in the paravector expansion.

Proof.
See
[
4
]
Section 4.
\hfill\square

As Ahlfors demonstrates (following the ideas of Vahlen and Maass), this is essentially the statement of the invariance of the hyperbolic metric on
\mathbb{U}^{n+1}
, and this is enough to recover the entire (orientation-preserving) isometry group
PSL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})=SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})/\{I,-I\}
.

1.4.
Two-Component Lipschitz Spinors

We can projectivise the action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on the generalised Riemann sphere. Given
\xi,\eta\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
satisfying
\xi\eta^{-1}=\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, instead of working with the paravector
\mathbf{V}
we can work with these ‘projective coordinates’
(\xi,\eta)
.

Definition 1.29
.

A
Lipschitz spinor
is a pair

\kappa=\begin{pmatrix}\xi\\
\eta\end{pmatrix},

with
\xi,\eta\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
(not both equally zero) which satisfy
\xi\overline{\eta}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, or equivalently
\xi\eta^{-1}\in\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
. The set of Lipschitz spinors is denoted
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. More formally, we can define the ‘ratio map’:

\mathscr{R}:\ S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},\ (\xi,\eta)\to\xi\eta^{-1},

which sends Lipschitz spinors to the generalised Riemann sphere. Lipschitz spinors also have a conjugate transpose, indicated by a superscript dagger:

\kappa^{\dagger}=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{\xi}&\overline{\eta}\end{pmatrix}.

To the author’s knowledge, this definition of a generalised two-component spinor has not appeared in the literature before.

Lemma 1.30
.

For
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
,

\xi^{*}\eta,\ \xi\eta^{*}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

Proof.
As
\kappa
is a Lipschitz spinor,
\xi\eta^{-1}=\mathbf{V}\in\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
. If
\eta=0
then
\xi^{*}\eta=0\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, and if
\eta\neq 0
we compute

\xi=\mathbf{V}\eta\implies\xi^{*}\eta=\eta^{*}\mathbf{V}\eta=\sigma(\eta^{*})(\mathbf{V})\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

as
\sigma(x)
for
x\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
is an action on
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. For
\xi\eta^{*}
, again if
\eta=0
then
\xi\eta^{*}=0\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. If
\eta\neq 0
, then

\xi=\mathbf{V}\eta=\eta\eta^{-1}\mathbf{V}\eta=\eta Ad_{\eta^{-1}}(\mathbf{V})=\eta\mathbf{U},

where
\mathbf{U}=Ad_{\eta^{-1}}(\mathbf{V})\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Then

\xi\eta^{*}=\eta\mathbf{U}\eta^{*}=\sigma(\eta)(\mathbf{U})\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

Corollary 1.31
.

For
(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
,

\xi^{*}\eta=\eta^{*}\xi,\quad\xi\eta^{*}=\eta\xi^{*}.

Proof.
Since
\xi^{*}\eta\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and paravectors are invariant under the reversal conjugation,
\xi^{*}\eta=(\xi^{*}\eta)^{*}=\eta^{*}\xi
. The same argument holds for
\xi\eta^{*}
.
\hfill\square

The conditions of the previous corollary seem reminiscent of the conditions on the columns and rows of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
, and that isn’t a coincidence. The generalised special linear groups can also be characterised as
2\times 2
matrices with Lipschitz spinors for rows and columns, and pseudo-determinant
1
.

Proposition 1.32
.

SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
can equivalently be characterised as the matrices
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}
satisfying

i)

a,b,c,d\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
,

ii)

a^{*}d-c^{*}b=1
,

iii)

(a,b),(c,d),(a,c),(b,d)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
.

Proof.
Obviously conditions i), ii) are identical to the definition of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
, so we need to show that condition iii) of Definition
1.26
implies condition iii) above, and vice versa.

Note that
(a,b)\neq(0,0)
, or else the pseudo-determinant would be zero; similar holds for the other pairs in iii). If
ab^{*}=\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, then

a=\mathbf{V}b^{*-1}=bb^{-1}\mathbf{V}b^{*-1}=b\sigma(b^{-1})(\mathbf{V}).

As
\sigma
is a map
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
this is of the form ‘b times a paravector’. By Lemma
1.19
, there exists a
\mathbf{W}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
a=\mathbf{W}b
, which implies
a\overline{b}=|b|^{2}\mathbf{W}
and therefore
(a,b)\neq(0,0)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. The same argument holds for the other cases.

For the converse, if
(a,b)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
then
ab^{*}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
by Lemma
1.30
, and the same argument holds for the other three spinors in iii).
\hfill\square

1.4.1.
The Action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on Lipschitz Spinors

We want to act on these Lipschitz spinors by our special linear groups. The obvious thing to try is acting by matrix multiplication; take
(\xi,\eta)^{T}\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
to be a column vector, and multiply by
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on the left. Not only does this send Lipschitz spinors to Lipschitz spinors (ensuring it truly is an action on
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
), but it is also equivalent to acting by Möbius transformations on the paravector
\xi\eta^{-1}
, so it really is the right choice.

Lemma 1.33
.

Left-multiplication by
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
defines an action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. The action of
A
on
\mathbf{V}\in\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
corresponds equivariantly to the action by matrix multiplication on the Lipschitz spinor
(\xi,\eta)
with
\xi\eta^{-1}=\mathbf{V}
.

Proof.
Take the action by matrix multiplication:

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\xi\\
\eta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a\xi+b\eta\\
c\xi+d\eta\\
\end{pmatrix}.

Let
\mathbf{W}=(a\xi+b\eta)(c\xi+d\eta)^{-1}
be the projective image of the transformation. We can insert
1=\eta^{-1}\eta
between the brackets:

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}

\displaystyle=(a\xi+b\eta)\eta^{-1}\eta(c\xi+d\eta)^{-1}=(a\xi\eta^{-1}+b)(c\xi\eta^{-1}+d)^{-1}=(a\mathbf{V}+b)(c\mathbf{V}+d)^{-1}.

This is the same fractional linear transformation as (
1.27
), which implies left-multiplication by
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
is a well-defined action
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, and implies equivariance of the
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
action through the map
\mathscr{R}
from Lipschitz spinors to the generalised Riemann sphere.
\hfill\square

We may also denote this action by
A.\kappa=A.(\xi,\eta)
. We can extend this to an action by matrix multiplication of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
more generally. This is more obviously a map
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}\to\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
, as algebras are closed under addition and multiplication.

Lemma 1.34
.

The action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
is transitive on
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
.

Proof.
Any Lipschitz spinor
(\xi,\eta)
can be found as the first column of an element of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
:

(1.35)

A=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{*-1}\\
\eta&\frac{1}{2}\xi^{*-1}\end{pmatrix}.

It is easy to check that such an
A
lies in
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
for all
(\xi,\eta)
. Thus, for two spinors
\kappa_{1}
,
\kappa_{2}\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
there are matrices
A_{1}
,
A_{2}\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
with first columns
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
respectively. Then
A_{1}.(1,0)=\kappa_{1}
and
A_{2}.(1,0)=\kappa_{2}
, and
A_{2}A_{1}^{-1}\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
sends
\kappa_{1}
to
\kappa_{2}
.
\hfill\square

We will make heavy use of the transitivity of the
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
action to extend statements from simple examples to the general case.

1.5.
Products and Forms

There are various important binary operations on the complex spinors
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. The Lipschitz spinors carry generalisations of these.

1.5.1.
Two Inner Products

In Definition
1.7
we defined an inner product (the
dot product
) on paravectors in the obvious way, essentially just the Euclidean dot product. It was also extended to
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
more generally, as
a\cdot b=\text{Re}(a\overline{b})
.

Lemma 1.36
.

\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
equipped with
x\cdot y:\ \mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}\times\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}\to\mathbb{R}
is a real inner product space.

Proof.
We must show i) symmetry, ii) linearity in the first argument, and iii) positive definiteness. For i) we appeal to the invariance of real numbers under
\overline{\cdot}
:

x\cdot y=\text{Re}(x\overline{y})=\overline{\text{Re}(x\overline{y})}=\text{Re}(y\overline{x})=y\cdot x.

For ii), take
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and
x,y,z\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
:

(ax+by)\cdot z=\text{Re}((ax+by)\overline{z})=a\text{Re}(x\overline{z})+b\text{Re}(y\overline{z})=a(x\cdot z)+b(y\cdot z).

So the map is real linear in the first argument, and in the second by symmetry.

For iii), let
x=\sum_{I}x_{I}i_{I}
,
x_{I}\in\mathbb{R}
be the expansion of
x\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}\backslash\{0\}
in the standard basis.

x\cdot x=\text{Re}(x\overline{x})=\sum_{I}x_{I}i_{I}\overline{x_{I}i_{I}}=\sum_{I}x_{I}^{2}i_{I}\overline{i_{I}}.

If
i_{I}=i_{j}i_{k}...i_{z}
, then
\overline{i_{I}}=(-i_{z})...(-i_{k})(-i_{j})
. This implies

\displaystyle i_{I}\overline{i_{I}}

\displaystyle=i_{j}i_{k}...i_{z}(-i_{z})...(-i_{k})(-i_{j})=1,

\hfill\square

\displaystyle x\cdot x

\displaystyle=\sum_{I}x_{I}^{2}>0.

We can also put an inner product on
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
, of which the Lipschitz spinors are a subset.

Definition 1.37
.

Given
D_{m}=(x_{m},y_{m})\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
for
m=1,2
, we define

(D_{1}|D_{2})=\text{Re}(\overline{x}_{1}x_{2}+\overline{y}_{1}y_{2}).

We also define
|D|^{2}=(D|D)
.

\lrcorner

We move the conjugation to the first terms to align with the generalised Hermitian form of Section
1.5.2
. There, the choice matters; here,
(D_{1}|D_{2})
is the same as if we chose Re
(x_{1}\overline{x}_{2}+y_{1}\overline{y}_{2})
.

Lemma 1.38
.

For
x,y\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
,
\text{Re}(x\overline{y})=\text{Re}(\overline{x}y)
.

Proof.
Real numbers are invariant under Clifford conjugation, implying
\text{Re}(x\overline{y})=\overline{\text{Re}(x\overline{y})}=\text{Re}(y\overline{x})
. We then apply Lemma
1.4
to see
\text{Re}(y\overline{x})=\text{Re}(\overline{x}y)
.
\hfill\square

We define a multiplication action of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
on
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
in the obvious way; given
z\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
and
D=(x,y)\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
, then
zD=z(x,y)=(zx,zy)
and
Dz=(x,y)z=(xz,yz)
.

Lemma 1.39
.

\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
equipped with the map
(\cdot|\cdot):\ \mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}\times\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is a real inner product space.

Proof.

Let
D_{j}
have components
(x_{m},y_{m})
for
m=1,2,3
. The map is symmetric, as

(D_{1}|D_{2})=\text{Re}(\overline{x}_{1}x_{2}+\overline{y}_{1}y_{2})=\overline{\text{Re}(\overline{x}_{1}x_{2}+\overline{y}_{1}y_{2})}=\text{Re}(\overline{x}_{2}x_{1}+\overline{y}_{2}y_{1})=(D_{2}|D_{1}).

The second equality follows from the invariance of
\mathbb{R}
under Clifford conjugation. It is real-linear in the first entry; for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and
D_{1},D_{2},D_{3}\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
:

(aD_{1}+bD_{2}|D_{3})=\text{Re}((a\overline{x}_{1}+b\overline{x}_{2})x_{3}+(a\overline{y}_{1}+b\overline{y}_{2})y_{3})=a(D_{1}|D_{3})+b(D_{2}|D_{3}).

It is positive definite, as for
D=(x,y)\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}\neq(0,0)
:

(D|D)=\text{Re}(\overline{x}x+\overline{y}y)=\text{Re}(\overline{x}x)+\text{Re}(\overline{y}y)=x\cdot x+y\cdot y>0,

since the inner product on
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
was shown in Lemma
1.36
to be positive definite.
\hfill\square

In practice, we’re primarily interested in taking these forms on
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, or on pairs
\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{2}
. The following lemma collates properties of these two inner products that will prove useful.

Lemma 1.40
.

Let
D_{m}=(x_{m},y_{m})\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
for
m=\{1,2\}
,
K=(x,y)\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{2}
,
\alpha\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
,
c\in\mathbb{R}
,
b\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
satisfying
b+\overline{b}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

i)

(D_{1}\alpha|D_{2}\alpha)=|\alpha|^{2}(D_{1}|D_{2})
,

ii)

(Kc|Kb)=|K|^{2}c\cdot b
,

iii)

If
\text{Re}(b)=0
, then
(K|Kb)=(K|bK)=0
,

iv)

(K\mathbf{V}|K\mathbf{W})=|K|^{2}\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{W}
.

Proof.
We can show i) by a simple computation. As
\text{Re}(\overline{x}_{1}x_{2}+\overline{y}_{1}y_{2})=\text{Re}(x_{1}\overline{x}_{2}+y_{1}\overline{y}_{2})
, we compute

(D_{1}\alpha|D_{2}\alpha)=\text{Re}(x_{1}\alpha\overline{x_{2}\alpha}+y_{1}\alpha\overline{y_{2}\alpha})=\text{Re}\left(x_{1}|\alpha|^{2}\overline{x_{2}}+y_{1}|\alpha|^{2}\overline{y_{2}}\right)=|\alpha|^{2}(D_{1}|D_{2}).

For ii), we can also do a direct computation. Computing the left-hand side:

(Kc|Kb)=c\text{Re}(x\overline{xb}+y\overline{yb})=c\text{Re}(x\overline{b}\overline{x}+y\overline{b}\overline{y}).

As the real component is preserved under Clifford conjugation, we rewrite this as

(Kc|Kb)=\frac{c}{2}\text{Re}(x(b+\overline{b})\overline{x}+y(b+\overline{b})\overline{y}).

Because
b+\overline{b}=2\text{Re}(b)\in\mathbb{R}
we can simplify this to

c\text{Re}(b)\text{Re}(x\overline{x}+y\overline{y})=c\text{Re}(b)(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}).

Noting that
c\cdot b=\text{Re}(c\overline{b})=c\text{Re}(b)
, we conclude

(Kc|Kb)=(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})c\text{Re}(b)=|K|^{2}c\cdot b.

For iii), consider first having
b
on the left:

(K|bK)=\text{Re}(x\overline{bx}+y\overline{by})=\text{Re}((|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})\overline{b})=(|x|^{2}+|y|^{2})\text{Re}(\overline{b})=0.

If instead we have
b
on the right we can make use of ii):

(K|Kb)=|K|^{2}(1\cdot b)=|K|^{2}\text{Re}(\overline{b})=0.

For iv), we begin with a similar computation to ii).

(K\mathbf{V}|K\mathbf{W})=\text{Re}\left(x\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{W}}\overline{x}+y\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{W}}\overline{y}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\text{Re}\left(x\left(\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{W}}+\mathbf{W}\overline{\mathbf{V}}\right)\overline{x}+y\left(\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{W}}+\mathbf{W}\overline{\mathbf{V}}\right)\overline{y}\right).

\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{W}}
is a product of paravectors and thus has terms of degree
\leq 2
, which implies

\left(\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{W}}+\mathbf{W}\overline{\mathbf{V}}\right)=\left(\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{W}}+\overline{\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{W}}}\right)=2\text{Re}(\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{W}}).

Taken together, we conclude
(K\mathbf{V}|K\mathbf{W})=\text{Re}(x\overline{x}+y\overline{y})\text{Re}(\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{W}})=|K|^{2}\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{W}
.
\hfill\square

1.5.2.
The Generalised Hermitian Form

We can also define a ‘generalised Hermitian form’ that extends the Hermitian inner product of a complex vector space to our Clifford algebras.

Definition 1.41
.

Given
D_{m}=(x_{m},y_{m})\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
for
m=1,2
, we define

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}\times\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}\to\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n},\ \langle D_{1},D_{2}\rangle=\overline{x_{1}}x_{2}+\overline{y_{1}}y_{2}.

Note that
(D_{1}|D_{2})=\text{Re}(\langle D_{1},D_{2}\rangle)
. This form is not technically Hermitian as its range is not a subset of
\mathbb{C}
, but it is a straightforward extension where
\mathbb{C}
is replaced with some higher Clifford algebra and complex conjugation is replaced by the Clifford conjugate
\overline{\cdot}
.

Definition 1.42
.

A
generalised Hermitian form
on a real vector space
V
bearing a right
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
-module structure is a function
h:V\times V\to\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
such that for all
u,v,w\in V
and all
a,b\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{p,q}}
,

i)

h(u,va+wb)=h(u,v)a+h(u,w)b
,

ii)

h(u,v)=\overline{h(v,u)}
, where
\overline{\cdot}
is the Clifford conjugate.

\lrcorner

Lemma 1.43
.

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}\times\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}\to\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
is a generalised Hermitian form.

Proof.
We show the form is linear in the second argument. Let
D_{m}=(x_{m},y_{m})\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
for
m=1,2,3
and
a,b\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
.

\langle D_{1},D_{2}a+D_{3}b\rangle=(\overline{x_{1}}x_{2}+\overline{y_{1}}y_{2})a+(\overline{x_{1}}x_{3}+\overline{y_{1}}y_{3})b=\langle D_{1},D_{2}\rangle a+\langle D_{1},D_{3}\rangle b.

To be generalised Hermitian, it must be equal to its conjugate.

\overline{\langle D_{2},D_{1}\rangle}=\overline{\overline{x_{2}}x_{1}+\overline{y_{2}}y_{1}}=\overline{x_{1}}x_{2}+\overline{y_{1}}y_{2}=\langle D_{1},D_{2}\rangle.

This form has some nice properties of its own.

Lemma 1.44
.

Given
D_{m}=(x_{m},y_{m})\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
for
m=1,2
,
\alpha\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, and
b,c\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
,

i)

\langle\alpha D_{1},\alpha D_{2}\rangle=|\alpha|^{2}\langle D_{1},D_{2}\rangle
,

ii)

\langle D_{1}b,D_{2}c\rangle=\overline{b}\langle D_{1},D_{2}\rangle c
.

Proof.
A straightforward computation. For i),

\langle\alpha D_{1},\alpha D_{2}\rangle=\overline{x_{1}}(\overline{\alpha}\alpha)x_{2}+\overline{y_{1}}(\overline{\alpha}\alpha)y_{2}=|\alpha|^{2}\langle D_{1},D_{2}\rangle,

where we’ve used the antiautomorphism of the Clifford conjugate in the first equality. If we instead try right multiplication we find ii):

\langle D_{1}b,D_{2}c\rangle=\overline{b}(\overline{x_{1}}x_{2})c+\overline{b}(\overline{y_{1}}y_{2})c=\overline{b}\langle D_{1},D_{2}\rangle c.

The generalised Hermitian form also nicely restricts to a map on Lipschitz spinors.

Lemma 1.45
.

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
restricts to a map
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\times S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
.

Proof.
Let
\kappa_{1}=(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})
,
\kappa_{2}=(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. Assume first that all components are non-zero. Then

\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle=\overline{\xi_{1}}\xi_{2}+\overline{\eta_{1}}\eta_{2}=\overline{\eta_{1}}(\overline{\mathbf{V}_{1}}\xi_{2}+\eta_{2})=\overline{\eta_{1}}(\overline{\mathbf{V}_{1}}+\mathbf{V}_{2}^{-1})\xi_{2}

for the paravectors
\mathbf{V}_{1}=\xi_{1}\eta_{1}^{-1}
,
\mathbf{V}_{2}=\xi_{2}\eta_{2}^{-1}
. If
\overline{\mathbf{V}_{1}}+\mathbf{V}_{2}^{-1}\neq 0
then this is the product of 3 elements of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, which is closed under multiplication. If
\overline{\mathbf{V}_{1}}+\mathbf{V}_{2}^{-1}=0
then the form is zero. Both cases imply
\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
.

If any component
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
is zero, then
\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle
reduces to a single term which is a product of two elements of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
. Monoids are closed under multiplication, so in this case we can also conclude
\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
.
\hfill\square

1.5.3.
The Bracket

The
\mathbb{C}^{2}
space of complex spinors has a ‘natural’ symplectic form, given by putting the spinors into a matrix and taking the determinant. We can extend this to our noncommutative Lipschitz spinors using the pseudo-determinant.

Definition 1.46

(The Bracket)
.

Let
D_{m}=(x_{m},y_{m})
for
m=1,2
.
The bracket

\{\cdot,\cdot\}:\ \mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}\times\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}\to\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
is given by

\{D_{1},D_{2}\}=\text{pdet}\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&x_{2}\\
y_{1}&y_{2}\end{pmatrix}=x_{1}^{*}y_{2}-y_{1}^{*}x_{2}.

This form is
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
-sesquilinear on right-hand multiplication. For
a,b\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
and
D_{1},D_{2}\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\{D_{1},D_{2}(a+b)\}

\displaystyle=\{D_{1},D_{2}\}a+\{D_{1},D_{2}\}b,

\displaystyle\{D_{1}(a+b),D_{2}\}

\displaystyle=a^{*}\{D_{1},D_{2}\}+b^{*}\{D_{1},D_{2}\}.

The antisymmetry of the symplectic form on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is now modified, and includes the ‘reverse’ involution.

(1.47)

\{D_{2},D_{1}\}=-\{D_{1},D_{2}\}^{*}.

Note that, because of this loosened notion of antisymmetry,
\{D,D\}
is not necessarily zero for general elements
D\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
. The equation
x=-x^{*}
is solved by, for example,
x=i_{1}i_{2}
, so for
D=(1,i_{1}i_{2})
,

\{D,D\}=\text{pdet}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
i_{1}i_{2}&i_{1}i_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}=i_{1}i_{2}-i_{2}i_{1}=2i_{1}i_{2}.

When we restrict to Lipschitz spinors, we find things to be more nicely behaved.

Lemma 1.48
.

given
a\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
and
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
,
\{\kappa,\kappa a\}=0
.

Proof.

\{\kappa,\kappa a\}=0
is equivalent to
(\xi^{*}\eta-\eta^{*}\xi)a=0
. By Corollary
1.31
we have
\xi^{*}\eta=\eta^{*}\xi
, reducing the above to
0a=0
which is vacuously true as
a
is finite.
\hfill\square

If we put a Lipschitz spinor
\kappa
in the left-hand entry of
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
, Lemma
1.48
gives the entire kernel.

Lemma 1.49
.

The kernel of the map

\{\kappa,\cdot\}:\ \mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}\to\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n},\ \kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}

is precisely
\kappa a
for
a\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
.

Proof.
Fix a spinor
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, and let
D=(x,y)\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
be a prospective element of the kernel. If
\xi=0
, then
\eta\neq 0
and

\{\kappa,D\}=-\eta^{*}x=0\implies x=0.

We can rewrite
y=\eta a
for
a=\eta^{-1}y
, implying
D=(0,\eta a)=\kappa a
. A similar argument holds when
\eta=0
.

Assume, then, that
\xi,\eta\neq 0
. We want to find all
D=(x,y)\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
satisfying

\{\kappa,D\}=\xi^{*}y-\eta^{*}x=0\implies\xi^{*}y=\eta^{*}x.

Rewrite
x=\xi a
for
a=\xi^{-1}x
, and similarly
y=\eta b
for
b=\eta^{-1}y
. As
\xi
and
\eta
are invertible, we can rewrite
x
and
y
in this form without loss of generality, implying
\xi^{*}\eta b=\eta^{*}\xi a
. From Corollary
1.31
we have
\xi^{*}\eta=\eta^{*}\xi
, and both of these expressions are invertible. Therefore
a=b
and
D=(\xi a,\eta a)=\kappa a
. This is enough to show both that elements of the kernel take the form
\kappa a
, and the converse.
\hfill\square

We will mostly be applying the bracket to Lipschitz spinors, and when we do everything restricts to live in
\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
.

Lemma 1.50
.

The bracket restricts to a map

\{\cdot,\cdot\}:S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\times S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}.

Proof.
Consider two Lipschitz spinors
\kappa_{1}=(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})
,
\kappa_{2}=(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. Assume first that all the components are non-zero.

\xi_{1}^{*}\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}^{*}\xi_{2}=\eta_{1}^{*}((\eta_{1}^{*})^{-1}\xi_{1}^{*}\eta_{2}-\xi_{2})=\eta_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{V}_{1}\eta_{2}-\xi_{2})=\eta_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{V}_{1}-\xi_{2}\eta_{2}^{-1})\eta_{2}^{-1}=\eta_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{V}_{1}-\mathbf{V}_{2})\eta_{2}^{-1},

for
\mathbf{V}_{1}=\xi_{1}\eta_{1}^{-1}
,
\mathbf{V}_{2}=\xi_{2}\eta_{2}^{-1}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, and since all non-zero paravectors are included in
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, this is a product of elements of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
and is thus contained in
\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
. If
\mathbf{V}_{1}=\mathbf{V}_{2}
then the bracket maps to
0
, otherwise it maps to
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
.

If any components
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
are zero, then
\{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\}
reduces to a single product of 2 elements of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
. As the monoid
\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
is closed under multiplication, the bracket restricts as claimed.
\hfill\square

How does our bracket interact with the action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
?

Lemma 1.51
.

Precomposition of both inputs with the action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
preserves the bracket operation.

Proof.
Pick an element of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
:

A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1}).

Act on two elements
D_{m}=(x_{m},y_{m})\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
for
m=1,2
, and take their bracket. After simplification, we find

\{AD_{1},AD_{2}\}=x_{1}^{*}(a^{*}d-c^{*}b)y_{2}+y_{1}^{*}(b^{*}c-d^{*}a)x_{2}.

the expressions in brackets are copies (or conjugates) of the pseudo-determinant.
By definition, pdet
(A)=1
, and this reduces to

\{AD_{1},AD_{2}\}=x_{1}^{*}y_{2}-y_{1}^{*}x_{2}=\{D_{1},D_{2}\}.

1.6.
Complementary Elements and a ‘Kähler-like’ Structure

For
\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, the conjugate transpose
\kappa^{\dagger}
satisfies
\kappa^{\dagger}\kappa=|\kappa|^{2}
. With the addition of the bracket operation, we define a new form of ‘conjugate’; the
complement

\check{\kappa}
, satisfying
\{\kappa,\check{\kappa}\}=-|\kappa|^{2}
.

Definition 1.52

(Complementary Structure)
.

Given
D=(x,y)\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
, we define
\check{D}=(y^{\prime},-x^{\prime})
.

\lrcorner

Restricting again to
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, the map
\kappa\to\check{\kappa}
is something like a complex structure in a Kähler manifold. The complementary element
\check{\kappa}
is orthogonal to
\kappa
under the inner product
(\cdot|\cdot)
on
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
; in fact, as the following lemma shows, the entire subspace of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
generated by the complement is orthogonal to that generated by
\kappa
.

Lemma 1.53
.

Let
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
and
x,y\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
.

i)

\check{\kappa}\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
,

ii)

(\kappa x|\check{\kappa}y)=0
,

iii)

\langle\kappa,\check{\kappa}\rangle=0
.

Proof.
Given
\check{\kappa}
, we write
[\check{\kappa}]_{j}
for the
j
th component. For i), as
\xi\overline{\eta}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
then

[\check{\kappa}]_{1}\overline{[\check{\kappa}]_{2}}=-\eta^{\prime}\overline{\xi^{\prime}}=-\eta^{\prime}\xi^{*}=-(\xi\overline{\eta})^{*}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

as
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
is closed under the conjugations and negation, as is
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. For ii), first assume
\xi\eta^{-1}=\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Compute the left-hand side:

(\kappa x|\check{\kappa}y)=\text{Re}(\xi x\overline{\eta^{\prime}y}-\eta x\overline{\xi^{\prime}y})=\text{Re}(\mathbf{V}\eta x\overline{y}\eta^{*}-\eta x\overline{y}\eta^{*}\mathbf{V})=\text{Re}(AB-BA),

where
A=\mathbf{V}=\xi\eta^{-1},\ B=\eta x\overline{y}\eta^{*}
. Then Lemma
1.4
implies
(\kappa x|\check{\kappa}y)=0
. If
\xi\eta^{-1}=\infty
instead, then
\eta=\eta^{*}=0
and
(\kappa x|\check{\kappa}y)=\text{Re}(\xi x\overline{y}\eta^{*}-\eta x\overline{y}\xi^{*})=0
. For iii), we simply compute
\langle\kappa,\check{\kappa}\rangle=\overline{\xi}\eta^{\prime}-\overline{\eta}\xi^{\prime}=(\xi^{*}\eta)^{\prime}-(\eta^{*}\xi)^{\prime}=0
.
\hfill\square

Part ii) of the above lemma suggests there may be a direct sum decomposition of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
in terms of ‘things generated by
\kappa
’ and ‘things generated by
\check{\kappa}
’.

Corollary 1.54
.

Fix a spinor
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. Then

\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}=\kappa\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}\oplus\check{\kappa}\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}.

In particular,

(a,b)\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}=\kappa x+\check{\kappa}y

for

x=\frac{\overline{\xi}a+\overline{\eta}b}{|\kappa|^{2}},\ y=\frac{\eta^{*}a-\xi^{*}b}{|\kappa|^{2}}.

Proof.
Let
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)
, and let
D=(a,b)\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
. We wish to show
D=\kappa x+\check{\kappa}y
for some
x,y\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
. Expanding this expression in terms of components and solving the simultaneous equations, we find

x=\frac{\overline{\xi}a+\overline{\eta}b}{|\kappa|^{2}},\ y=\frac{\eta^{*}a-\xi^{*}b}{|\kappa|^{2}}.

We can check they give
D
as claimed:

\xi x+\eta^{\prime}y=\frac{|\xi|^{2}a+\xi\overline{\eta}b+|\eta|^{2}a-\eta^{\prime}\xi^{*}b}{|\kappa|^{2}}=\frac{|\kappa|^{2}a+\xi\overline{\eta}b-(\xi\overline{\eta})^{*}b}{|\kappa|^{2}}=a,

where the last equality follows because
\xi\overline{\eta}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is invariant under
\cdot^{*}
. Similarly
\eta x-\xi^{\prime}y=b
, so we can write every element of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
in this form, and by Lemma
1.53
ii) these subspaces are orthogonal and this expression will be unique.
\hfill\square

As an extension of the orthogonality of
\kappa
and
\check{\kappa}
we have the following technical lemma, which will be of use when constructing null flags.

Lemma 1.55
.

Let
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. Then any row vector
\tau=(\alpha,\beta)\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{2}\backslash\{0,0\}
satisfying
\tau\kappa=0
must satisfy
\tau\check{\kappa}\neq 0
. Similarly, any row vector
\tau=(\alpha,\beta)\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{2}\backslash\{0,0\}
satisfying
\tau\check{\kappa}=0
must satisfy
\tau\kappa\neq 0
.

Proof.
Consider the sets
T_{\kappa}
,
T_{\check{\kappa}}
of row vectors sending
\kappa
or
\check{\kappa}
to zero, respectively.

T_{\kappa}=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{2}\backslash\{(0,0)\}\ |\ \alpha\xi+\beta\eta=0\},

T_{\check{\kappa}}=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{2}\backslash\{(0,0)\}\ |\ \alpha\eta^{\prime}-\beta\xi^{\prime}=0\}.

If, say,
\xi=0
, the sets are clearly distinct, because the set
T_{(0,\eta)}=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{2}\backslash\{(0,0)\}\ |\ \beta\eta=0\}
consists of
(\alpha,\beta)=(\alpha,0)
, while
T_{\widecheck{(0,\eta)}}=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{2}\backslash\{(0,0)\}\ |\ \alpha\eta^{\prime}=0\}
consists of
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,\beta)
. A similar fact holds if
\eta=0
(and we cannot have
\xi=\eta=0
), so we assume going forward that all components are non-zero.

If both
\xi,\eta
are non-zero, then
T_{\kappa}
consists of elements
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})=\beta_{1}(-\eta\xi^{-1},1)\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{2}\backslash\{(0,0)\}
parameterised by
\beta_{1}\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
(which excludes zero), while
T_{\check{\kappa}}
is given by elements
(\alpha_{2},\beta_{2})=\beta_{2}(\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime-1},1)\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{2}\backslash\{(0,0)\}
, and is parameterised by
\beta_{2}\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
(which, again, must be non-zero). These sets are nonempty as we can choose
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=1
.

Say we found a row vector
\tau
that is within both sets. Equating the second entries implies
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}
, and equating the first implies

(1.56)

\eta\xi^{-1}=-\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime-1}.

As
(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
we know
\eta\xi^{-1}=(\xi\eta^{-1})^{-1}=\mathbf{V}
is a paravector, and so is
\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime-1}=(\xi\eta^{-1})^{\prime}=(\mathbf{V}^{-1})^{\prime}
. Applying this to (
1.56
):

\mathbf{V}=-(\mathbf{V}^{-1})^{\prime}=-\mathbf{V}|\mathbf{V}|^{-2}\implies 0=\mathbf{V}(1+|\mathbf{V}|^{-2}).

Because
|\cdot|\geq 0
(and therefore
(1+|\mathbf{V}|^{-2})>0
), we conclude
\eta\xi^{-1}=\mathbf{V}=0
. But this contradicts our assumption that
\xi,\eta\neq 0
; the sets must be disjoint.
\hfill\square

The generalised Hermitian product, the bracket, and the map
\check{\cdot}
together form a ‘Kähler-like’ structure on
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
; the bracket plays the role of the symplectic form, the generalised Hermitian product that of the Hermitian metric, and the map
\check{\cdot}
that of the almost complex structure. It isn’t actually a Kähler structure in general;
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
isn’t actually Hermitian, and the bracket (with its added reversal conjugation) isn’t quite skew-symmetric. But the analogy is strong, as the following pair of results show.

Proposition 1.57
.

Given elements
D_{m}=(x_{m},y_{m})\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
for
m=1,2
,
\langle D_{1},D_{2}\rangle=\{\check{D}_{1},D_{2}\}
.

Proof.

\{\check{D}_{1},D_{2}\}=(\eta_{1}^{\prime})^{*}\eta_{2}-(-\xi_{1}^{\prime})^{*}\xi_{2}=\overline{\xi_{1}}\xi_{2}+\overline{\eta_{1}}\eta_{2}=\langle D_{1},D_{2}\rangle
.
\hfill\square

Restricting to the real component, this then implies

Corollary 1.58
.

Given elements
D_{m}=(x_{m},y_{m})\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
for
m=1,2
,
(D_{1}|D_{2})=\text{Re}\left(\{\check{D}_{1},D_{2}\}\right)
.
\hfill\square

The complementary element also provides a nice way to embed
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
into
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
, by the following simple construction.

Proposition 1.59
.

For any
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
,

A_{\kappa}=\begin{pmatrix}\kappa&-\frac{\check{\kappa}}{|\kappa|^{2}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&-\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{|\xi|^{2}+|\eta|^{2}}\\
\eta&\frac{\xi^{\prime}}{|\xi|^{2}+|\eta|^{2}}\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1}).

Proof.
Both
\kappa
and
\check{\kappa}
are spinors (see Lemma
1.53
i)), which implies their components are elements of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
, which is condition i) for Clifford matrices (see Definition
1.26
). Combined with Lemma
1.30
(and the property
\xi\overline{\eta}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
for spinors
(\xi,\eta)
), we also get condition iii). All that’s left is condition ii), which requires that the pseudo-determinant be
1
.

\text{pdet}(A_{\kappa})=\xi^{*}\frac{\xi^{\prime}}{|\kappa|^{2}}+\eta^{*}\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{|\kappa|^{2}}=\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{|\kappa|^{2}}+\frac{|\eta|^{2}}{|\kappa|^{2}}=1.

1.7.
Climbing the Clifford Algebra Ladder

The Cayley-Dickson construction is a process for building a sequence of algebras. It begins with the division algebras
20
20

20

Denoting the real numbers
\mathbb{R}
, the complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
, the quaternions
\mathbb{H}
, and the octonions
\mathbb{O}
.

\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H},\mathbb{O}
over the reals, and is used to prove various theorems about these algebras.

With a slight tweak, we can apply the method to instead construct the Clifford algebras. The following construction is a variation
21
21

21

There’s more than one way to embed pairs of elements of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{0,n}}
into
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{0,n+1}}
; we’ve chosen to vary Lounesto’s formulation to play nicely with right-multiplication in the other structures we define.
on that found in
[
19
]
, Chapter 21.

Lemma 1.60

(Iterative Clifford Algebras)
.

Consider pairs
(u,v)\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
. Then
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{0,n+1}}
is isomorphic to the algebra of such pairs equipped with the product

(u_{1},v_{1})\cdot(u_{2},v_{2})=(u_{1}u_{2}-v_{1}^{\prime}v_{2},u_{1}^{\prime}v_{2}+v_{1}u_{2}).

Proof.
Every element of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{0,n+1}}
can be uniquely written as
u+i_{n+1}v
for
u,v\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
, where
i_{n+1}v
groups all terms containing the generator
i_{n+1}
and
u
contains all other terms. We can take a product of two such elements:
