(u_{1}+i_{n+1}v_{1})(u_{2}+i_{n+1}v_{2})=u_{1}u_{2}+i_{n+1}v_{1}u_{2}+u_{1}i_{n+1}v_{2}+i_{n+1}v_{1}i_{n+1}v_{2},

and, because
u,\ v
do not contain
i_{n+1}
, commuting it past them has the effect of sending
i_{j}\to-i_{j}
for
j\in\{1,2,...,n\}
as it passes. So we can write

(u_{1}+i_{n+1}v_{1})(u_{2}+i_{n+1}v_{2})=(u_{1}u_{2}-v_{1}^{\prime}v_{2})+i_{n+1}(v_{1}u_{2}+u_{1}^{\prime}v_{2}),

which confirms the claim.
\hfill\square

Given some
z=(u,v)
as an element of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{0,n+1}}
, the conjugate
\overline{z}
has components
(\overline{u},-v^{*})
; the ‘imaginary’ coefficient has a different conjugation as the generator
i_{n+1}
has to commute to the left-hand side after applying the anti-involution. This gives us a different way to view
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
, as an embedding in a higher Clifford algebra; and it dovetails nicely with the Lipschitz spinor structure.

Lemma 1.61
.

Given a pair of Lipschitz spinors
\kappa_{m}=(\xi_{m},\eta_{m})\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
for
m=1,2
, if we write
z_{m}=\xi_{m}+i_{n+1}\eta_{m}\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{{0,n+1}}
then
\overline{z_{1}}z_{2}=\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle+i_{n+1}\{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\}
.

Proof.
We apply the Cayley-Dickson type construction of Lemma
1.60
.

\hfill\square

\displaystyle\overline{z_{1}}z_{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{\xi_{1}}\xi_{2}-(-\eta_{1}^{*})^{\prime}\eta_{2},\overline{\xi_{1}}^{\prime}\eta_{2}+(-\eta_{1}^{*})\xi_{2}\right)=\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle+i_{n+1}\{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\}.

We see the two forms naturally arise in the embedding of Lipschitz spinors
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
into
\mathcal{C}\ell_{{0,n+1}}
. And, in fact, they are the components of a spinor themselves.

Lemma 1.62
.

Let
\kappa_{1}=(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})
,
\kappa_{2}=(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. Then
\left(\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle,\{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\}\right)
is a Lipschitz spinor.

Proof.
Both the forms are elements of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
(see Lemma
1.45
and Lemma
1.50
), so all we need to check is that
\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle\overline{\{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\}}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

\displaystyle(\overline{\xi_{1}}\xi_{2}+\overline{\eta_{1}}\eta_{2})\overline{(\xi_{1}^{*}\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}^{*}\xi_{2})}

\displaystyle=\overline{\xi_{1}}\xi_{2}\overline{\eta_{2}}\xi_{1}^{\prime}-\overline{\xi_{1}}\xi_{2}\overline{\xi_{2}}\eta_{1}^{\prime}+\overline{\eta_{1}}\eta_{2}\overline{\eta_{2}}\xi_{1}^{\prime}-\overline{\eta_{1}}\eta_{2}\overline{\xi_{2}}\eta_{1}^{\prime}.

We substitute
\mathbf{V}_{j}=\xi_{j}\overline{\eta_{j}}
, and
\mathbf{W}_{j}=\xi_{j}^{*}\eta_{j}
.

(1.63)

\displaystyle\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle\overline{\{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\}}

\displaystyle=\sigma(\overline{\xi_{1}})(\mathbf{V}_{2})-\sigma(\overline{\eta_{1}})(\overline{\mathbf{V}_{2}})+(|\eta_{2}|^{2}-|\xi_{2}|^{2})\mathbf{W}_{1}^{\prime}.

All these components are paravectors, so
\langle\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\rangle\overline{\{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\}}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and the claim follows.
\hfill\square

1.8.
The Tangent Bundle of
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}

As
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
is a vector space, its tangent space at any point is naturally isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
.
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
is a submanifold of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
, and thus the tangent spaces of
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
at varying points are isomorphic to varying subspaces of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
. That is, we can write tangent vectors to
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
as pairs of elements in the Clifford algebra.

Theorem 1.64
.

The tangent space
T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
at
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
can be characterised as

(1.65)

T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}=\{(a,b)\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}\ |\ a\overline{\eta}+\xi\overline{b}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\}.

It decomposes into real orthogonal subspaces (under
(\cdot|\cdot)
) as

T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}=\kappa\mathbb{R}\oplus\kappa\left(\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\oplus{\bigwedge}^{\!2}\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\right)\oplus\check{\kappa}\left(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right).

It can be explicitly constructed as a subspace of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
; if
\eta\neq 0
, then

T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}=\{(\eta^{\prime}\mathbf{V}+\xi H,\eta H)\ |\ \mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\ H\in\$\mathfrak{g}_{n+1}\},

and if
\xi\neq 0
then

T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}=\{(\xi\Xi,\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{U}+\eta\Xi)\ |\ \mathbf{U}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\ \Xi\in\$\mathfrak{g}_{n+1}\}.

Proof.
Points
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
satisfy
\xi\overline{\eta}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. For the first characterisation, we take a small variation and differentiate. For some
a,b\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
we have:

(\xi+ta)\overline{(\eta+tb)}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\implies a\overline{\eta}+\xi\overline{b}\in T_{\xi\overline{\eta}}\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\cong\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

This implies (
1.65
). Consider the subset
\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
(recalling
\mathcal{B}^{n}
are the bi-paravectors). We check that they satisfy (
1.65
); for
(\xi B,\eta B)\in\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}
:

a\overline{\eta}+\xi\overline{b}=\xi B\overline{\eta}+\xi\overline{B}\overline{\eta}=\xi(B+\overline{B})\overline{\eta}.

Since bi-paravectors only have terms of degree 2 or less,
B+\overline{B}=2\text{Re}(B)
.

a\overline{\eta}+\xi\overline{b}=2\text{Re}(B)\xi\overline{\eta}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

So
\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}
is contained in
T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. The space
\mathcal{B}^{n}
can be decomposed into ‘real’ and ‘non-real’ components (‘non-real’ components are anything of degree 1 or higher):

\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}=\kappa\mathbb{R}\oplus\kappa\left(\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\oplus{\bigwedge}^{\!2}\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\right),

and, because purely imaginary bi-paravectors
B
satisfy
B+\overline{B}=0
, we appeal to Lemma
1.40
iii) to see that the subspaces
\kappa\mathbb{R}
and
\kappa\left(\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\oplus{\bigwedge}^{\!2}\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\right)
are orthogonal.

Now consider the subspace
\check{\kappa}(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
. We check it sits within the tangent space
T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
using (
1.65
):

\eta^{\prime}\mathbf{V}\overline{\eta}-\xi\overline{\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{V}}=\sigma(\eta^{\prime})(\mathbf{V})-\sigma(\xi)(\overline{\mathbf{V}})\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

It follows from Lemma
1.53
ii) that this subspace is orthogonal to
\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}
under
(\cdot|\cdot)
. The total dimension of
\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}\oplus\check{\kappa}(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
matches the dimension of
T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, so we conclude they are isomorphic and the tangent space decomposes as

T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}=\kappa\mathbb{R}\oplus\kappa\left(\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\oplus{\bigwedge}^{\!2}\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\right)\oplus\check{\kappa}\left(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right).

For the latter two characterisations we start instead with the pair of conditions

(1.66)

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=\mathbf{V}\eta,\ \mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

(1.67)

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}\xi,\ \mathbf{W}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

The first case applies when
\eta\neq 0
, and the second when
\xi\neq 0
.

Consider a curve
\kappa_{t}=(\xi_{t},\eta_{t})
in
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
for
t\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
that passes through the point
(\xi_{0},\eta_{0})=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
at
t=0
. Define
\mathbf{V}_{t}=\xi_{t}\eta^{-1}_{t}
(restricting
\varepsilon
as necessary), as
\eta_{t}\neq 0
over a small interval around
t=0
by continuity. As this curve lies within the manifold of Lipschitz spinors, it satisfies the pair of conditions (
1.66
), (
1.67
).

Deriving (
1.66
) gives a condition on the derivative of
\xi
:

\dot{\xi_{t}}=\dot{\mathbf{V}}_{t}\eta_{t}+\mathbf{V}_{t}\dot{\eta}_{t}=\dot{\mathbf{V}}_{t}\eta_{t}+\xi_{t}\eta^{-1}_{t}\dot{\eta}_{t}.

At
t=0
,
(\xi_{t},\eta_{t})=(\xi,\eta)
and the tangent space will then be
(\dot{\mathbf{V}}_{0}\eta+\xi\eta^{-1}\dot{\eta}_{0},\dot{\eta}_{0})
for arbitrary choices of
(\dot{\mathbf{V}}_{0},\dot{\eta}_{0})\in\left(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right)\times T_{\eta}\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. If we instead take
\xi\neq 0
and apply the same arguments to (
1.67
), the tangent vectors take the form
(\dot{\xi}_{0},\dot{\mathbf{W}}_{0}\xi+\eta\xi^{-1}\dot{\xi}_{0})
for arbitrary choices of
(\dot{\mathbf{W}}_{0},\dot{\xi}_{0})\in\left(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right)\times T_{\xi}\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. Renaming the arbitrary derivatives, these arguments imply that tangent vectors can be written in the form

(1.68)

(\mathbf{V}\eta+\xi\eta^{-1}G,G)\subset\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2},

for
\eta\neq 0,\ \mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\ G\in T_{\eta}\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, and

(1.69)

(N,\mathbf{U}\xi+\eta\xi^{-1}N)\subset\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2},

for
\xi\neq 0,\ \mathbf{U}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\ N\in T_{\xi}\$\Gamma_{n+1}
.

Because
G=\eta H
for
H\in\$\mathfrak{g}_{n+1}
and
N=\xi\Xi
for
\Xi\in\$\mathfrak{g}_{n+1}
, by Corollary
1.24
we find the form given in the theorem statement. The above arguments imply
T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
is a subset of the vector spaces (
1.68
) and (
1.69
) wherever those spaces are defined, but as the dimensions agree these spaces characterise the tangent space fully.
\hfill\square

The tangent vectors in the kernel of
\{\kappa,\cdot\}
will prove useful for a later theorem, so we provide the following corollary to the larger characterisation of tangent spaces.

Corollary 1.70
.

Given
\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, if
a\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
satisfies
\kappa a\in T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
then
a\in\mathcal{B}^{n}
and
a+\overline{a}=2\text{Re}(a)\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.
Lemma
1.53
ii) tells us the subspaces generated by multiples of
\kappa
and
\check{\kappa}
in
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
are orthogonal under
(\cdot|\cdot)
; so, if
\kappa a\in T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, it cannot be within the
\check{\kappa}
summand of the orthogonal decomposition of Theorem
1.64
, and must lie within the
\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}
summand. This implies
a
is a bi-paravector, which can be expressed as the sum of monomials of degree at most 2. Elements of degree
1
and
2
are sent to their negative by Clifford conjugation, so
a+\overline{a}
is real.
\hfill\square

1.8.1.
Sections of the Tangent Bundle

Following
[
23
]
, we define the scaling factor of a conformal map.

Definition 1.71
.

Let
V,W
be real vector spaces equipped with nondegenerate bilinear symmetric real-valued forms
(\cdot,\cdot)_{V}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)_{W}
. Let
f:V\to W
be a conformal linear map. The real constant
K
such that for all
V_{1},V_{2}\in V
,

(f(V_{1}),f(V_{2}))_{W}=K(V_{1},V_{2})_{V},

is the
scaling factor
of
f
.

\lrcorner

We define a family of sections of
TS\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, parameterised by paravectors.

Definition 1.72
.

For any
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, the section
s_{\mathbf{V}}:S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to TS\$\Gamma_{n+1}
of
TS\$\Gamma_{n+1}
is
s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)=\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}
.
More generally, the family of such sections forms a map

s:\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\times S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to TS\$\Gamma_{n+1},\ s(\mathbf{V},\kappa)=s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)=\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}.

These sections behave nicely under the bracket, as
\{\kappa,s_{\mathbf{V}}\kappa\}=\{\kappa,\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}\}=-|\kappa|^{2}\mathbf{V}
. They are real linear; for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,

s_{a\mathbf{V}+b\mathbf{W}}(\kappa)=\check{\kappa}(a\mathbf{V}+b\mathbf{W})=a\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}+b\check{\kappa}\mathbf{W}=as_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)+bs_{\mathbf{W}}(\kappa).

As
\check{\kappa}(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
is a real-linear subspace of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
, it has an inner product and norm induced by the inner product
(\cdot|\cdot)
on
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
. That is, the inner product
(\cdot|\cdot)
can be applied to this family of sections of
TS\$\Gamma_{n+1}
.

Lemma 1.73
.

For any
\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, the map
s(\cdot,\kappa)
is conformal with scaling factor
|\kappa|^{2}
.

Proof.
Since
s(\cdot,\kappa)
sends
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
to
\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}
, for all
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}|\check{\kappa}\mathbf{W})=|\check{\kappa}|^{2}\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{W}
by Lemma
1.40
iv). We also have that
|\check{\kappa}|=|\kappa|
, implying the claimed result.
\hfill\square

Later, we will consider the isomorphic copy of
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
within the decomposition of
T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
by quotienting out the other summands to get
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\cong T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}/\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}
. The points in this quotient space are the affine
\left(\tfrac{n^{2}+n}{2}+1\right)
-planes in
T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
of the form
\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}+\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}
for
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. The following characterisation will then prove useful, identifying the set of points forming these affine planes using the bracket.

Lemma 1.74
.

Let
\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
,
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, and
w\in T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. Then
w\in s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)+\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}
if and only if
\{\kappa,w\}=-\mathbf{V}|\kappa|^{2}
.

Proof.
We have that
\{\kappa,s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)\}=-\mathbf{V}|\kappa|^{2}
. If
w\in s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)+\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}
with representative
s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)+\kappa B
, then applying properties of the bracket we find:

\{\kappa,w\}=\{\kappa,s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)\}+\{\kappa,\kappa B\}=\{\kappa,s_{\mathbf{V}}\kappa\}=-\mathbf{V}|\kappa|^{2}.

The second equality holds for all
B\in\mathcal{B}^{n}
by Lemma
1.48
.

Conversely, if
\{\kappa,w\}=-\mathbf{V}|\kappa|^{2}
then
\{\kappa,w-s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)\}=0
by linearity and by Lemma
1.49

w=s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)+\kappa x
for some
x\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
. But if
w\in TS\$\Gamma_{n+1}
then
\kappa x\in\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}
, by Corollary
1.70
.
\hfill\square

We can introduce an inner product on the quotient as well, following Definition 3.11.4 of
[
21
]
. As noted in that work, there are several ways to introduce an inner product, but as the direct sum decomposition is orthogonal and all spaces involved are positive definite they all agree.

Definition 1.75
.

Let
\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. We define a positive definite inner product

(\cdot|\cdot):T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}/\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}\times T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}/\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}

as follows. Each element of the quotient is of the form
\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}+\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}
for a unique
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
; for
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
we define

\left(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}+\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}|\check{\kappa}\mathbf{W}+\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}\right)=(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}|\check{\kappa}\mathbf{W}),

where
(\cdot|\cdot)
is the inner product
22
22

22

It is because of this close association to the inner product on
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
that we use the same notation for each.
on
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
.

\lrcorner

We see this inner product is positive definite since Lemma
1.40
iv) tells us
(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}|\check{\kappa}\mathbf{W})=|\check{\kappa}|^{2}\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{W}
, and the dot product on
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is positive-definite.

2.
Light-Cones, Multiflags, and Decorated Ideal Points

Our first geometric realisation of the Lipschitz spinors
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
follows the path established by Penrose and Rindler
[
27
]
, relating the spinor to a null flag structure in Minkowski space. We’ll begin with some definitions and facts about the
generalised Minkowski spaces

\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
.

2.1.
Geometry of Generalised Minkowski Space

We may write points in
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
either as a vector
23
23

23

Unless otherwise specified this will be intended ordering of the variables. T and Z are separated by a semicolon to remind us that the other space variables are collected in a paravector
\mathbf{X}
; essentially, mimicking the matrix presentation.

(2.1)

(T,Z;X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{n})_{\mathbb{R}}=(T,Z;\mathbf{X})_{\mathbb{R}}

with quadratic form
dT^{2}-dZ^{2}-...-dX_{n}^{2}
, or as a
paravector Hermitian matrix
.

Definition 2.2

(Paravector Hermitian Matrices)
.

The paravector Hermitian matrices
\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2}
are the matrices in
\mathcal{M}(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
of the form
24
24

24

The factor of
\tfrac{1}{2}
ensures the trace is precisely
T
.

\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}T+Z&\mathbf{X}\\
\overline{\mathbf{X}}&T-Z\end{pmatrix}

for
T,Z\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{X}=X_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}X_{j}i_{j}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with
X_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
for
j\in\{0,1,...,n\}
. They provide an embedding of
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
into the space
\mathcal{M}(2,\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
of
2\times 2
matrices with
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
entries, generalising the standard embedding of
\mathbb{R}^{1,3}
as the Hermitian matrices. Just like standard Hermitian matrices, they satisfy the defining property

P=P^{\dagger},\ P\in\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2},

where
P^{\dagger}=(\overline{P})^{T}
is the conjugate transpose.

\lrcorner

\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
and
\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2}
are isomorphic vector spaces; we default to writing
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
for both, unless we want to draw attention to the matrix form. Denote the generalised Minkowski metric
dT^{2}-dZ^{2}-...-dX_{n}^{2}
by
(\cdot|\cdot)_{1,n+2}
, and the norm by
|p|^{2}=(p|p)_{1,n+2}
for
p\in\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
.

The set of points
\{p\in\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}:\ (p|p)_{1,n+2}=0\}
forms the
light-cone

L
, while the restricted set with positive
T
coordinate form the positive light-cone
L^{+}=\{p=(T,Z;\textbf{X})\in\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}:\ |p|^{2}=0\text{ and }T>0\}
.

For a point
p=(T,Z;\mathbf{X})\in\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
corresponding to a paravector Hermitian matrix
S_{p}\in\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2}
, we have the following properties:

\text{Tr }S_{p}=T,\quad 4\,\text{pdet }S_{p}=|p|^{2}.

We define the
celestial sphere
, or the
sphere at infinity
, to be the quotient of the positive light-cone
L^{+}
by the relation
p\sim kp,k\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. That is, the celestial sphere is the space of light-like directions (or rays)
\ell
from the origin with positive
T
coordinate. Denote the celestial sphere by
\mathscr{S}^{+}
. It can be modelled concretely by taking a slice of
L^{+}
at a fixed time
T
, as each point of the slice will be a representative of an equivalence class in
L^{+}/{\sim}
; we call such a model the
celestial sphere at constant
T
, and denote it by
\mathscr{S}^{+}_{T}
. The celestial sphere and the models at constant
T
are naturally diffeomorphic, and both are diffeomorphic to the hypersphere
S^{n+1}
.

2.1.1.
The Tangent Spaces to Light-Cones

Penrose and Rindler’s null flag lives in the tangent bundle to the light-cone. To extend it, we need a description of the tangent space to
L^{+}
in
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
.

Lemma 2.3
.

In a quadratic vector space
V
with metric
(\cdot,\cdot)
, let
L_{K}
be the manifold of points
x\in V
satisfying
(x,x)=K
for fixed
K\in\mathbb{R}
. At a point
p\in L_{K}
where
L_{k}
is locally a smooth manifold, the tangent space is
p^{\perp}
, the space of vectors in
V
orthogonal to
p
.

Proof.
Let
N_{p}\subset L_{K}
be a neighbourhood of
p
. At
p
, all tangent vectors can be constructed as the derivative of some smooth curve
p_{t}\subset N_{p}\subset L_{K}
,
t\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
for some
\varepsilon>0
satisfying
p_{0}=p
. Because
p_{t}\in L_{K}
it satisfies
(p_{t},p_{t})=K
, and we differentiate this relation to find
2(p_{t},\dot{p}_{t})=0
, which, at
t=0
, implies the tangent vector
\dot{p}_{0}
is orthogonal to the position vector
p_{0}=p
, implying
T_{p}L_{K}\subseteq p^{\perp}
. But the dimensions agree, so
T_{p}L_{K}=p^{\perp}
.
\hfill\square

Accordingly, we have
T_{p}L^{+}=p^{\perp}
. Following
[
21
]
, we parlay this into a description of the tangent space of the celestial sphere by taking a quotient. If
p\in L^{+}
has
T
coordinate
T_{0}
and
\ell=p\mathbb{R}
is the associated ray in
L^{+}
, projectivisation (that is, taking the space of 1 dimensional vector subspaces) yields an isomorphism
T_{\ell}\mathscr{S}^{+}\cong T_{p}\mathscr{S}_{T_{0}}^{+}
. The light-cone has product structure
L^{+}\cong S^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
, and accordingly we get an orthogonal decomposition of the tangent space:

T_{p}L^{+}=p^{\perp}=\ell^{\perp}=p\mathbb{R}\oplus T_{p}\mathscr{S}_{T_{0}}^{+}=\ell\oplus T_{p}\mathscr{S}_{T_{0}}^{+},

and a corresponding description of the tangent space of the celestial sphere:

T_{p}\mathscr{S}_{T}^{+}\cong T_{\ell}\mathscr{S}^{+}\cong\frac{T_{p}L^{+}}{p\mathbb{R}}=\frac{p^{\perp}}{p\mathbb{R}}=\frac{\ell^{\perp}}{\ell}.

2.1.2.
Orientations in Minkowski Space

We use the following orientation conventions (which are a variation on those in
[
21
]
; note that here we append vectors to the
beginning
of bases instead of the end):

•

Orientations of codimension-1 subspaces.
Given a codimension-1 subspace
W
of an oriented vector space
V
, a transverse vector
v
to
W
induces an orientation on
W
; an ordered basis
B
for
W
is positively oriented iff appending
v
to the start of
B
yields a positively oriented basis for
V
.

•

Orientations of codimension-1 quotient spaces.
Given a 1-dimensional subspace
W=\mathbb{R}w
of an oriented vector space
V
, we orient
V/W
as follows. An ordered basis
B
for
V/W
is positively oriented if and only if taking
w
followed by an ordered set of coset representatives from
B
yields a positively oriented basis for
V
.

In
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
we have unit (norm
\pm 1
) vector fields
\partial T,\partial Z,\partial X_{0},...,\partial X_{n}
in the coordinate directions. We give
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
an orientation by declaring this ordered basis to be positively oriented. We also define two radial vector fields

\partial_{r}=T\partial T+Z\partial_{Z}+...+X_{n}\partial X_{n},\ \partial_{-}=Z\partial_{Z}+X_{0}\partial X_{0}+...+X_{n}\partial X_{n}.

The field
\partial_{r}
is non-zero everywhere in
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
except at
p=0
, and
\partial_{-}
is non-zero except along the
T
-axis. The field
\partial_{-}
is also space-like, pointing along
(n+2)
-planes with constant
T
. On each space-like
(n+2)
-plane
\Pi_{T_{0}}
(defined by
T=T_{0}
for
T_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
), the vector field
\partial_{T}
is normal to
\Pi_{T_{0}}
. Following the conventions above we endow
\Pi_{T_{0}}
with the induced orientation, which will be the same as coordinatising the plane as
\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
with coordinates
(Z,X_{0},...,X_{n})
.

We define two orientations, the
outward
and
inward
orientations, on
\mathscr{S}_{T_{0}}^{+}
, the celestial sphere at constant
T_{0}
. This is a codimension 1 submanifold of
\Pi_{T_{0}}
with outwards normal vector field
\partial_{-}
. The outward orientation is the one induced by
\partial_{-}
, and the inward orientation is induced by
-\partial_{-}
. Any basis of a tangent space to
\mathscr{S}^{+}
is, accordingly, either oriented inward or outward. Explicitly, at
p\in\mathscr{S}_{T_{0}}^{+}
, an ordered basis
(B_{1},...,B_{n+1})
of
T_{p}\mathscr{S}_{T_{0}}^{+}
has outward orientation if and only if
(\partial_{-},B_{1},...,B_{n+1})
is a positively oriented basis of
\Pi_{T_{0}}
, if and only if
(\partial_{T},\partial_{-},B_{1},...,B_{n+1})
is a positively oriented basis of
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
.

But there is another way to define an orientation on the tangent space.
L^{+}
obtains an orientation as a codimension-1 submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
, with the transverse vector field
\partial_{-}
pointing out of the solid cone of time-like vectors. At any
p\in L^{+}
, the
(n+2)
-plane
T_{p}L^{+}=p^{\perp}
also obtains an orientation. The quotient
p^{\perp}/p\mathbb{R}=T_{\ell}\mathscr{S}^{+}
then obtains an orientation as a codimension-1 quotient. With this orientation, an ordered basis
(\underline{B}_{1},...,\underline{B}_{n+1})
of
p^{\perp}/p\mathbb{R}
is positively oriented if and only if
(\partial_{r},B_{1},...,B_{n+1})
forms a positively oriented basis of
p^{\perp}
(where each
B_{m}
is a representative of
\underline{B}_{m}
), if and only if
(\partial_{-},\partial_{r},B_{1},...,B_{n+1})
forms a positively oriented basis of
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
. This corresponds to the inward orientation defined above, because the swap
(\partial_{T},\partial_{-})\to(\partial_{-},\partial_{r})
is orientation reversing.

2.2.
The Basepoint Map

In
[
23
]
, the relationship between Hermitian matrices and
\mathbb{R}^{1,3}
is exploited to map the complex spinors into Minkowski space. Given a complex spinor
(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
,

\begin{pmatrix}\xi\\
\eta\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\overline{\xi}&\overline{\eta}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}|\xi|^{2}&\xi\overline{\eta}\\
\eta\overline{\xi}&|\eta|^{2}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}T+Z&X+Yi\\
X-Yi&T-Z\end{pmatrix}.

The determinant of the outer product is zero, and the right-hand side has determinant proportional to the Minkowski metric, so this maps complex spinors to the light-cone
L
in
\mathbb{R}^{1,3}
. Because the trace satisfies
|\xi|^{2}+|\eta|^{2}=T>0
, it in fact maps complex spinors to
L^{+}
.

We have the Clifford conjugation on Lipschitz spinors, so generalisation of this map is straightforward. Given
\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, we think of mapping it into
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
by thinking of
\kappa
as a column vector and taking an outer product with its conjugate transpose to get a paravector Hermitian matrix. We recall the conjugate transpose
\overline{\kappa}^{T}
of a column spinor
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)^{T}
is notated
\kappa^{\dagger}
.

Definition 2.4
.

The map
\phi_{1}:S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
is given by

\phi_{1}(\kappa)=\kappa\kappa^{\dagger}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi\\
\eta\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\overline{\xi}&\overline{\eta}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}T+Z&X_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}X_{j}i_{j}\\
X_{0}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}X_{j}i_{j}&T-Z\end{pmatrix}.

It is evident that
\text{Im}(\phi_{1})\subseteq L^{+}
(where Im
(\cdot)
indicates the image, not the imaginary component) as the pseudo-determinant of the outer product is zero and the trace is positive. In fact, the image is precisely the positive light-cone.

Lemma 2.5
.

The image of
\phi_{1}
is the positive light-cone
L^{+}
.

Proof.

\text{Im}(\phi_{1})\subseteq L^{+}
as pdet
(\phi_{1}(\kappa))=0
and
|\xi|^{2}+|\eta|^{2}=T>0
. To show surjectivity, consider an arbitrary point
p\in L^{+}
:

p=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}T+Z&\mathbf{X}\\
\overline{\mathbf{X}}&T-Z\end{pmatrix}.

By definition, points of
L^{+}
satisfy
T^{2}-Z^{2}-|\mathbf{X}|^{2}=0,\ T>0
. Taken together, these conditions imply
T\geq Z
; it is then clear that the spinor

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\mathbf{X}}{\sqrt{T-Z}}\\
\sqrt{T-Z}\end{pmatrix}

is defined and maps to
p
.

\hfill\square

It is helpful to express the metric on
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
using these
\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2}
presentations of points. We may also occasionally have cause to treat the space as
\mathbb{R}^{n+3}
and consider the space equipped with the Euclidean metric
dT^{2}+dZ^{2}+...+dX_{n}^{2}
.

Proposition 2.6
.

Given two points
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2}
,

P=\begin{pmatrix}P_{11}&P_{\mathbf{X}}\\
\overline{P_{\mathbf{X}}}&P_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}T_{1}+Z_{1}&\mathbf{X}_{1}\\
\overline{\mathbf{X}_{1}}&T_{1}-Z_{1}\end{pmatrix},\ Q=\begin{pmatrix}Q_{11}&Q_{\mathbf{X}}\\
\overline{Q_{\mathbf{X}}}&Q_{22}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}T_{2}+Z_{2}&\mathbf{X}_{2}\\
\overline{\mathbf{X}_{2}}&T_{2}-Z_{2}\end{pmatrix},

their generalised Minkowski inner product is given by

(2.7)

(P|Q)_{1,n+2}=2(P_{11}Q_{22}+P_{22}Q_{11}-P_{\mathbf{X}}\overline{Q_{\mathbf{X}}}-Q_{\mathbf{X}}\overline{P_{\mathbf{X}}}).

Their Euclidean inner product is

(2.8)

(P|Q)_{n+3}=2(P_{11}Q_{11}+Q_{22}P_{22}+P_{\mathbf{X}}\overline{Q_{\mathbf{X}}}+Q_{\mathbf{X}}\overline{P_{\mathbf{X}}}).

Proof.
The dot product (see Definition
1.7
) on paravectors
\mathbf{X}_{1}
and
\mathbf{X}_{2}
is given by

\frac{\mathbf{X}_{1}\overline{\mathbf{X}_{2}}+\mathbf{X}_{2}\overline{\mathbf{X}_{1}}}{2}=2(P_{\mathbf{X}}\overline{Q_{\mathbf{X}}}+Q_{\mathbf{X}}\overline{P_{\mathbf{X}}}).

We can wrangle the
T
and
Z
terms from the diagonal, noting they’re real and therefore commute.

\displaystyle(T_{1}T_{2}-Z_{1}Z_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{(T_{1}+Z_{1})(T_{2}-Z_{2})+(T_{2}+Z_{2})(T_{1}-Z_{1})}{2}

\displaystyle=2(P_{11}Q_{22}+P_{22}Q_{11}).

In total, we find the Minkowski metric is given by

(P,Q)_{1,n+2}=2(P_{11}Q_{22}+P_{22}Q_{11}-P_{\mathbf{X}}\overline{Q_{\mathbf{X}}}-Q_{\mathbf{X}}\overline{P_{\mathbf{X}}}).

To instead get the Euclidean inner product we compute

\displaystyle(T_{1}T_{2}+Z_{1}Z_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{(T_{1}+Z_{1})(T_{2}+Z_{2})+(T_{1}-Z_{1})(T_{2}-Z_{2})}{2}

\displaystyle=2(P_{11}Q_{11}+P_{22}Q_{22}),

which gives us

(P|Q)_{n+3}=2(P_{11}Q_{11}+P_{22}Q_{22}+P_{\mathbf{X}}\overline{Q_{\mathbf{X}}}+Q_{\mathbf{X}}\overline{P_{\mathbf{X}}}).

2.3.
Tangent Data

The kernel of
D\phi_{1}
is large; in fact
\phi_{1}
is a fibre bundle map over
L^{+}
with fibres homeomorphic to Spin
(n+1)
. We find an indication of this when examining the preimages
\phi_{1}^{-1}
of points in the light-cone, as they resemble the diagonal map inclusion of Spin
(n+1)
into
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
; to be precise, they are an inclusion
s\to(s,\mathbf{X}s)
or
s\to(\mathbf{Y}s,s)
of Spin
(n+1)
into
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
for some elements
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Given a point
p\in L^{+}
and a single preimage point
\kappa\in\phi^{-1}(p)
,

i)

the entire preimage is given by
\kappa s,\ s\in\text{Spin}(n+1)
, where the spin group Spin
(n+1)
is realised as
\{s\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}\ |\ N(s)=1\}
.

ii)

Alternatively, the preimage
\phi_{1}^{-1}(p)
for a point

p=\begin{pmatrix}p_{11}&p_{\mathbf{X}}\\
\overline{p_{\mathbf{X}}}&p_{22}\end{pmatrix}\in L^{+}

is given when
p_{11}>0
by
(s,p_{11}^{-1}\overline{p_{\mathbf{X}}}s)
for
\{s\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}\ |\ N(s)=p_{11}\}
.

When
p_{22}>0
, it can instead be given by
(p_{22}^{-1}p_{\mathbf{X}}s,s)
for
\{s\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}\ |\ N(s)=p_{22}\}
.

Proof.
Consider a point
p
on the light-cone. The preimage will be the set of
(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
which satisfy

\begin{pmatrix}|\xi|^{2}&\xi\overline{\eta}\\
\eta\overline{\xi}&|\eta|^{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}p_{11}&p_{\mathbf{X}}\\
\overline{p_{\mathbf{X}}}&p_{22}\end{pmatrix}.

If
\eta\neq 0
, then
\xi=\mathbf{V}\eta
for some
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and we can decompose the above matrix:

p=|\eta|^{2}\begin{pmatrix}|\mathbf{V}|^{2}&\mathbf{V}\\
\overline{\mathbf{V}}&1\end{pmatrix}.

Our choice of
\mathbf{V}
and
|\eta|^{2}
is fixed by the equalities
|\eta|^{2}=p_{22}
and
\mathbf{V}=p_{\mathbf{X}}/|\eta|^{2}
. This gives us freedom to choose any
\eta
satisfying

(2.10)

\{\eta\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}\ |\ N(\eta)=p_{22}\}.

Taking
\xi\neq 0
instead, by the analogous argument we get a preimage with a choice of
\{\xi\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}\ |\ N(\xi)=p_{11}\}
, which demonstrates ii).

Now consider i). From Corollary
1.18
, we know the group
\text{Spin}(n+1)
can be defined as
\{s\in\$\Gamma_{n+1,0}\ |\ N(s)=1\}
. Comparing this with (
2.10
), we notice that the preimage
\phi^{-1}(p)
is almost exactly Spin
(n+1)
, except rescaled so
N(\eta)=p_{22}
. If we take an element satisfying (
2.10
) and multiply by
s\in\text{Spin}(n+1)
, then
\eta s\overline{s}\,\overline{\eta}=\eta\overline{\eta}
and we still satisfy (
2.10
). Every element that satisfies (
2.10
) can be generated this way, for if
s\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
and
N(s)=p_{22}=N(\eta)
then
N(s^{-1}\eta)=1
and
s^{-1}\eta\in\text{Spin}(n+1)
. And, of course,
ss^{-1}\eta=\eta
. The analogous argument holds if we take
\xi\neq 0
instead, which completes the proof of i).
\hfill\square

This shows that
\phi_{1}
loses a lot of information. To counter this, we can add data via derivatives. Following Section 4.4 of
[
21
]
, the derivative at a point
\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
in the direction
v
can be found as follows:

\phi_{1}(\kappa+tv)=(\kappa+tv)(\kappa+tv)^{\dagger}=\kappa\kappa^{\dagger}+\left(\kappa v^{\dagger}+v\kappa^{\dagger}\right)t+vv^{\dagger}t^{2},\ t\in\mathbb{R}.

Taking the derivative and evaluating at
t=0
, we find

(2.11)

(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(v)=\kappa v^{\dagger}+v\kappa^{\dagger}.

This derivative respects the orthogonal decomposition of the spinor tangent space (see Theorem
1.64
); in particular, each summand has a nice geometric interpretation.

Theorem 2.12
.

Let
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
and
\phi_{1}(\kappa)=p=(T,Z;\mathbf{X})_{\mathbb{R}}
.

i)

D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}
maps
\kappa\mathbb{R}
isomorphically onto
p\mathbb{R}
,

ii)

D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}
maps
\check{\kappa}(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
isomorphically onto
T_{p}\mathscr{S}_{T}^{+}
,

iii)

The kernel of
D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}
is
\kappa(\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\oplus\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{R}^{0,n})
.

Proof.
We compute i) directly. For
a\in\mathbb{R}
:

(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(a\kappa)=2a\kappa\kappa^{\dagger}=2a\phi_{1}(\kappa)=2ap.

For ii), Let
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and compute

(2.13)

(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V})=\begin{pmatrix}\xi\overline{\mathbf{V}}\eta^{*}+\eta^{\prime}\mathbf{V}\overline{\xi}&-\xi\overline{\mathbf{V}}\xi^{*}+\eta^{\prime}\mathbf{V}\overline{\eta}\\
\eta\overline{\mathbf{V}}\eta^{*}-\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{V}\overline{\xi}&-\eta\overline{\mathbf{V}}\xi^{*}-\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{V}\overline{\eta}\end{pmatrix}.

This derivative lives in the tangent space to
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
(which we’ve identified with
\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2}
), which is canonically isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
itself. Thus the diagonal entries are real, and invariant under conjugation. We compute the
T
component, applying some conjugations to cancel terms:

(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V})_{T}=\left(\xi\overline{\mathbf{V}}\eta^{*}+\eta^{\prime}\mathbf{V}\overline{\xi}-(\eta\overline{\mathbf{V}}\xi^{*}+\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{V}\overline{\eta})^{*}\right)=0.

As it lives in the tangent space to
L^{+}
at
p
, we know
(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V})\in p^{\perp}
. Having no change in the
T
coordinate means it stays tangent to the fixed
T
-slice
\mathscr{S}_{T}^{+}
that models the celestial sphere. We conclude

(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V})\in T_{p}\mathscr{S}_{T}^{+}.

To show surjectivity, we appeal to the rank-nullity theorem and show that
D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}
has trivial kernel on
\check{\kappa}(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
. As the components of
\check{\kappa}
and
\mathbf{V}
are elements of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}^{\triangleright}
they contain no zero divisors, and as both
\check{\kappa}
and
\mathbf{V}
are non-zero
\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}\neq 0
. Suppose that there exists some
\mathbf{V}\neq 0
such that
D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V})=0
. Then, by (
2.11
),

(2.14)

\kappa(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V})^{\dagger}=-\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}\kappa^{\dagger}.

By Lemma
1.55
, there exists a row vector
\tau
such that precisely one of
\tau\kappa
,
\tau\check{\kappa}
is zero. This implies precisely one of
\tau\kappa(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V})^{\dagger}
and
-\tau\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}\kappa^{\dagger}
is zero, but these are the same matrix by (
2.14
) and we have a contradiction.

Finally, we compute iii). For
B\in\mathcal{B}^{n}
a bi-paravector with no real component:

(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\kappa B)=\kappa\overline{B}\kappa^{\dagger}+\kappa B\kappa^{\dagger}=\kappa(B+\overline{B})\kappa^{\dagger}=0.

The final equality is because
B+\overline{B}=2\text{Re}(B)
for bi-paravectors. As the other dimensions of
T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
are accounted for, this must comprise the entire kernel.
\hfill\square

In
[
23
]
, a function
Z
is chosen to coordinatise parts of the tangent space in a convenient way at
\kappa
; this is precisely the role of our complementary map
\check{\cdot}
, the analogue of a complex structure. In general there are
n
tangent directions to capture, directions spanned by the basis elements
\check{\kappa}i_{j},\ 1\leq j\leq n
. This (along with
\check{\kappa}
itself) forms an orthogonal basis for the
\check{\kappa}(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
summand of the tangent space.

We can also capture
\check{\cdot}
via a ‘complex structure’ matrix.

Definition 2.15
.

J=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\\
\end{bmatrix}.

It satisfies
J\kappa^{\prime}=\check{\kappa}
.

\lrcorner

2.4.
Decorated Ideal Points

In Sections
2.4
and
2.5
we present two ways of capturing the degrees of freedom within the kernel of
D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}
. The first, following
[
21
]
, are
decorated ideal points
; essentially, these involve identifying a basis for
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
in the tangent spaces to
\mathscr{S}^{+}
. We do this using the various derivatives
(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{j})
to make an identification of
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with the tangent space. In particular, this construction clearly shows conformality of the correspondence. It is a generalisation of the ‘tangent vector to
\mathscr{S}^{+}
’ construction of Penrose and Rindler
[
27
]
.

Definition 2.16
.

Let
\ell\in\mathscr{S}^{+}
be a point in the sphere at infinity (an ‘ideal point’). A
decoration
on
\ell
is a conformal orientation-preserving
\mathbb{R}
-linear isomorphism

\psi:\ \$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\frac{\ell^{\perp}}{\ell}

with respect to the outward orientation on
\ell^{\perp}/\ell
, conformal with respect to the norm
N
on
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and the norm induced on
\ell^{\perp}/\ell
by the generalised Minkowski metric.
The set of all decorated ideal points
(\ell,\psi)
is denoted
\mathscr{S}^{+D}
.

\lrcorner

Informally, a decoration is a choice of orthonormal basis (plus a global scaling factor) in the tangent space
T_{\ell}\mathscr{S}^{+}
that identifies it with
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. The family of oriented orthonormal bases can be traversed by taking a starting basis and allowing all rotations, so the space of decorations at
\ell\in\mathscr{S}^{+}
is really just
SO(n+1)\times\mathbb{R}
.

2.4.1.
The Action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on
TS\$\Gamma_{n+1}

Elements
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
act real linearly on
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, and also act real linearly on its tangent spaces, sending
T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to T_{A\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
by left-multiplication. We recall from Theorem
1.64
that the tangent space
T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
has a decomposition
\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}\oplus\check{\kappa}\left(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right)
,
where
\mathcal{B}^{n}
are the bi-paravectors. Acting by
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
, the tangent space is sent to

A.T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}=A\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}\oplus A\check{\kappa}\left(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right).

We can also apply this decomposition to the target tangent space:

T_{A\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}=A\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}\oplus\widecheck{(A\kappa)}\left(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right).

There is an immediate problem; the first summand is preserved under the action, but the second is not. Not all is lost, however, as the second summand of both
A.T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
and
T_{A\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
is isomorphic to
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, just identified differently.

To focus on the second summand, we can quotient out the first (as the first is preserved by the action it can safely be ignored) to get an action by
A
as an
\mathbb{R}
-linear map
\tfrac{T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}}{\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}}\to\tfrac{T_{A\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}}{A\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}}
. These quotients are isomorphic to the vector spaces
\check{\kappa}\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
\widecheck{(A\kappa)}\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
respectively. The points of
T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}/\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}
are affine
\left(\tfrac{n^{2}+n}{2}+1\right)
-planes (which we shorthand as
higher affine planes
) in
T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
of the form
\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}+\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}=s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)+\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}
over all
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
; recall that
s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)
are our sections of the tangent bundle, as defined in Section
1.8.1
. We can characterise what points lie in these higher affine planes using the bracket operation, as shown in Lemma
1.74
. We can then describe the action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on the quotient.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
\kappa_{0}\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
,
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
, and
\kappa_{1}=A\kappa_{0}
. For each
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, the action of
A
restricts to a map

|\kappa_{0}|^{-2}s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa_{0})+\kappa_{0}\mathcal{B}^{n}\to|\kappa_{1}|^{-2}s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa_{1})+\kappa_{1}\mathcal{B}^{n},

which are higher affine planes in
T_{\kappa_{0}}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
and
T_{\kappa_{1}}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, respectively.

Proof.
By Lemma
1.74
, the first set is the higher affine plane of
w\in T_{\kappa_{0}}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
such that
\{\kappa_{0},w\}=-\mathbf{V}
, while the second is the higher affine plane of
m\in T_{\kappa_{1}}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
such that
\{\kappa_{1},m\}=-\mathbf{V}
. By Lemma
1.51
, for any
w\in T_{\kappa_{0}}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
we have
\{\kappa_{0},w\}=\{A\kappa_{0},Aw\}
, which implies
\{\kappa_{0},w\}=\{\kappa_{1},Aw\}=-\mathbf{V}
. So
Aw\in T_{\kappa_{1}}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, and as the action is invertible this map is bijective.
\hfill\square

Because
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
maps higher affine planes to higher affine planes, it induces a well-defined map on the quotient. This map is conformal under the inner product
(\cdot|\cdot)
of Definition
1.75
.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
\kappa_{0}\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
,
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
, and
\kappa_{1}=A\kappa_{0}
. The map

\frac{T_{\kappa_{0}}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}}{\kappa_{0}\mathcal{B}^{n}}\to\frac{T_{\kappa_{1}}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}}{\kappa_{1}\mathcal{B}^{n}}

induced by the action of
A
is an orientation-preserving conformal
\mathbb{R}
-linear isomorphism under
(\cdot|\cdot)
, the metric of Definition
1.75
.

Proof.
Let
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. By Lemma
2.17
,
A
sends the plane with representative
\check{\kappa}_{0}\mathbf{V}=s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa_{0})
to the plane represented by
\check{\kappa}_{1}\mathbf{V}|\kappa_{0}|^{2}|\kappa_{1}|^{-2}=s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa_{1})|\kappa_{0}|^{2}|\kappa_{1}|^{-2}
. We thus have

(A\check{\kappa}_{0}\mathbf{V}+\kappa_{1}\mathcal{B}^{n}|A\check{\kappa}_{0}\mathbf{W}+\kappa_{1}\mathcal{B}^{n})=\left(\check{\kappa}_{1}|\kappa_{0}|^{2}|\kappa_{1}|^{-2}\mathbf{V}\ \middle|\ \check{\kappa}_{1}|\kappa_{0}|^{2}|\kappa_{1}|^{-2}\mathbf{W}\right)=\frac{|\kappa_{0}|^{4}}{|\kappa_{1}|^{4}}(\check{\kappa}_{1}\mathbf{V}|\check{\kappa}_{1}\mathbf{W}).

We note that
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
is connected and contains the identity matrix, which it is easy to show acts trivially and is thus orientation-preserving. We conclude the action of the connected component
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
is also orientation-preserving, and
A
is an orientation-preserving conformal map.

Linearity then follows because the action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
is by matrices, and it is an isomorphism because we can act by
A^{-1}
(which exists as
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
is a group) to reverse the action of
A
.
\hfill\square

2.4.2.
Conformality of
D\phi_{1}

From Theorem
2.12
we have that, at
\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, the derivative
D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}
restricts to an isomorphism
\check{\kappa}(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1})\to T_{\phi_{1}(\kappa)}\mathscr{S}_{T}^{+}
. We also have a map
s(\cdot,\kappa):\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\check{\kappa}(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
from our map of sections of
TS\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. We get a composition of linear isomorphisms

\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\overset{s(\cdot,\kappa)}{\longrightarrow}\check{\kappa}(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1})\overset{D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}}{\longrightarrow}T_{\phi_{1}(\kappa)}\mathscr{S}_{T}^{+}.

The first and second spaces have positive-definite inner products, and Lemma
1.73
shows
s(\cdot,\kappa)
is conformal. The third space
T_{\phi_{1}(\kappa)}\mathscr{S}_{T}^{+}
is a space-like subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
, and inherits a negative-definite metric by restriction of the Minkowski metric. We show that the map
D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}:\check{\kappa}(\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1})\to T_{\phi_{1}(\kappa)}\mathscr{S}_{T}^{+}
in the sequence above is conformal.

Lemma 2.19
.

For
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
we have
\textnormal{pdet}(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}))=-|\mathbf{V}|^{2}|\kappa|^{4}
.

Proof.
From (
2.13
) we have the matrix form of
D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V})
:

D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}\left(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}\xi\overline{\mathbf{V}}\eta^{*}+\eta^{\prime}\mathbf{V}\overline{\xi}&-\xi\overline{\mathbf{V}}\xi^{*}+\eta^{\prime}\mathbf{V}\overline{\eta}\\
\eta\overline{\mathbf{V}}\eta^{*}-\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{V}\overline{\xi}&-\eta\overline{\mathbf{V}}\xi^{*}-\xi^{\prime}\mathbf{V}\overline{\eta}\end{pmatrix}.

The pseudo-determinant can then be computed, and

\textnormal{pdet}(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\check{\kappa}v))=-|\mathbf{V}|^{2}|\kappa|^{4}

as claimed.
\hfill\square

Proposition 2.20
.

For any
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
,

(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V})|D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{W}))_{1,n+2}=-4|\kappa|^{4}\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{W}=-4|\kappa|^{2}(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V}|\check{\kappa}\mathbf{W}),

applying the generalised Minkowski inner product, the dot product (
1.8
) on
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
, and the inner product
(\cdot|\cdot)
on
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
(Definition
1.37
) respectively.

Proof.
Recall
25
25

25

See the discussion of paravector Hermitian matrices below Definition
2.2
.
that if a matrix
S_{p}\in\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2}
corresponds to a point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
then
4\text{pdet}(S_{p})=|p|^{2}
. If we let
S_{D}
be the paravector Hermitian matrix corresponding to
D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V})
, Lemma
2.19
then tells us

(2.21)

|S_{D}|^{2}=4\text{pdet}\left(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V})\right)=-4|\mathbf{V}|^{2}|\kappa|^{4}.

We can polarise on both norms (that is, the Minkowski norm and the norm
N
on
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
) to find a relationship between the inner products. The norm on the left-hand side of (
2.21
) polarises as

4(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{V})|D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\check{\kappa}\mathbf{W}))_{1,n+2}=|D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\check{\kappa}(\mathbf{V}+\mathbf{W}))|^{2}-|D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\check{\kappa}(\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{W}))|^{2},

while the norm on paravectors polarises as
4\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{W}=|\mathbf{V}+\mathbf{W}|^{2}-|\mathbf{V}-\mathbf{W}|^{2}
. Apply these to (
2.21
) for the first equality. The second follows from Lemma
1.40
iv).
\hfill\square

With tools in hand, we now show the derivative map on the quotient is conformal.

Lemma 2.22
.

The quotient map

D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}:\frac{T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}}{\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}}\to\frac{\ell^{\perp}}{\ell}

from sections
s(\cdot,\kappa)
to
T_{p}\mathscr{S}_{T}^{+}
is conformal with scaling factor
-4|\kappa|^{2}
. It is orientation-preserving with respect to the outward orientation on
\mathscr{S}^{+}
.

Proof.
In Section
1.8.1
,
every element of the quotient
T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}/\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}
is seen to have a unique representative that is a section
s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)
for
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. For
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,

(s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)+\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}|s_{\mathbf{W}}(\kappa)+\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n})=(s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)|s_{\mathbf{W}}(\kappa))=|\kappa|^{2}\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{W},

where the left inner product is on
\tfrac{T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}}{\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}}
, the central is the inner product on
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{2}
, and the rightmost is the dot product on
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
. Conformality now follows from Proposition
2.20
:

(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa))|D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(s_{\mathbf{W}}(\kappa)))_{1,n+2}=-4|\kappa|^{2}(s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)|s_{\mathbf{W}}(\kappa))=-4|\kappa|^{2}(s_{\mathbf{V}}(\kappa)+\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}|s_{\mathbf{W}}(\kappa)+\kappa\mathcal{B}^{n}).

To show the map is orientation-preserving, note that at each
\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
the map
D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}
sends a standard oriented basis
s_{1}(\kappa),s_{i_{1}}(\kappa),...,s_{i_{n}}(\kappa)
to a basis of
T_{p}\mathscr{S}_{T}^{+}
. The space
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
is connected (except for the case of
n=0
, but there the decoration becomes trivial), so we can check the orientation for a single
\kappa
and continuously vary to argue the map is orientation-preserving (or reversing) equally over all of
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
.

The spinor
\kappa_{0}=(1,0)
with tangent space
T_{\kappa_{0}}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}=\kappa_{0}\mathcal{B}^{n}\oplus\check{\kappa}_{0}\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is sent to
p_{0}=(1,1;0,...,0)_{\mathbb{R}}
, with tangent space
T_{p_{0}}L^{+}=p_{0}^{\perp}
. The tangent space
p_{0}^{\perp}
is spanned by
p_{0}
and the basis
\partial X_{0},...,\partial X_{n}
of
T_{p_{0}/{\sim}}\mathscr{S}^{+}
. We find the derivative maps the tangent vectors as follows:

(2.23)

D_{\kappa_{0}}(s_{1}(\kappa_{0}))=2\partial_{X_{0}},\ D_{\kappa_{0}}(s_{i_{j}}(\kappa_{0}))=2\partial_{X_{j}},

and so the oriented basis
s_{1}(\kappa_{0}),s_{i_{1}}(\kappa_{1}),...,s_{i_{n}}(\kappa_{n})
is mapped to the basis
2\partial X_{0},...,2\partial X_{n}
of
T_{p_{0}}\mathscr{S}^{+}
, which is outward oriented. We conclude the map is orientation-preserving.
\hfill\square

2.5.
Multiflags

The missing degrees of freedom lost by
\phi_{1}
can also be recovered via a flag structure, generalising the ‘null flag’ picture of Penrose and Rindler
[
27
]
in the complex spinor case.

Definition 2.24

(Flag)
.

Given a vector space
V^{n}
, a flag of
V^{n}
is a strictly ascending chain of vector subspaces
V_{0}\subset V_{1}\subset...\subset V_{r}
. Because the inclusions are strict,

0\leq\text{dim}(V_{0})<\text{dim}(V_{1})<...\text{dim}(V_{r})\leq n,

where dim
(V_{j})
is the dimension of
V_{j}
. A flag’s
signature
is the sequence
(\text{dim}(V_{0}),...,\text{dim}(V_{r}))
.

An
oriented flag
is a flag with an orientation on each quotient space
V_{j+1}/V_{j}
, where
V_{j}\subset V_{j+1}
are neighbouring subspaces in the flag.

\lrcorner

The
null flag
is a particular type of flag structure living on the light-cone in Minkowski space, so named because the light-cone is also the space of ‘null directions’ in Minkowski, the vectors of length zero.

Definition 2.25

(Null Flag)
.

Consider the light-cone
L^{+}\subset\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
. Given a point
p\in L^{+}
and a vector
v\in T_{p}L^{+}
linearly independent of
p
, let
F_{p,v}
be the span of
\mathbb{R}p
and
v
and denote the flag
0\subset\mathbb{R}p\subset F_{p,v}
by
[[p,v]]
. We call
p
the
base-point
and
F_{p,v}
the
flag plane
, and say the flag is
based
at
p
. The
flagpole

p\mathbb{R}
is chosen to be future-oriented, and
F_{p,v}/p\mathbb{R}
is oriented by the equivalence class of the vector
v
. Such a flag is called a
null flag
.

\lrcorner

Following
[
23
]
, all flags are taken to be oriented and of signature (1,2). That is, all flags are of the form
\{0\}=V_{0}\subset V_{1}\subset V_{2}
, where dim
V_{1}=1
, dim
V_{2}=2
, and
V_{1}/V_{0}=V_{1}
and
V_{2}/V_{1}
are endowed with orientations.

The fibres of
\phi_{1}
are (up to scaling) the spin group
Spin(n+1)
, and it is this group we seek to reconstruct with flags. Given a particular Lipschitz spinor
\kappa
, it should map to a point of the light-cone and some collection of flags with an orthogonality condition upon them, to recover something like a spin group.

But orthogonal in what sense? Where should our flags live? Our copy of
Spin(n+1)
in the fibres should act on an
(n+1)
-dimensional vector space, so we should look for a ‘natural’ choice of such a space within
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
. Similar to the decorated ideal points, we choose to act on
T_{\ell}\mathscr{S}^{+}
, where
\ell=\phi_{1}(\kappa)\mathbb{R}
. Our flags will all share a flagpole (given by the span of
\phi_{1}(\kappa)
), and the flag planes will be determined by various derivatives in the tangent space to
L^{+}
.

The sphere at infinity
\mathscr{S}^{+}
can be thought of as the set of directions available along the light-cone; each ray of the light-cone becomes a point in the sphere. Thus, a flag
[[p,v]]
would have its flagpole mapped to the equivalence class of
p
in
\mathscr{S}^{+}
. We can also imagine mapping the tangent space of
L^{+}
at
p
to the tangent space of
\mathscr{S}^{+}
by quotienting out
\mathbb{R}p
, the ray pointing along
p
. This would send the flag
[[p,v]]
to the equivalence class of
p\in\mathscr{S}^{+}
with tangent vector
v+\mathbb{R}p
.

However,
p
is orthogonal to itself under the Minkowski metric. By the bilinearity of the metric, then, orthogonality of these quotients in the tangent space
T_{p}\mathscr{S}^{+}
is equivalent to orthogonality of the flag plane vectors
v_{j}
. That is, if we take two vectors
v_{1},v_{2}\in T_{p}L^{+}
and two arbitrary real numbers
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, to have orthogonality we require

(v_{1}+r_{1}p|v_{2}+r_{2}p)_{1,n+2}=(v_{1}|v_{2})_{1,n+2}+r_{2}(p|v_{2})_{1,n+2}+r_{1}(v_{1}|p)_{1,n+2}+r_{1}r_{2}(p|p)_{1,n+2}=0.

Then, because
v_{1},v_{2},p\in p^{\perp}
, this implies

(v_{1}+r_{1}p|v_{2}+r_{2}p)_{1,n+2}=(v_{1}|v_{2})_{1,n+2}=0.

It follows that, rather than dealing with quotients, we can consider the orthogonality of flags simply by comparing the tangent vectors defining the flag plane.

Definition 2.26

(Orthogonality of Flags)
.

Consider two flags
F_{1}=[[p,v_{1}]]
and
F_{2}=[[p,v_{2}]]
that share the same flagpole vector. These flags are orthogonal if
(v_{1}|v_{2})_{1,n+2}=0
.

\lrcorner

All of this suggests the correct ‘space of flags’ should be those with orthogonal flag planes.

Definition 2.27
.

The
null multiflag
(or simply
multiflag
)
[[p;v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{n}]]
is the ordered set (or tuple) of null flags
([[p,v_{1}]],\ [[p,v_{2}]],...,[[p,v_{n}]])
, each based at
p
and bearing a ‘flag plane vector’
v_{j}\in T_{p}L^{+}
. The flag plane vectors
v_{j}
must be mutually orthogonal, and the collection
\{v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{n},p\}
must form a linearly independent set. The individual constituent flags
[[p,v_{j}]]
are called
subflags
.

\lrcorner

Lemma 2.28
.

The subflags of
[[\phi_{1}(\kappa);(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{1}),(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{2}),...,(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{n})]]
are orthogonal under the Minkowski metric; that is, they do indeed collect to form a proper multiflag.

Proof.
The vectors
i_{j}
are orthogonal, and remain orthogonal under the conformal map
D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}
(see Lemma
2.22
). This implies the flags themselves are orthogonal, by Definition
2.26
.
\hfill\square

As an appropriate target space for our map from
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, we define a bundle of multiflags over the light-cone.

Definition 2.29
.

The space of null multiflags
\mathcal{MF}^{n}_{p}
at a point
p\in L^{+}
consists of all
[[p;v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{n}]]
based at
p
with flag plane vectors
v_{j}
that are mutually orthogonal under
(\cdot|\cdot)_{1,n+2}
, and form a positively oriented frame. The bundle of null multiflags
\mathcal{MF}^{n}
is then the fibre bundle with fibre
\mathcal{MF}^{n}_{p}
at each
p\in L^{+}
.

\lrcorner

The space of multiflags
\mathcal{MF}^{n}_{p}
based at
p
is diffeomorphic to the space of positively oriented orthonormal frames at
p
, which is diffeomorphic to
SO(n+1)
.
\mathcal{MF}^{n}
is then a frame bundle over
L^{+}\cong S^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 2.30
.

The map
\Phi_{1}
from non-zero spinors to multiflags is given by

\Phi_{1}:S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to\mathcal{MF}^{n},\ \Phi_{1}(\kappa)=[[\phi_{1}(\kappa);(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{1}),(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{2}),...,(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{n})]].

We refer to the individual flag
[[\phi_{1}(\kappa),(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{j})]]
as the
i_{j}
flag, or
i_{j}
subflag.

\lrcorner

Multiflags and decorated ideal points carry the same information, as the following proposition (an extension of Proposition 4.8.2 of
[
21
]
) shows. Recall that
\mathscr{S}^{+D}
is the space of decorated ideal points, pairs
(\ell,\psi)
where
\ell\in\mathscr{S}^{+}
is a point in the celestial sphere and the decoration
\psi
is a conformal orientation-preserving
\mathbb{R}
-linear isomorphism from
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\to\ell^{\perp}/\ell
(informally, an orthonormal basis up to a global scale factor for the tangent space
T_{\ell}\mathscr{S}^{+}
).

Proposition 2.31
.

There is a smooth bijective correspondence
\Psi:\ \mathcal{MF}^{n}\to\mathscr{S}^{+D}
, as follows.

i)

Given a multiflag
[[p;v_{1},...,v_{n}]]\in\mathcal{MF}^{n}
, let
p
have
T
coordinate
T_{0}
, and let
a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n}>0
be chosen such that
a_{1}v_{1},a_{2}v_{2},...,a_{n}v_{n}
have generalised Minkowski norm
-4T_{0}^{2}
. Then

\Psi([[p;v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{n}]])=(p\mathbb{R},\psi),

where
\psi
is uniquely defined by
\psi(i_{j})=a_{j}v_{j}+p\mathbb{R}
.

ii)

Given
(\ell,\psi)\in\mathscr{S}^{+D}
, let
K<0
be the conformal scale factor of
\psi
. Then

(\ell,\psi)\to[[p;\tilde{\psi}(i_{1}),...,\tilde{\psi}(i_{n})]],

where
p
is the point on
\ell
whose
T
-coordinate
T_{0}
satisfies
K=-4T_{0}^{2}
, and
\tilde{\psi}(i_{j})\in\ell^{\perp}
for
j\in\{1,...,n\}
are arbitrary lifts of
\psi(i_{j})\in\ell^{\perp}/\ell
to
\ell^{\perp}=T_{p}L^{+}
.

Proof.
Consider the multiflag
[[p;v_{1},...,v_{n}]]
, and let
\ell=p\mathbb{R}
. Flags at
p
are subsets of
T_{p}L^{+}=\ell^{\perp}
, and correspond to oriented lines in
\ell^{\perp}/\ell
. The equivalence classes of the
v_{j}
in
\ell^{\perp}/\ell
orient the lines corresponding to the
i_{j}
flags. The
v_{j}
are space-like, as are their equivalence classes in
\ell^{\perp}/\ell
.

As
T_{0}
is strictly positive and the
v_{j}
are space-like (and thus have negative Minkowski norm), by linearity there are a unique set of
a_{j}>0
such that
a_{j}v_{j}
have generalised Minkowski norm
-4T_{0}^{2}
, and therefore a unique set of
a_{j}>0
such that
a_{j}v_{j}+\ell\in\ell^{\perp}/\ell
also have norm
-4T_{0}^{2}
. Note that
(\cdot|\cdot)_{1,n+2}
descends to the quotient
\ell^{\perp}/\ell
because it is zero on
\ell
.

As
\ell^{\perp}/\ell
is oriented,
n+1
-dimensional, and negative definite under the norm descending from
(\cdot|\cdot)_{1,n+2}
, there exists a unique
v_{0}
of norm
-4T_{0}^{2}
such that
(v_{0}+\ell,a_{1}v_{1}+\ell,...,a_{n}v_{n}+\ell)
forms an oriented orthogonal basis. There is a unique orientation-preserving conformal
\psi
such that
\psi(i_{j})=a_{j}v_{j}+\ell
, and because we chose
v_{0}
with norm
-4T_{0}^{2}
, it also satisfies
\psi(1)=v_{0}
. Since
\psi
sends the generators
1,i_{1},...,i_{n}
(all elements of unit
N
-norm) to elements of norm
-4T_{0}^{2}
,
\psi
has scale factor
K=-4T_{0}^{2}
.

Conversely, from
(\ell,\psi)
we can set
p
as the point on
\ell
with
T
-coordinate
T_{0}>0
such that the scale factor
K
of
\psi
is
K=-4T_{0}^{2}
. Then
\psi(i_{j})\in\ell^{\perp}/\ell=T_{p}L^{+}/p\mathbb{R}
gives us the
i_{j}
flag plane vector. Taking arbitrary lifts
\tilde{\psi}(i_{j})\in\ell^{\perp}
we find the
i_{j}
-flag is
[[p;\tilde{\psi}(i_{j})]]
, and as
\psi
is conformal and the
i_{j}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
are orthogonal the
\psi(i_{j})
are also orthogonal, which implies the flags they define are orthogonal, and we have a multiflag as claimed.

To see that these correspondences are inverses of one another, suppose
[[p;v_{1},...,v_{n}]]
maps to
(\ell,\psi)
, so
\ell=p\mathbb{R}
,
\psi
has scaling factor
K=-4T_{0}^{2}
, and
\psi(i_{j})=a_{j}v_{j}+p\mathbb{R}
as above. Let
(\ell,\psi)
map to
[[q;\tilde{\psi}(i_{1}),...,\tilde{\psi}(i_{n})]]
. Since
q
lies on
p\mathbb{R}
and has
T
-coordinate
T^{\prime}
such that
K=-4T_{0}^{2}=-4T^{\prime 2}
and
T_{0},T^{\prime}>0
, we conclude
p=q
. Since
\psi(i_{j})=a_{j}v_{j}+p\mathbb{R}
and
a_{j}>0
, any lift
\tilde{\psi}(i_{j})\in\ell^{\perp}
yields the same flag as
v_{j}
. Thus
[[p;v_{1},...,v_{n}]]=[[q;\tilde{\psi}(i_{1}),...,\tilde{\psi}(i_{n})]]
.

Similarly, suppose
(\ell,\psi)
maps to
[[p;v_{1},...,v_{n}]]
where
\psi
has scaling factor
K
, the
T
-coordinate
T_{0}
of p satisfies
K=-4T_{0}^{2}
, and the
v_{j}\in\ell^{\perp}
generate the same flags as
\psi(i_{j})\in\ell^{\perp}/\ell
. Let
[[p;v_{1},...,v_{n}]]
map to
(\ell^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
. Then
\ell^{\prime}=p\mathbb{R}
, so
\ell^{\prime}=\ell
.
\psi^{\prime}
has scaling factor
-4T_{0}^{2}
, so sends each
i_{j}
to an element of
\ell^{\perp}/\ell
of norm
-4T_{0}^{2}
, generating the flags
[[p,v_{1}]]
,…,
[[p,v_{n}]]
, just like
\psi
. Thus
\psi=\psi^{\prime}
and so
(\ell,\psi)=(\ell^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
. The correspondences are inverses of one another, and clearly smooth.
\hfill\square

2.6.
The Actions of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})

To relate spinors and geometric objects in a useful way, our correspondences should be equivariant under the action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
. To have equivariance, though, we need an
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
action on each of our spaces. We have an action by Möbius transformations on the paravectors
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
(
1.27
), and on
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
we have an action by matrix multiplication that is equivalent to Möbius transformations under projectivisation (see Lemma
1.33
).

Definition 2.32
.

For a paravector Hermitian matrix
S_{p}\in\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2}
representing
p\in\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
, the action of
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
is defined to be
A.S_{p}=AS_{p}A^{\dagger}
.

\lrcorner

This is generalising the standard conjugation action of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
on
\mathbb{R}^{1,3}
.

Lemma 2.33
.

Let
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
, and
S_{p}\in\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2}
. The action
A.S_{p}=AS_{p}A^{\dagger}
of
A
on
S_{p}
is a map
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}\to\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
.

Proof.
It is easy to show this action preserves the light-cone; by Lemma
2.5
every point in
L^{+}
is the image
\phi_{1}(\kappa)
of some
\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, and

A.\phi_{1}(\kappa)=A\phi_{1}(\kappa)A^{\dagger}=(A\kappa)(\kappa^{\dagger}A^{\dagger})=\phi_{1}(A\kappa).

We have already seen that
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
has a well-defined action on
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, so
A\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
and
\phi_{1}(A\kappa)\in L^{+}
.

We can then choose a basis
B
for
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
from
L^{+}
, because the span of
L^{+}
is
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
. For example,

\displaystyle B_{+}

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(1,0)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\ B_{-}=\phi_{1}(0,1)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle B_{0}

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(1,1)=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},\ B_{j}=\phi_{1}(i_{j},1)=\begin{pmatrix}1&i_{j}\\
-i_{j}&1\end{pmatrix},

for
1\leq j\leq n
. We can recover the standard basis for
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
as follows:

\displaystyle(1,0;0)_{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(B_{+}+B_{-}),\ (0,1;0)_{\mathbb{R}}=\tfrac{1}{2}(B_{+}-B_{-}),

\displaystyle(0,0;1)_{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(B_{0}-B_{+}-B_{-}),\ (0,0;i_{j})_{\mathbb{R}}=\tfrac{1}{2}(B_{j}-B_{+}-B_{-}).

As the action is a combination of left and right matrix multiplication, the action of
A
is a real-linear map
L^{+}\to L^{+}
. A point of
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
written as a linear combination of elements of
B
in
L^{+}
will be sent to another linear combination of points in
L^{+}
, and as the the span of
L^{+}
is
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
we conclude the image is also in
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
.

\hfill\square

This action is defined on both
\phi_{1}(\kappa)
and its derivatives (both are in generalised Minkowski space and thus
\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2}
), so it also acts on multiflags. We denote the action on a multiflag
[[p;v_{1},...,v_{n}]]
by

A.[[p;v_{1},...,v_{n}]]=[[A.p;A.v_{1},...,A.v_{n}]].

Lemma 2.34
.

The action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
is by orientation-preserving isometries.

Proof.
Represent a point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
as a matrix
S_{p}\in\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2}
:

S_{p}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}T+Z&\mathbf{X}\\
\overline{\mathbf{X}}&T-Z\end{pmatrix}.

The norm on
S_{p}\in\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2}
is given by the pseudo-determinant
\text{pdet}(S_{p})
(up to a constant factor). Define
GL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})^{\triangleright}
by extending the pseudo-determinant condition of
GL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
to allow any value in
\mathbb{R}
; this will allow us to consider
L^{+}\subset GL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
. Action by
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
gives
AS_{p}A^{\dagger}
, but the pseudo-determinant is multiplicative on elements of
GL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})^{\triangleright}
, and it is easy to check that
S_{p}\in GL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})^{\triangleright}
also. This implies

\text{pdet}(AS_{p}A^{\dagger})=\text{pdet}(A)\text{pdet}(S_{p})\text{pdet}(A^{\dagger})=\text{pdet}(S_{p}).

SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
then acts via isometries as it preserves the norm.

Theorem B of
[
1
]
tells us the group
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
is the double cover of the connected orientation-preserving group of Möbius transformations
M_{+}(\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, which implies
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
is also connected; so, if one element of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
has an orientation-preserving action, they all do. But the identity matrix is an element of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
, and its action is orientation-preserving.
\hfill\square

2.7.
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
Equivariance of
\Phi_{1}

The map
\phi_{1}
is equivariant with respect to the
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
-action. For
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
:

(2.35)

\phi_{1}(A\kappa)=(A\kappa)(\kappa^{\dagger}A^{\dagger})=A\phi_{1}(\kappa)A^{\dagger}=A.\phi_{1}(\kappa).

Equivariance also holds for the derivatives:

A.(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(v)=A.(\kappa v^{\dagger}+v\kappa^{\dagger})=A\kappa v^{\dagger}A^{\dagger}+Av\kappa^{\dagger}A^{\dagger}=(A\kappa)(Av)^{\dagger}+(Av)(A\kappa)^{\dagger}=(D_{A\kappa}\phi_{1})(Av).

This equivariance also extends to
\Phi_{1}
. We first generalise Lemma 2.8 of
[
23
]
(and Lemma 4.6.5 of
[
21
]
) to the Lipschitz spinor case.

Lemma 2.36
.

Given a spinor
\kappa
and a set of tangent vectors
\nu_{j},\ 1\leq j\leq n
at
\kappa\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
(that is,
\nu_{j}\in T_{\kappa}S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
), the following are equivalent:

i)

\{\kappa,\nu_{j}\}
is a (non-zero) negative multiple of
i_{j}
for all
j
,

ii)

\nu_{j}=\kappa a_{j}+b_{j}\check{\kappa}i_{j}
for
a_{j}\in\mathcal{B}^{n}
,
b_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
for all
j
,

iii)

[[\phi_{1}(\kappa);(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\nu_{1}),...,(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\nu_{n})]]=\Phi_{1}(\kappa)
.

Proof
.

i)\to ii)
:
\check{\kappa}
is defined so
\{\kappa,\check{\kappa}i_{j}\}
is a negative multiple of
i_{j}
. We can construct any other negative multiple of
i_{j}
scaling by some
b_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Thus, if
\{\kappa,\nu_{j}\}
is a negative multiple of
i_{j}
, there is some choice of
b_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
so that
\{\kappa,\nu_{j}\}=b_{j}\{\kappa,\check{\kappa}i_{j}\}=\{\kappa,b_{j}\check{\kappa}i_{j}\}
. By linearity
\{\kappa,\nu_{j}-b_{j}\check{\kappa}i_{j}\}=0
, so
\nu_{j}=b_{j}\check{\kappa}i_{j}+D
for some
D
in the kernel of the map
\{\kappa,\cdot\}
. From Lemma
1.49
, we know the kernel is all elements of the form
\kappa a_{j}
,
a_{j}\in\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
, and therefore
\nu_{j}=\kappa a_{j}+b_{j}\check{\kappa}i_{j}
. But because
\nu_{j}-b_{j}\check{\kappa}i_{j}=\kappa a_{j}
is a tangent vector, Corollary
1.70
implies
a_{j}\in\mathcal{B}^{n}
.

ii\to i)
: We compute
\{\kappa,\nu_{j}\}
using properties of the bracket from Section
1.5.3
:

\{\kappa,\nu_{j}\}=\{\kappa,\kappa a_{j}\}+b_{j}\{\kappa,\check{\kappa}\}i_{j}=-b_{j}|\kappa|^{2}i_{j}.

As
b_{j}>0
this is a non-zero negative multiple of
i_{j}
.

ii)\to iii)
: Consider (
2.11
). If we apply this to
\nu_{j}=\kappa a_{j}+b_{j}\check{\kappa}i_{j}
and expand we find:

\displaystyle\text{Re}(a_{j})(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\kappa)+b_{j}(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{j}).

The derivative
\text{Re}(a_{j})(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\kappa)=2\text{Re}(a_{j})\kappa\kappa^{\dagger}
is proportional to
\phi_{1}(\kappa)
, so we can rewrite this as:

(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})\left(\kappa a_{j}+b_{j}\check{\kappa}i_{j}\right)=2\text{Re}(a_{j})\phi_{1}(\kappa)+b_{j}(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{j}).

That is, the derivative
(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})\left(\kappa a_{j}+b_{j}\check{\kappa}i_{j}\right)
is a linear combination of
\phi_{1}(\kappa)
and
(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{j})
, for some
b_{j}>0
. This implies the subflags
[[\phi_{1}(\kappa),(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\nu_{j})]]
and
[[\phi_{1}(\kappa),(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{j})]]
are the same for all
j
, and therefore the multiflags are equal.

[[\phi_{1}(\kappa);(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\nu_{1}),...,(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\nu_{n})]]=\Phi_{1}(\kappa).

iii)\to ii)
: The equality of multiflags is equivalent to the equality of their subflags for all
j
. If the
i_{j}
subflags are equal, then the flag plane vectors
(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\nu_{j})
must be a linear combination of the vectors spanning the
i_{j}
subflag in
\Phi_{1}(\kappa)
:

(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\nu_{j})=a_{j}\phi_{1}(\kappa)+b_{j}(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{j}),

where
a_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
and
b_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Substituting
D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\kappa)=2\phi_{1}(\kappa)
, we find

(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\nu_{j})=\frac{a_{j}}{2}D_{\kappa}\phi_{1}(\kappa)+b_{j}(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{j}),

which by the linearity of the derivative implies

\nu_{j}-\frac{a_{j}}{2}\kappa-b_{j}\check{\kappa}i_{j}\in\text{Ker}(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})=\kappa\left(\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\oplus\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\right),

with the last equality following from Theorem
2.12
iii). This implies
\nu_{j}-\frac{a_{j}}{2}\kappa-b_{j}\check{\kappa}i_{j}=\kappa c_{j}
for some
c_{j}\in\mathcal{B}^{n}
, and so
\nu_{j}=\kappa\left(c_{j}+\frac{a_{j}}{2}\right)+b_{j}\check{\kappa}
, satisfying ii).

\hfill\square

With this in hand, we can prove the equivariance of
\Phi_{1}
.

Theorem 2.37
.

The map
\Phi_{1}
is equivariant with respect to the
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
actions on
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
and
\mathcal{MF}^{n}
.

Proof.

Given
\Phi_{1}(\kappa)=[[\phi_{1}(\kappa);(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{1}),(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{2}),...,(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{n})]]
, act on it with an element
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
:

\displaystyle A.\Phi_{1}(\kappa)

\displaystyle=[[A.\phi_{1}(\kappa);A.(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{1}),A.(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{2}),...,A.(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{n})]]

\displaystyle=[[\phi_{1}(A\kappa);(D_{A\kappa}\phi_{1})(A(\check{\kappa}i_{1})),(D_{A\kappa}\phi_{1})(A(\check{\kappa}i_{2})),...,(D_{A\kappa}\phi_{1})(A(\check{\kappa}i_{n}))]].

As
A
preserves the bracket,

(2.38)

\{A\kappa,A(\check{\kappa}i_{j})\}=\{\kappa,\check{\kappa}i_{j}\}=-|\kappa|^{2}i_{j}.

Now we apply Lemma
2.36
; taken together with (
2.38
), it implies the
A\check{\kappa}
will generate the same multiflag as the
\widecheck{A\kappa}
.

\displaystyle[[\phi_{1}(A\kappa);(D_{A\kappa}\phi_{1})(A(\check{\kappa}i_{1})),...,(D_{A\kappa}\phi_{1})(A(\check{\kappa}i_{n}))]]

\displaystyle=[[\phi_{1}(A\kappa);(D_{A\kappa}\phi_{1})(\widecheck{A\kappa}i_{1}),...,(D_{A\kappa}\phi_{1})(\widecheck{A\kappa}i_{n})]]

\hfill\square

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}(A\kappa).

With the domain and codomain of
\Phi_{1}
properly understood, we can consider injectivity and surjectivity.

Theorem 2.39
.

The map
\Phi_{1}:S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to\mathcal{MF}^{n}
is a smooth double cover.

Proof.
Given a point
F\in\mathcal{MF}^{n}
,
F=[[p;v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{n}]]
, we have by definition of
\mathcal{MF}^{n}
that
p\in L^{+}
, but
L^{+}=\text{Im}(\phi_{1})
by Lemma
2.5
, so there is a non-empty set of spinors
\kappa_{p}
which map to
\Phi_{1}(\kappa_{p})=[[p;...]]
. Consider
\Phi_{1}(\kappa)
for
\kappa=(\xi,0)
,
|\xi|=1
. All such
\kappa
have base-point
\phi_{1}(\kappa)=(1,1;0)_{\mathbb{R}}\in L^{+}
, and they can all be found by acting on a base spinor
\kappa_{0}=(1,0)
.

\kappa=A\kappa_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&0\\
0&\xi^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\ A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1}).

\kappa_{0}
is sent to the multiflag
\Phi_{1}(\kappa_{0})=[[(1,1;0)_{\mathbb{R}};\partial_{X_{1}},\partial_{X_{2}},...,\partial_{X_{n}}]]
(the computation follows from (
2.23
)), and by the equivariance of
\Phi_{1}
shown in Theorem
2.37
we have

\Phi_{1}(\kappa)=A.\Phi_{1}(\kappa_{0})=[[(1,1;0)_{\mathbb{R}};A.\partial_{X_{1}},A.\partial_{X_{2}},...,A.\partial_{X_{n}}]].

If we check how
A
acts on these vector fields, it is by the
\sigma(\cdot)
action, which is just a rotation:

A.\partial_{X_{j}}=A\partial_{j}A^{\dagger}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\xi i_{j}\xi^{*}\\
-\xi^{\prime}i_{j}\overline{\xi}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\sigma(\xi)(i_{j})\\
\overline{\sigma(\xi)(i_{j})}&0\end{pmatrix}.

\sigma(\cdot)
is a homomorphism of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
onto the group of rotations of
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, with a fibre
\mathbb{R}^{+}
that can be partially recovered via the norm
N(\cdot)
. As
|\xi|=1
, the only two preimages of a particular rotation will be
\xi,-\xi
.

The set of
\xi\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
satisfying
|\xi|=1
is just the spin group
Spin(n+1)
; all rotations
SO(n+1)
can be given as
\sigma(x)
for
x\in Spin(n+1)
, so all positively oriented orthonormal frames can be reached from
\kappa_{0}
, and thus all multiflags based at
(1,1;0)_{\mathbb{R}}
are mapped to by some spinor
\kappa
by rotating
\kappa_{0}
appropriately.

As
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
acts transitively on
L^{+}
(and, as we’ve just seen, on the space of multiflags based at
(1,1;0)_{\mathbb{R}}
), it acts transitively on
\mathcal{MF}^{n}
, we can send any base-point to any other, and any flag based at one point to any other, implying
\Phi_{1}
is surjective.
\Phi_{1}(\kappa_{1})=\Phi_{1}(\kappa_{2})
if and only if
\kappa_{2}=\pm\kappa_{1}
, implying the map is a double cover. Smoothness follows because the various maps are either defined in terms of polynomial functions, or derivatives of smooth functions.
\hfill\square

Topologically, the map
\Phi_{1}:\ S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to\mathcal{MF}^{n}
is a double covering map from a fibre bundle locally modelled on
Spin(n+1)\times S^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}\to SO(n+1)\times S^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
.

2.8.
Example Computation of a Multiflag

The exponential map sends
\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
to the unit sphere in
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
(see Proposition
1.12
), which sheds some light on why paravectors are a natural object to consider; just as the vectors
\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
are distinguished in the Lie algebra
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}
, their exponentials (the unit paravectors) are distinguished in the Lie group
\mathcal{C}\ell_{0,n}^{\times}
.

Take
\kappa=(e^{V\theta},0)
for
V\in\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
of unit
N
-norm, and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
.
The various derivatives that define our flag planes can be computed to have the following matrix form:

(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{j})=\begin{pmatrix}0&i_{j}-2V_{j}\sin\theta e^{V\theta}\\
-i_{j}-2V_{j}\sin\theta e^{-V\theta}&0\end{pmatrix}.

We can easily read off that
T=Z=0
, but to compute the remaining terms in the standard coordinates
(T,Z;X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{n})_{\mathbb{R}}
we expand the
(1,2)
entry out further.

i_{j}-2V_{j}\sin\theta e^{V\theta}=-2V_{j}\sin\theta\cos\theta-2V_{j}\sin^{2}\theta V+i_{j}.

Expand
V
as
\sum_{k=1}^{n}V_{k}i_{k}
and apply a few double angle formulae.

i_{j}-2V_{j}\sin\theta e^{V\theta}=-V_{j}\sin(2\theta)+V_{j}(\cos(2\theta)-1)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}V_{k}i_{k}\right)+i_{j}.

Clearly the term
X_{0}=-V_{j}\sin(2\theta)
, while for all other
k\in\{1,...,n\},\ k\neq j
we have
X_{k}=V_{j}V_{k}(\cos(2\theta)-1)
. The remaining term is
X_{j}=V_{j}^{2}(\cos(2\theta)-1)+1
, and so our flag plane vectors
(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{j})
can be explicitly written as

(0,0;-V_{j}\sin(2\theta),V_{1}V_{j}(\cos(2\theta)-1),V_{2}V_{j}(\cos(2\theta)-1),...,V_{j}^{2}(\cos(2\theta)-1)+1,...,V_{n}V_{j}(\cos(2\theta)-1))_{\mathbb{R}}.

As with Penrose and Rindler’s null flags
[
27
]
, rotating the spinor by
\theta
rotates the subflags by
2\theta
.

3.
From Multiflags to Horospheres

The multiflag (or, equivalently, decorated ideal point) structure is one way to represent a Lipschitz spinor as a geometric object; it is the natural extension of the null flag of Penrose and Rindler (respectively, their picture of a spin-vector as a tangent vector to
\mathscr{S}^{+}
). But we can go further, and following
[
23
]
,
[
21
]
extend the map into hyperbolic space.

The obvious model to employ is the hyperboloid model of hyperbolic space living ‘inside’ the light-cone. Following
[
23
]
and
[
25
]
we map flagpole vectors to horospheres, which in the hyperboloid model are given by the intersection of particular hyperplanes in
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
with the hyperboloid. We can then intersect the multiflag with the horospheres (or more precisely their tangent spaces); these intersections generate oriented line fields in the Euclidean geometry of the horosphere. These line fields have some nice properties, namely being parallel and orthogonal.

These constructions exploit the close relationship between the hyperboloid model and the light-cone, but our final destination is the upper half-space model; it is there that the nicest explicit forms for the correspondence are found.

Definition 3.1

(Coordinate conventions)
.

The hyperboloid model
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
lives in the generalised Minkowski space
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
. Similar to the coordinate notation introduced at the top of the previous section, we can write coordinates

(T,Z;X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{n})_{\mathbb{H}}\text{ or }(T,Z;\mathbf{X})_{\mathbb{H}},\text{ with }T^{2}-Z^{2}-|\mathbf{X}|^{2}=1,

for the hyperboloid. As this is a submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
we could simply use the subscript
\mathbb{R}
as in the prior section, but the subscript
\mathbb{H}
helps recall our focus on hyperbolic space, and connects more naturally to the variant subscripts
\partial\mathbb{H}
and
T\mathbb{H}
defined below.

The ball model
\mathbb{B}^{n+2}
can be identified (as a topological space) with the unit
26
26

26

under the metric
N(\mathbf{V})=\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{V}}
.
ball of
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
, just as the disc model can be identified with the unit disc in
\mathbb{C}
by applying a new metric. For later convenience we also identify the natural copy of
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\subset\$\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
found by setting the
i_{n+1}
coefficient to zero, and coordinatise
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
as
y_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}i_{j}+wi_{n+1}
; we then use the coordinates

(w;y_{0},y_{1},...,y_{n})_{\mathbb{B}}\text{ or }(w;\textbf{y})_{\mathbb{B}},\ \textbf{y}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1},\ w^{2}+|\textbf{y}|^{2}<1\text{ for the ball model.}

Finally, the upper half-space model is identified as
\mathbb{U}^{n+2}\subset\$\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
, where the boundary plane is identified with
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. We coordinatise
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
as
x_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}i_{j}+zi_{n+1}
, and we write

(z;x_{0},x_{1},...,x_{n})_{\mathbb{U}}\text{ or }(z;\textbf{x})_{\mathbb{U}},\ z>0,\text{ for the upper half-space model.}

We use the same coordinate layout with subscript
\partial\mathbb{H}
,
\partial\mathbb{B}
, or
\partial\mathbb{U}
for the boundary of the hyperboloid, ball, and upper half-space models respectively; the boundary of the hyperboloid is given by
\mathscr{S}^{+}
(the space of rays on the light-cone), the boundary of
\mathbb{B}^{n+2}
is given by the unit length paravectors under the positive-definite metric
N(\mathbf{V})=\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{V}}
on
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
, and the boundary in
\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
is given by the points
(0;\textbf{x})_{\mathbb{U}}\cup\{\infty\}
. As the boundary of
\mathbb{U}
can be identified with
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\cup\{\infty\}
, we often simply write a paravector
x_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}i_{j}
for a coordinate in
\partial\mathbb{U}
.

We also define coordinates with subscripts
T\mathbb{H}
,
T\mathbb{B}
, or
T\mathbb{U}
for the tangent spaces of each model. The arrangement of coordinates is identical to their manifold counterparts.

\lrcorner

3.1.
Mapping Spinors to the Hyperboloid Model

Define
\mathbb{H}^{n}
to be the positive sheet of the hyperboloid given by points
x\in\mathbb{R}^{1,n}
satisfying
(x|x)_{1,n}=1
, and endow it with a metric by restricting the Minkowski metric. This gives a model of hyperbolic space called the
hyperboloid model
.

Definition 3.2
.

A
horosphere
in hyperbolic
n
-space is the limit as
r\to\infty
of a series of
(n-1)
-spheres of radius
r
