which are tangent at a fixed point, and on a fixed side, of a given hyperplane. When
n=2
, we may also call this a
horocycle
.

\lrcorner

In the hyperboloid model, horospheres are given by the intersection of the hyperboloid with a hyperplane that has null normal vector;
27
27

27

A normal vector of norm zero under
(\cdot|\cdot)_{1,n}
.
points of the light-cone parameterise this space, as the light-cone is the space of null vectors (see
[
13
]
for more discussion on this). The first stage of our map will send flagpoles to a hyperplane they define as a normal vector, and then to horospheres defined by the intersection of this hyperplane with the hyperboloid model.

Definition 3.3
.

Denote the space of horospheres in
n
-dimensional hyperbolic space as Hor
n
, regardless of model. The map
\phi_{2}:\ L^{+}\to
Hor
n
sends
p\in L^{+}
to the horosphere defined by the intersection of the hyperplane
\Pi_{p}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}\ |\ (x|p)_{1,n}=1\}
, and the hyperboloid
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}\ |\ (x|x)_{1,n}=1\}
.
We also define
\phi=\phi_{2}\circ\phi_{1}
.

The map
\phi_{2}^{\partial}:\ L^{+}\to\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}
instead sends
p
to the point at infinity of
\phi_{2}(p)
(also called the
centre
of the horosphere). We also define
\phi^{\partial}=\phi_{2}^{\partial}\circ\phi_{1}
.

\lrcorner

Visualising the boundary at infinity is tricky in the hyperboloid model. However, we already have a good representation; the boundary can be defined as the space of rays on the light-cone, which is our celestial sphere
\mathscr{S}^{+}
. The centre of the horosphere
\phi_{2}(p)
is then represented by the ray
\mathbb{R}^{+}p
through the point
p
(see
[
25
]
for details).

To consider questions of equivariance, we need to define an action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on the hyperboloid. The obvious choice is to inherit the action on generalised Minkowski space (see Section
2.6
).

Definition 3.4
.

The action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
is the restriction of the conjugation action defined on
\mathcal{P}^{1,n+2}
. When we introduce other models of hyperbolic space (say a model
M^{n+2}
), the action on
M^{n+2}
is then inherited through an isometry
\pi:\mathbb{H}^{n+2}\to M^{n+2}
. That is, the action of
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on some
x\in M^{n+2}
is defined as
\pi\left(A.\pi^{-1}(x)\right)
. It is equivariant by definition. As it acts on
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
by isometries (and, as we shall see, maps
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}\to\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
), this action also maps null planes to null planes and thus horospheres to horospheres, and so defines an action on Hor
n+2
.

\lrcorner

We must check that this action does restrict to an action on the hyperboloid model. We know from Lemma
2.34
that it preserves the metric, so it does map the hyperboloid to itself; but it must also preserve the positive sheet.

Lemma 3.5
.

The action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
restricts to an action by orientation-preserving isometries on the hyperboloid model
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
.

Proof.
All we need is to show the action restricts to a map
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}\to\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
. To show this, we use the connectedness of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
; because the identity matrix maps the positive sheet to itself, all elements of the connected component containing the identity map also preserve the positive sheet. But the connected component containing the identity is the whole of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
.
\hfill\square

With an action defined, we can check the equivariance of
\phi_{2}
.

Lemma 3.6
.

The maps
\phi_{2}
and
\phi_{2}^{\partial}
are equivariant under the action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
.

Proof.

A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
acts as an isometry on the hyperboloid, as implied by Lemma
2.34
. Explicitly, given points
x,y\in\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
,
(Ax|Ay)_{1,n+2}=(x|y)_{1,n+2}
. A horosphere
\phi_{2}(p)
is defined by the hyperplane
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}\ |\ (x|p)_{1,n+2}=1\}
where it intersects the hyperboloid. The action of
A
then gives us the horosphere
A.\phi_{2}(p)
whose points
x\in\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
satisfy
(A^{-1}x|p)_{1,n+2}=1
. Because
A
is an isometry,
A.\phi_{2}(p)
is defined equivalently by the hyperplane with defining condition

1=(A^{-1}x|p)_{1,n+2}=(AA^{-1}x|Ap)_{1,n+2}=(x|Ap)_{1,n+2},

which is the defining hyperplane for
\phi_{2}(Ap)
.

Similarly,
\phi_{2}^{\partial}
is equivariant as we can just drop all information about the horospheres except their centres.
\hfill\square

3.1.1.
Flags to Line Fields

Consider a multiflag as extending through Minkowski space and intersecting with our newly-found horosphere. These intersections will be straight lines in the Euclidean geometry of the horosphere. Foliating Minkowski space by parallel copies of the flag-planes, this will generate an oriented line field on the horosphere.

Such a line field, strictly speaking, lives in the tangent bundle to the horosphere, so we need a description of the tangent spaces of horospheres. The tangent space to a horosphere is a subspace of both the tangent space of the hyperboloid model, and the tangent space of the defining hyperplane for that horosphere, so it lives in the intersection of those two tangent spaces.

We know from Lemma
2.3
that the tangent space at a point
q
on the hyperboloid is
q^{\perp}
, so we look for the tangent space to the hyperplane.

Lemma 3.7
.

In a quadratic vector space
V
with metric
(\cdot,\cdot)
, the tangent space to the hyperplane
\{x\in V\ |\ (x,p)=k\}
where
p\in V
,
k\in\mathbb{R}
is given by
p^{\perp}
.

Proof.
Similar to Lemma
2.3
, we construct a tangent vector as a derivative of some curve
q_{t}
in the hyperplane.
(q_{t},p)=K\implies(\dot{q}_{t},p)=0
, so
\dot{q}_{t}\in p^{\perp}
, and as the dimensions of the tangent space and the orthogonal complement agree they are in fact equal.
\hfill\square

The tangent space at the point
q
of the horosphere
H_{p}
defined by the hyperplane

\Pi_{p}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}\ |\ (x|p)_{1,n+2}=1\}

will be an
n+1
-dimensional subspace of the intersection
q^{\perp}\cap p^{\perp}
, which implies dim
(q^{\perp}\cap p^{\perp})\geq n+1
. Both
q^{\perp}
and
p^{\perp}
are
n+2
-dimensional, but
q^{\perp}
is spacelike, while
T_{q}\Pi_{p}
contains the light-like direction
\mathbb{R}p
by Lemma
3.7
. So the intersection must be precisely
n+1
-dimensional, and the tangent space of the horosphere at
q
is exactly
q^{\perp}\cap p^{\perp}
.

We can now define line fields by intersecting the flag planes of
\Phi_{1}(\kappa)
with the tangent spaces of
\phi(\kappa)
. The intersection of the
i_{j}
subflag will define the
i_{j}

line field
.

Lemma 3.8
.

The
n
subflags of the multiflag
\Phi_{1}(\kappa)
intersect the horosphere
\phi(\kappa)
to form
n
linearly independent oriented lines. By taking the intersection with the family of parallel translates of the multiflag across
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
, we instead get
n
linearly independent oriented line fields.

Proof.
As we know the subflags only intersect one another along the flagpole
p\mathbb{R}
, the multiflag spans a subspace of dimension
n+1
(including the light-like flagpole); call this subspace
F_{n}
. The tangent space at a point
q
on the horosphere
\phi(\kappa)
is given by
T_{q}(\phi(\kappa))=q^{\perp}\cap\phi_{1}(\kappa)^{\perp}
.

F_{n}
lies within the tangent space to the light-cone, so
F_{n}\subset\phi_{1}(\kappa)^{\perp}
. The intersection of the horosphere with the subspace
F_{n}
is thus

T_{q}(\phi_{2}(p))\cap F_{n}=q^{\perp}\cap\phi_{1}(\kappa)^{\perp}\cap F_{n}=q^{\perp}\cap F_{n}.

This intersection of vector spaces is at most
n+1
dimensional, the dimension of
F_{n}
; but at minimum it is
n
dimensional, because
q^{\perp}
is
n+2
dimensional and
F_{n}
is
n+1
dimensional, while they are within an
n+3
dimensional vector space
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
. There is at least one light-like vector
\phi_{1}(\kappa)
contained in
F_{n}
that does not sit within the
q^{\perp}
, as
q^{\perp}
is necessarily spacelike (since the metric restricts to be positive definite on the hyperboloid). This implies the intersection is precisely
n
dimensional. All the subflags must therefore intersect the horosphere in at least a 1 dimensional subspace; but as each subflag has one light-like dimension, the intersection is precisely 1 dimensional.

Say a subflag is parameterised as
f_{j}=p\mathbb{R}+v_{j}\mathbb{R}
, where
p\mathbb{R}
is the flagpole. Via Section
2.1.2
, given one of these flag-planes an orientation is defined on
f_{j}/p\mathbb{R}
, which then defines an orientation on the corresponding line. Across the
n
subflags, we then have
n
linearly independent oriented lines. By taking parallel copies of the multiflag we foliate
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
, and so we define a line field at every point of the horosphere.
\hfill\square

3.1.2.
The Map
\Phi_{2}

The full map from
\mathcal{MF}^{n}
to hyperbolic space will send a multiflag
[[p;v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{n}]]
to the horosphere
\phi_{2}(p)
with line fields in its Euclidean geometry defined by taking all parallel translates of the subflags
[[p,v_{j}]]
, and intersecting them with the horosphere. Note that, when describing a line field by a vector pointing along the lines, scalar multiples of the same vector
v_{j}
denote the same field; we can always normalise the vectors describing these line fields where necessary. We say such a vector
directs
or
specifies
the line field.

Definition 3.9
.

The space
L
Hor
n+2
is the space of horospheres in
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
carrying an ordered set of
n
linearly independent oriented line fields
(L_{1},...,L_{n})
. The individual line field
L_{j}
is referred to as the
i_{j}
line field
.

The map
\Phi_{2}:\mathcal{MF}^{n}\to L\text{Hor}^{n+2}
sends a multiflag
[[p;v_{1},v_{2},...,v_{n}]]
to the horosphere
\phi_{2}(p)
with the line field defined at each point
q
by
T_{q}\phi_{2}(p)\cap[[p,v_{j}]]
and the induced orientations. We also define the map
\Phi=\Phi_{2}\circ\Phi_{1}
.

\lrcorner

We will later find that the image of
\Phi_{2}
only includes certain rather nice line fields, so the codomain will be restricted; but, for now, all we know is that it maps multiflags to horospheres with
n
linearly independent oriented line fields defined upon them.

We define an action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on
L
Hor
n+2
by restricting its action on
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
(which is equivalent to an action by
SO(1,n+2)^{+}
). The action on hyperbolic space is by orientation-preserving isometries and sends horospheres to horospheres, and its derivative sends line fields on horospheres to line fields on horospheres.

3.1.3.
Parallel Line Fields

The line fields defined by these intersections have another property, namely being
parallel
(as demonstrated later, in Corollary
3.38
). This means nothing other than being parallel in the Euclidean sense, but it is worth making a precise definition for this context.

Isometries that generate translations along a horosphere form a subgroup of the parabolic transformations in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
. However, as noted in Section 3.9 of
[
21
]
, in dimension 4 and above there are parabolic transformations that act on a horosphere, but do not act by translations. Combining and extending Definition 3.9.3 and Lemma 3.9.1 of that paper, we can define the translations appropriately.

Definition 3.10
.

We call a matrix
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
that satisfies any of the following conditions:

i)

A\neq 1
and
(A-1)^{2}=0
,

ii)

A
is conjugate to
P_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}
,

iii)

A=\begin{pmatrix}1-\xi\eta^{*}&\xi\xi^{*}\\
-\eta\eta^{*}&1+\eta\xi^{*}\end{pmatrix}
for some
(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
,

a
parabolic translation
. The set of parabolic translation matrices is denoted
P
.

A line field on the horosphere
H\in\text{Hor}^{n+2}
is
parallel
if it is invariant under the action of all parabolic translations in
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
fixing
H
(also referred to as
translations on the horosphere
).

\lrcorner

We demonstrate the 3 conditions of Definition
3.10
are, in fact, equivalent. First, though, a technical lemma.

Lemma 3.11
.

For the Clifford matrix

A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1}),

the following variations on the pseudo-determinant condition hold:

(3.12)

ad^{*}-bc^{*}=da^{*}-cb^{*}=d^{*}a-b^{*}c=1.

Proof.
Taking the original condition on the pseudo-determinant that holds for
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
, we manipulate it to get the new forms.

|d|^{2}\cdot 1=|d|^{2}(a^{*}d-c^{*}b)=d(a^{*}d-c^{*}b)\overline{d}=da^{*}|d|^{2}-dc^{*}b\overline{d}.

By Corollary
1.31
we have
dc^{*}=cd^{*}
, and as
b\overline{d}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
we have
b\overline{d}=(b\overline{d})^{*}=d^{\prime}b^{*}
.

|d|^{2}=da^{*}|d|^{2}-cd^{*}d^{\prime}b^{*}=|d|^{2}(da^{*}-cb^{*}).

This either tells us
da^{*}-cb^{*}=1
, or
|d|^{2}=0
. Varying the argument a little, we find

|c|^{2}\cdot 1=|c|^{2}(a^{*}d-c^{*}b)=c^{\prime}(a^{*}d-c^{*}b)c^{*}=c^{\prime}a^{*}dc^{*}-|c|^{2}bc^{*}=|c|^{2}(ad^{*}-bc^{*}),

implying either
|c|^{2}=0
or
ad^{*}-bc^{*}=1
. As the pseudo-determinant
a^{*}d-c^{*}b\neq 0
we must have at least one of
c,d
non-zero, so at least one of
da^{*}-cb^{*}=1,\text{ or }ad^{*}-bc^{*}=1
must hold. But if
ad^{*}-bc^{*}=1
then
(ad^{*}-bc^{*})^{*}=da^{*}-cb^{*}=1
,
and similarly if
da^{*}-cb^{*}=1
then
1=(da^{*}-cb^{*})^{*}=ad^{*}-bc^{*}
. We conclude both ‘variant pseudo-determinants’ are equal to
1
. The final equality of (
3.12
) then holds because the pseudo-determinant
a^{*}d-c^{*}b=1
is invariant under
\cdot^{*}
.
\hfill\square

Lemma 3.13
.

The following conditions on a matrix
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
are equivalent.

i)

A\neq 1
and
(A-1)^{2}=0
,

ii)

A
is conjugate to
P_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\\
\end{pmatrix}
,

iii)

A=\begin{pmatrix}1-\xi\eta^{*}&\xi\xi^{*}\\
-\eta\eta^{*}&1+\eta\xi^{*}\end{pmatrix}
for some
(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
.

Proof.

ii)\to iii)
: Given a Clifford matrix

B=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\\
\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1}),

we compute

\displaystyle B\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}B^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}ad^{*}-bc^{*}-ac^{*}&aa^{*}+ba^{*}-ab^{*}\\
-cc^{*}+cd^{*}-dc^{*}&da^{*}-cb^{*}+ca^{*}\end{pmatrix}.

From Lemma
3.11
we have that
ad^{*}-bc^{*}=da^{*}-cb^{*}=1
, and we also know
ab^{*}=ba^{*}
and
cd^{*}=dc^{*}
from Corollary
1.31
. Upon simplifying:

B\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}B^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1-ac^{*}&a^{*}\\
-cc^{*}&1+ca^{*}\end{pmatrix}.

As
B,P_{0}\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
the rows and columns are spinors by Proposition
1.32
, so
(a,c)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
and ii) implies iii).

iii)\to ii)
: If
A
satisfies condition iii) then, by Proposition
1.59
, there is a matrix
B\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
with first column
(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, and conjugation of
P_{0}
by the matrix
B
gives
A
by the computation above.

ii)\to i)
: The matrix
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
satisfies i) if and only if any conjugate of
A
by
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
satisfies i). To see this, let
B\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
:

\displaystyle(BAB^{-1}-1)^{2}=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow BA^{2}B^{-1}-2BAB^{-1}+1=0\Leftrightarrow(A-1)^{2}=0.

The matrix
P_{0}
satisfies i), so ii) implies i).

i)\to ii)
: Suppose the matrix
A=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{pmatrix}
satisfies i). We show
A
is conjugate to either an upper or lower triangular matrix with both diagonal entries
1
.

If
\gamma=0
then, as
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
, the restriction on the pseudo-determinant implies
\delta=\alpha^{*-1}
. The
(1,1)
entry of the equation
(A-1)^{2}=0
is
(\alpha-1)^{2}=0
, so
\alpha=\delta=1
and
A
is an upper triangular matrix with
1
along the diagonal as claimed. Note that, as the second column must form a Lipschitz spinor, we have
\beta\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

If
\gamma\neq 0
, then by
[
2
]
equation (15) we can conjugate
A
into the ‘normal form’

N_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{V}\gamma&\mathbf{V}\gamma\mathbf{V}-\gamma^{*-1}\\
\gamma&\gamma\mathbf{V}\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. As above,
A
satisfies i) if and only if any conjugate of
A
does; we compute the lower left entry of

(N_{A}-1)^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{V}\gamma-1&\mathbf{V}\gamma\mathbf{V}-\gamma^{*-1}\\
\gamma&\gamma\mathbf{V}-1\end{pmatrix}^{2},

and use
(N_{A}-1)^{2}=0
to find the equation
\gamma(\mathbf{V}\gamma-1)+(\gamma\mathbf{V}-1)\gamma=2\gamma(\mathbf{V}\gamma-1)=0
. We’ve chosen
\gamma\neq 0
so this implies
\mathbf{V}\gamma=1
and
\mathbf{V}=\gamma^{-1}
, so
\gamma
is a paravector and

N_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\gamma&1\end{pmatrix}.

A
is conjugate to a lower triangular matrix with diagonal entries
1
, as claimed.

This shows an
A
satisfying i) is conjugate to one of the two following forms:

N_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\beta\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\ \beta\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\text{ or }N_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\gamma&1\end{pmatrix},\ \gamma\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

We know
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
acts on
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
by
\sigma
as the group of rotations plus a homothety, so we can send
1
to any other paravector in
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Thus there is either an element
\rho_{\beta}\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
such that

(3.14)

\sigma(\rho_{\beta})(1)=\rho_{\beta}\rho_{\beta}^{*}=\beta

in the first case, or an element
\rho_{\gamma}\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
such that
\sigma(\rho_{\gamma})(1)=\rho_{\gamma}\rho_{\gamma}^{*}=-\gamma
in the second. This lets us write
N_{A}
as either

N_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\rho_{\beta}\rho_{\beta}^{*}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\text{ or }N_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\rho_{\gamma}\rho_{\gamma}^{*}&1\end{pmatrix},

which we recognise as the form of iii) for either the spinor
(\rho_{\beta},0)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
or
(0,\rho_{\gamma})\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. Then, by the previously proven equivalence of iii) with ii), this implies
N_{A}
is conjugate to
P_{0}
, and as
A
is conjugate to
N_{A}
it is also conjugate to
P_{0}
as claimed.

\hfill\square

Now we find these parabolic translations in an explicit form for a given spinor
\kappa
.

Lemma 3.15
.

The subgroup of parabolic translations fixing a Lipschitz spinor
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)
is given by

(3.16)

P^{\kappa}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1-\xi\mathbf{V}\eta^{*}&\xi\mathbf{V}\xi^{*}\\
-\eta\mathbf{V}\eta^{*}&1+\eta\mathbf{V}\xi^{*}\end{pmatrix}\ \middle|\ \mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right\}.

For a fixed
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, the corresponding group element is denoted
P^{\kappa}_{\mathbf{V}}
. This provides an explicit isomorphism between
P^{\kappa}
and
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

Proof.

The subgroup of parabolic translations fixing
(1,0)
takes the form

P^{(1,0)}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbf{V}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\ \middle|\ \mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right\}.

It is simple to check these matrices fix
(1,0)
; for the converse, take an element
A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
fixing
(1,0)
:

\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix},

and we read off the equations
a=1,\ c=0
. The pseudo-determinant condition for
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
then imposes
a^{*}d-c^{*}b=d=1
.
But
(a,b)=(1,b)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
by Proposition
1.32
, which implies
b\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and
A\in P^{(1,0)}
. This implies
P^{(1,0)}
is the maximal subgroup fixing
(1,0)
, and therefore the maximal subgroup of parabolic translations fixing
(1,0)
.

For an arbitrary spinor
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
we conjugate by the matrix of (
1.35
) to send
\kappa
to
(1,0)
, and act by
P^{(1,0)}
.

\begin{pmatrix}\xi&-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{*-1}\\
\eta&\frac{1}{2}\xi^{*-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbf{V}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\xi^{-1}&\frac{1}{2}\eta^{-1}\\
-\eta^{*}&\xi^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\xi\\
\eta\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi\\
\eta\end{pmatrix}.

The associated matrix subgroup
P^{\kappa}
is then

(3.17)

\begin{pmatrix}\xi&-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{*-1}\\
\eta&\frac{1}{2}\xi^{*-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbf{V}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\xi^{-1}&\frac{1}{2}\eta^{-1}\\
-\eta^{*}&\xi^{*}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1-\xi\mathbf{V}\eta^{*}&\xi\mathbf{V}\xi^{*}\\
-\eta\mathbf{V}\eta^{*}&1+\eta\mathbf{V}\xi^{*}\end{pmatrix}.

If there were an element
k\notin P^{\kappa}
fixing
\kappa
, then by conjugation we would find an element
\tilde{k}
fixing
(1,0)
not in
P^{(1,0)}
, a contradiction.

It is clear that the map
\mathbf{V}\to P^{\kappa}_{\mathbf{V}}
is surjective, and a simple matrix multiplication shows it is a homomorphism, so to prove this is an isomorphism we only need consider injectivity. Let
P^{\kappa}_{\mathbf{V}_{1}}=P^{\kappa}_{\mathbf{V}_{2}}
, and assume
\xi\neq 0
. Then the
(1,2)
entry of equating these matrices gives
\xi\mathbf{V}_{1}\xi^{*}=\xi\mathbf{V}_{2}\xi^{*}
, but as
\xi\neq 0
both
\xi
and
\xi^{*}
are elements of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
and are thus invertible, so
\mathbf{V}_{1}=\mathbf{V}_{2}
. If
\xi=0
then
\eta\neq 0
and we can make a similar argument from equating the
(2,1)
entries instead.
\hfill\square

Remark
.

Parabolic translation matrices can be characterised by Definition
3.10
iii):

A=\begin{pmatrix}1-\xi\eta^{*}&\xi\xi^{*}\\
-\eta\eta^{*}&1+\eta\xi^{*}\end{pmatrix}\text{ for some }(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}.

Compared to this, the matrices

P^{\kappa}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1-\xi\mathbf{V}\eta^{*}&\xi\mathbf{V}\xi^{*}\\
-\eta\mathbf{V}\eta^{*}&1+\eta\mathbf{V}\xi^{*}\end{pmatrix}\ \middle|\ \mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right\}

of (
3.16
) (a special family of parabolic translation matrices) seem to have an extra parameter. Of course, for
P^{\kappa}
we have
\xi,\eta
fixed, and by a similar argument to that preceeding (
3.14
) we will have
\mathbf{V}=\alpha\alpha^{*}
for some
\alpha\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. Then the matrices of
P^{\kappa}
will take the form

\begin{pmatrix}1-(\xi\alpha)(\eta\alpha)^{*}&(\xi\alpha)(\xi\alpha)^{*}\\
-(\eta\alpha)(\eta\alpha)^{*}&1+(\eta\alpha)(\xi\alpha)^{*}\end{pmatrix},

which satisfies Definition
3.10
iii) for the spinor
(\xi\alpha,\eta\alpha)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
.

A horosphere
H
can be parameterised by taking a point
q\in H
and looking at its image under the action of the subgroup of parabolic translations fixing
H
. We can show this for the case of
\kappa=(1,0)
and handle all other cases by conjugation as above.

Proposition 3.18
.

The group
P^{(1,0)}
of parabolic translations fixing
(1,0)
acts simply transitively on the horosphere
H_{0}=\phi(1,0)
.

Proof.
To show transitivity, it is enough to show every point
h\in H_{0}
can be sent to the point
q_{0}=(1,0;0,...,0)_{\mathbb{H}}\in H_{0}
; we can then invert and multiply elements of
P^{(1,0)}
to make a map sending any point to any other.

Let

h=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}T+Z&\mathbf{X}\\
\overline{\mathbf{X}}&T-Z\end{pmatrix}\in H_{0}.

By definition it satisfies
(h|\phi_{1}(1,0))_{1,n+2}=T-Z=1
. We apply the parabolic transformation with
\mathbf{V}=-\overline{X}
:

\displaystyle P^{(1,0)}_{-\mathbf{X}}.h

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}(T+Z)-|\mathbf{X}|^{2}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

As the point
h
is in the hyperboloid model and
T-Z=1
we know
|h|^{2}=4\text{pdet}(h)=(T+Z)-|X|^{2}=1
, which tells us
h
was sent to
q_{0}
, as desired.

P^{(1,0)}_{-\mathbf{X}}.h=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}=q_{0}.

To prove the action is free, consider the action of an arbitrary element
P^{(1,0)}_{\mathbf{V}}\in P^{(1,0)}
on
h\in H_{0}
:

\displaystyle P^{(1,0)}_{\mathbf{V}}.h

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}(T+Z)+\mathbf{X}\overline{\mathbf{V}}+\overline{\mathbf{X}\overline{\mathbf{V}}}+|\mathbf{V}|^{2}&\mathbf{X}+\mathbf{V}\\
\overline{\mathbf{X}}+\overline{\mathbf{V}}&1\end{pmatrix}.

For
h
to be a fixed point of the transformation, we require

\begin{pmatrix}T+Z&\mathbf{X}\\
\overline{\mathbf{X}}&T-Z\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}(T+Z)+\mathbf{X}\overline{\mathbf{V}}+\overline{\mathbf{X}\overline{\mathbf{V}}}+|\mathbf{V}|^{2}&\mathbf{X}+\mathbf{V}\\
\overline{\mathbf{X}}+\overline{\mathbf{V}}&1\end{pmatrix},

and equating the
(1,2)
entries implies
\mathbf{V}=0
and
P^{(1,0)}_{\mathbf{V}}
is the identity transformation.
\hfill\square

Corollary 3.19
.

The group
P^{\kappa}
acts simply transitively on the horosphere
H_{\kappa}=\phi(\xi,\eta)
.

Proof.
The group
P^{\kappa}
is just a conjugation of
P^{(1,0)}
. Factored as in the left-hand side of (
3.17
), it maps
(\xi,\eta)
to
(1,0)
, acts by
P^{(1,0)}
, then maps it back to
(\xi,\eta)
. By Lemma
3.6
and (
2.35
) the map
\phi=\phi_{2}\circ\phi_{1}
is equivariant with respect to the
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
actions, so
A
also maps
\phi(\xi,\eta)
to
\phi(1,0)
bijectively. By Proposition
3.18
the action of
P^{(1,0)}
on
\phi(1,0)
is simply transitive, which taken with the bijective maps between the horospheres implies
P^{\kappa}
acts simply transitively on
\phi(\xi,\eta)
.
\hfill\square

3.2.
The Upper Half-Space Model

For explicit computations, the upper half-space model

\mathbb{U}^{n+2}=\{(z;x_{0},x_{1},...,x_{n})_{\mathbb{U}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+2}\ |\ z>0\}\text{ with metric }ds^{2}=\frac{dz^{2}+dx_{0}^{2}+dx_{1}^{2}+...+dx_{n}^{2}}{z^{2}}

gives clean expressions for the spinor-horosphere correspondence and the action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
. We identify
28
28

28

Thess identifications follows naturally from
[
1
]
, where he similarly identifies the upper half-space and its boundary with paravector spaces to allow the action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
to extend to hyperbolic space.

\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
with
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
, the hyperplane
z=0
with
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\subset\$\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
(treating
z
as the coefficient of
i_{n+1}
), and
\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
with
\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
. The coordinates on the boundary
(x_{0},x_{1},...,x_{n})_{\partial\mathbb{U}}
can then be given as a paravector
\textbf{x}=x_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}i_{j}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.

Horospheres in this model either appear as horizontal hyperplanes if centred at
\infty
(in which case we refer to the z-coordinate of the hyperplane as the
Euclidean height
) or as
(n+1)
-spheres tangent at points on the boundary; that point of tangency is the centre of the horosphere. Call the maximum value of
z
on such an
(n+1)
-sphere the
Euclidean diameter
of the horosphere.

3.2.1.
Maps Between Models

We can map the hyperboloid model to the conformal ball model
\mathbb{B}^{n+2}
via projection:

\pi_{1}:\ \mathbb{H}^{n+2}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}^{n+2},\ (T,Z;X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{n})_{\mathbb{H}}\to\frac{1}{1+T}(Z;X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{n})_{\mathbb{B}}=(w;y_{0},y_{1},...,y_{n})_{\mathbb{B}},

where we introduce coordinates
w,y_{0},...,y_{n}
on
\mathbb{B}^{n+2}
, thought of as

(3.20)

(w;y_{0},y_{1},...,y_{n})_{\mathbb{B}}\to y_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}i_{j}+wi_{n+1}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+2}.

We can extend this map to the boundary; the sphere at infinity is given by projectivising the light-cone, which we take advantage of to define a map
\partial\mathbb{H}^{n+2}\to\partial\mathbb{B}^{n+2}
as a map from the light-cone to the boundary sphere of the ball model.
29
29

29

This map is constant on rays of
L^{+}
, and so descends to
\mathscr{S}^{+}
.

\pi_{1}^{\partial}:\ L^{+}\to\partial\mathbb{B}^{n+2},\ (T,Z;X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{n})_{\partial\mathbb{H}}\to\frac{1}{T}(Z;X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{n})_{\partial\mathbb{B}}=(w;y_{0},y_{1},...,y_{n})_{\partial\mathbb{B}}.

The image of
\pi_{1}^{\partial}
satisfies a relation induced by the defining relation
|p|^{2}=0
for the light-cone:

w^{2}+y_{0}^{2}+y_{1}^{2}+...+y_{n}^{2}=1,

which confirms that the boundary really does map to the unit hypersphere in
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
. We then map to the upper half-space model via stereographic projection on the boundary:

\pi_{2}^{\partial}:\ \partial\mathbb{B}^{n+2}\rightarrow\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2},\ (w;y_{0},y_{1},...,y_{n})_{\partial\mathbb{B}}\to\frac{1}{1-w}\left(y_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}i_{j}\right)=x_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}i_{j},

where
x_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}i_{j}=\textbf{x}
are the paravector coordinates on
\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
introduced above. Let

\pi^{\partial}:L^{+}\to\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2},\ \pi^{\partial}=\pi_{2}^{\partial}\circ\pi_{1}^{\partial}

be the boundary map from the hyperboloid model to upper half-space.

\pi_{2}^{\partial}
extends to an isometry
\pi_{2}
on the interior, which we describe explicitly below. However, we can also compute what we need on the boundary by considering the action on the endpoints of intersecting geodesics; simply take two straight lines and see where their endpoints on
\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
get mapped to under the action, and you can track how their intersection moves.

Geodesics in hyperbolic space can be specified by the two points where they meet the boundary at right angles. Thus, an oriented geodesic
\gamma\in\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
can be specified by an ordered pair of paravectors
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
(with the possibility one is infinite) in
\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2}\cong\$\widehat{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
.

Definition 3.21
.

The geodesic
\gamma(\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Y})
is defined to be the unique geodesic in
\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
that intersects the boundary at
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}
, oriented from
\mathbf{X}
to
\mathbf{Y}
. The geodesic
\gamma(w_{1};\textbf{x}\to w_{2};\textbf{y})
is similarly the oriented geodesic from
(w_{1};x_{0},...,x_{n})_{\partial\mathbb{B}}
to
(w_{2};y_{0},...,y_{n})_{\partial\mathbb{B}}
in the ball model.

\lrcorner

On the final page of
[
1
]
, Ahlfors defines a generalised Cayley mapping
\mathfrak{S}
from the upper half-space model to the ball model, after identifying each with a subset of
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
appropriately. In the case of the ball model, this identification is given by (
3.20
); the identification for
\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
is given in the discussion of Section
3.2
.

Definition 3.22
.

For a model
M
of hyperbolic space, let
\widehat{M}=M\cup\partial M
be the closure of the model. The generalised Cayley transform
\mathfrak{S}
is the isometry defined by:

\mathfrak{S}:\ \widehat{\mathbb{U}}^{n+2}\to\widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{n+2},\ \mathfrak{S}(x)=(x-i_{n+1})(x+i_{n+1})^{-1}.

It is invertible, with inverse given by

\mathfrak{S}^{-1}:\ \widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{n+2}\to\widehat{\mathbb{U}}^{n+2},\ \mathfrak{S}^{-1}(x)=(1-x)^{-1}(1+x)i_{n+1}.

As written, this map doesn’t agree on the boundary with the map
\pi_{2}^{\partial}
. But, by adjusting it, we can align the maps.

Definition 3.23
.

Our map from the ball model to the upper half-space model is given by

\pi_{2}:\ \widehat{\mathbb{B}}^{n+2}\to\widehat{\mathbb{U}}^{n+2},\ \pi_{2}(x)=\frac{|1+i_{n+1}\overline{x}|^{2}}{|1-i_{n+1}\overline{x}|^{2}}\mathfrak{S}^{-1}(-i_{n+1}\overline{x}).

Proposition 3.24
.

\pi_{2}^{\partial}(W)=\pi_{2}(W)
, for all
W\in\partial\mathbb{B}^{n+2}
.

Proof.
We apply both sides to the same generic point
W\in\partial\mathbb{B}^{n+2}
:

(3.25)

W=y_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}i_{j}+wi_{n+1}\in\partial\mathbb{B}^{n+2}.

Taking our modified Cayley transform
\pi_{2}
, we substitute the map
\mathfrak{S}^{-1}
and expand some brackets.

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(W)

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{|1-i_{n+1}\overline{W}|^{2}}(Wi_{n+1}+i_{n+1}\overline{W})i_{n+1},

where we use that
|W|=1
for
W\in\partial\mathbb{B}^{n+2}
. If we expand
W
using (
3.25
), we can “commute” the
i_{n+1}
terms around (flipping the signs of
i_{j}
with
1\leq j\leq n
), and simplify further.

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(W)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{|1-i_{n+1}\overline{W}|^{2}}\left(y_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}i_{j}\right).

We then expand the scaling factor out front and expand
W
with (
3.25
), which gives us

\hfill\square

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(W)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-w}\left(y_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}i_{j}\right)=\pi_{2}^{\partial}(W).

Because this map agrees with
\pi_{2}^{\partial}
on the boundary, and isometries extend to the interior of hyperbolic space uniquely, this map is equivalent to the map given by looking at intersecting geodesics. However, while this is an explicit construction, in practice it often proves easier to apply the boundary map and look at intersecting geodesics to track points in the interior.

Proposition 3.26
.

Given a point
W=(w;y_{0},y_{1},...,y_{n})\in\mathbb{B}^{n+2}
, the map
\pi_{2}
can be written explicitly in component form as

\pi_{2}(W)=\frac{(1-|W|^{2};2y_{0},2y_{1},...,2y_{n})_{\mathbb{U}}}{1-2w+|W|^{2}},

or purely in terms of the coordinates:

\pi_{2}(W)=\frac{\left(1-\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n}y_{j}^{2}+w^{2}\right);2y_{0},2y_{1},...,2y_{n}\right)_{\mathbb{U}}}{\sum_{j=0}^{n}y_{j}^{2}+(w-1)^{2}}.

Proof.
We expand
W
as

(3.27)

W=y_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}i_{j}+wi_{n+1}\in\mathbb{B}^{n+2},

and compute:

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(W)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|1-i_{n+1}\overline{W}|^{2}}(1-|W|^{2}-(Wi_{n+1}+i_{n+1}\overline{W}))i_{n+1}.

We substitute (
3.27
) to allow some manoeuvring, and we find:

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(W)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|1-i_{n+1}\overline{W}|^{2}}\left((1-|W|^{2})i_{n+1}+2\left(y_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}i_{j}\right)\right).

We can expand and simplify the denominator:

\displaystyle|1-i_{n+1}\overline{W}|^{2}

\displaystyle=(1+|W|^{2}-i_{n+1}\overline{W}+Wi_{n+1}).

Expand
W
in terms of components once more to find
|1-i_{n+1}\overline{W}|^{2}=1-2w+|W|^{2}
.
In total, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(W)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\left(y_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}i_{j}\right)+(1-|W|^{2})i_{n+1}}{1-2w+|W|^{2}},

which can be rearranged into the forms given in the proposition statement.
\hfill\square

We combine our maps between models to give a map from the hyperboloid directly to the upper half-space model.

\pi:\mathbb{H}^{n+2}\to\mathbb{U}^{n+2},\ \pi=\pi_{2}\circ\pi_{1}.

3.2.2.
The Action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on Upper Half-Space

The action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on
\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
is, by Definition
3.4
, inherited from the action on the hyperboloid model in Minkowski space. That is, for
p\in\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
we define
A.p=\pi^{\partial}\left(A.(\pi^{\partial})^{-1}(p)\right)
, and we then extend the map on the boundary to the interior by considering the action on the endpoints of geodesics. But we have another, rather more natural action on
\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
, defined by letting
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
act on the boundary as Möbius transformations.

Lemma 3.28
.

The action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on
\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
inherited from the action on
\partial\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
is equivalent to the standard action by Möbius transformations.

Proof.

The map
\pi^{\partial}
is given in coordinates by

\pi^{\partial}:L^{+}\to\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2},\ (T,Z;X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{n})_{\partial\mathbb{H}}\to\frac{1}{T-Z}\left(X_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}X_{j}i_{j}\right)=\frac{1}{T-Z}\mathbf{X}.

Consider acting on a point
30
30

30

This is really a representative for a ray along
L^{+}
; we can think of quotienting by
\mathbb{R}^{+}
.

p=(T,Z;\mathbf{X})_{\partial\mathbb{H}}
in the boundary
\mathscr{S}^{+}
of the hyperboloid model. Action by the element

A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in\ SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})

results in the point
A.p
with coordinates
31
31

31

We use
\mathbf{Y}
since
\mathbf{X}
’ would clash with our involution
\cdot^{\prime}
.

(T^{\prime},Z^{\prime};\mathbf{Y})
:

\displaystyle 2T^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(T+Z)(|a|^{2}+|c|^{2})+(T-Z)(|b|^{2}+|d|^{2})+2\text{Re}(a\mathbf{X}\overline{b}+c\mathbf{X}\overline{d}),

\displaystyle 2Z^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(T+Z)(|a|^{2}-|c|^{2})+(T-Z)(|b|^{2}-|d|^{2})+2\text{Re}(a\mathbf{X}\overline{b}-c\mathbf{X}\overline{d}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}

\displaystyle=2\left((T+Z)a\overline{c}+(T-Z)b\overline{d}+a\mathbf{X}\overline{d}+b\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{c}\right).

Feeding this to
\pi^{\partial}
gives us the image

(3.29)

\pi^{\partial}(A.p)=\frac{(T+Z)a\overline{c}+(T-Z)b\overline{d}+a\mathbf{X}\overline{d}+b\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{c}}{((T+Z)|c|^{2}+(T-Z)|d|^{2}+2\text{Re}(c\mathbf{X}\overline{d}))}\in\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2}.

Now we map to
\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
first,
and apply the Möbius transformation.

\displaystyle A.\pi^{\partial}(p)

\displaystyle=A.\left(\frac{\mathbf{X}}{T-Z}\right)=\left(a\frac{\mathbf{X}}{T-Z}+b\right)\left(c\frac{\mathbf{X}}{T-Z}+d\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(a|\mathbf{X}|^{2}\overline{c}+(T-Z)(a\mathbf{X}\overline{d}+b\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{c})+(T-Z)^{2}b\overline{d}\right)\left|c\mathbf{X}+d(T-Z)\right|^{-2}.

As
p
is represented by a point on the light-cone its coordinates satisfy
T^{2}-Z^{2}-|\mathbf{X}|^{2}=0
, implying
|\mathbf{X}|^{2}=(T-Z)(T+Z)
. Making this substitution, our expression then reduces to (
3.29
):

\displaystyle A.\pi^{\partial}(p)

\displaystyle=(T-Z)\left((T+Z)a\overline{c}+(T-Z)b\overline{d}+a\mathbf{X}\overline{d}+b\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{c}\right)\left(\left(c\mathbf{X}+(T-Z)d\right)\left(\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{c}+(T-Z)\overline{d}\right)\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{(T+Z)a\overline{c}+(T-Z)b\overline{d}+a\mathbf{X}\overline{d}+b\overline{\mathbf{X}}\overline{c}}{(T+Z)|c|^{2}+(T-Z)|d|^{2}+2\text{Re}(c\mathbf{X}\overline{d})}.

For the final equality we need
c\mathbf{X}\overline{d}+\overline{c\mathbf{X}\overline{d}}\in\mathbb{R}
; if
c
or
d
is
0
this is trivial, so assume
c,d\neq 0
. This equality follows from the fact that
(c,d)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
by Proposition
1.32
; this implies
c=\mathbf{V}d
for some
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, and then
c\mathbf{X}\overline{d}=|d|^{2}\mathbf{V}Ad_{d}(\mathbf{X})
is of degree
\leq 2
, implying
c\mathbf{X}\overline{d}+\overline{c\mathbf{X}\overline{d}}\in\mathbb{R}
.
\hfill\square

From Section
2.7
, we know
\Phi_{1}
is equivariant. We can now extend this equivariance to the entire map
\Phi
.

Theorem 3.30
.

The map
\Phi:S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to L\text{Hor}^{n+2}
is equivariant with respect to the
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
-action.

Proof.
We’ve already defined an action on both domain and codomain, so all that’s left is to show that
\Phi
commutes with the group action. We have that
\Phi_{1}
is equivariant (Theorem
2.37
), as is
\phi_{2}
(Lemma
3.6
), so we focus on
\Phi_{2}
.

But the action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on both
\mathcal{MF}^{n}
and
L\text{Hor}^{n+2}
is inherited from the same linear action on
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
, one by restriction and one by taking a derivative. The derivative of a linear map is that very same map, so the action on the multiflags and on line fields is inherited from the same action on
\mathbb{R}^{1,n+2}
. This implies
\Phi_{2}
, and therefore
\Phi
, are
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
equivariant.
\hfill\square

3.3.
Explicit Computation of
\Phi

We can calculate the map
\Phi:\ \$\Gamma_{n+1}\to L\text{Hor}^{n+2}
in a simple case, and describe the general case via
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
equivariance.

3.3.1.
The Base Case

Lemma 3.31
.

\Phi(1,0)
is the horosphere
H_{0}
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
with point at infinity along the direction
p_{0}=(1,1;0,...,0)_{\partial\mathbb{H}}
on
L^{+}
and which passes through
q_{0}=(1,0;0,...,0)_{\mathbb{H}}
. The
n
oriented line fields are parallel and mutually orthogonal, and at
q_{0}
point in the directions
\partial_{j}=(0,0;...,1,...,0)_{T\mathbb{H}}
where the only non-zero entry is for
X_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq n
.

Proof.
Considering the example in Section
2.8
, setting
\theta=0
we see that
\phi_{1}(1,0)=p_{0}
as expected. The associated horosphere is the manifold
H_{0}=\{x\in\mathbb{H}^{n+2}\ |\ (x|p_{0})_{1,n+2}=1\}
, and
q_{0}
satisfies this relation, so it lies on
H_{0}
. Since the family of horospheres with a given centre has one parameter for radius, this is enough to specify the horosphere.

We can also compute the flag vectors from the example in Section
2.8
:

(D_{\kappa}\phi_{1})(\check{\kappa}i_{j})=(0,0;0,...,1,...,0)_{T\mathbb{H}},

where the only non-zero entry is the
X_{j}
component. Therefore the
i_{j}
subflag is given by
[[p_{0},\partial_{j}]]
with induced orientation.

To find the line fields we intersect this flag with
H_{0}
. As proven in Proposition
3.18
the action of the parabolic subgroup on
H_{0}
is simple transitive, so the entire horosphere is parameterised by the image of
q_{0}
under the group action. Write
q_{\mathbf{V}}=P^{(1,0)}_{\mathbf{V}}.q_{0}
, and then
H_{0}=\{q_{\mathbf{V}}\ |\ \mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\}
. The action of
P^{(1,0)}_{\mathbf{V}}
on a point
p
in Minkowski (written as a matrix
S_{p}\in\mathcal{P}^{n+2}
) is:

\displaystyle P^{(1,0)}_{\mathbf{V}}.S_{p}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}T+Z+|\mathbf{V}|^{2}(T-Z)+\mathbf{X}\overline{\mathbf{V}}+\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{X}}&\mathbf{X}+(T-Z)\mathbf{V}\\
\overline{\mathbf{X}}+(T-Z)\overline{\mathbf{V}}&T-Z\end{pmatrix}.

This gives us the following transformed coordinates for
P_{\mathbf{V}}^{(1,0)}.S_{p}
:

\displaystyle T^{\prime}

\displaystyle=T+\frac{\mathbf{X}\overline{\mathbf{V}}+\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{X}}}{2}+\frac{|\mathbf{V}|^{2}(T-Z)}{2},

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}

\displaystyle=Z+\frac{\mathbf{X}\overline{\mathbf{V}}+\mathbf{V}\overline{\mathbf{X}}}{2}+\frac{|\mathbf{V}|^{2}(T-Z)}{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}+(T-Z)\mathbf{V},

which when applied to
S_{p}=S_{q_{0}}
gives
q_{\mathbf{V}}=\left(1+\frac{|\mathbf{V}|^{2}}{2},\frac{|\mathbf{V}|^{2}}{2};\mathbf{V}\right)_{\mathbb{H}}
. These
q_{\mathbf{V}}
are the points of the horosphere
\phi(1,0)
in the hyperboloid model. At the point
q_{\mathbf{V}}
the
i_{j}
oriented line field is given by the intersection of the tangent space of
H_{0}
at
q_{\mathbf{V}}
(which is
q_{\mathbf{V}}^{\perp}\cap p_{0}^{\perp}
) with the
i_{j}
subflag:

q_{\mathbf{V}}^{\perp}\cap[[p_{0},\partial_{j}]]\cap p_{0}^{\perp}=q_{\mathbf{V}}^{\perp}\cap[[p_{0},\partial_{j}]],

as the null flags live in the tangent space to
L^{+}
at
p_{0}
already. The points
x=(T_{x},Z_{x};\mathbf{X}_{x})_{T\mathbb{H}}=(T_{x},Z_{x};[X_{x}]_{0},[X_{x}]_{1},...,[X_{x}]_{n})_{T\mathbb{H}}\in q_{\mathbf{V}}^{\perp}\cap[[p_{0},\partial_{j}]]
lie in the hyperplane
q_{\mathbf{V}}^{\perp}
and so satisfy the equation

(3.32)

(x|q_{\mathbf{V}})_{1,n+2}=\left(1+\frac{|\mathbf{V}|^{2}}{2}\right)T_{x}-\frac{|\mathbf{V}|^{2}}{2}Z_{x}-\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{x}=0.

But they also lie in the
i_{j}
subflag, and so satisfy the pair of conditions

(3.33)

[X_{x}]_{0},[X_{x}]_{1},...,\widehat{[X_{x}]}_{j},...,[X_{x}]_{n}=0,

(3.34)

T_{x}=Z_{x},

where the hat denotes exclusion. Simply speaking, this says all the coordinates of the paravector
\mathbf{X}_{x}
are zero except the
i_{j}
coordinate, and the
T
and
Z
coordinates match.

Combining (
3.32
) and (
3.34
), the points
x=(T_{x},Z_{x};\mathbf{X}_{x})_{T\mathbb{H}}\in q^{\perp}_{\mathbf{V}}\cap[[p_{0},\partial_{j}]]
of the intersection satisfy

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{|\mathbf{V}|^{2}}{2}\right)T_{x}-\frac{|\mathbf{V}|^{2}}{2}T_{x}-\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{x}

which implies
T_{x}=\mathbf{V}\cdot\mathbf{X}_{x}
. By (
3.33
),
[X_{x}]_{j}
is the only non-zero term of
\mathbf{X}_{x}
and this reduces to
T_{x}=V_{j}[X_{x}]_{j}
. This gives an intersection parameterised by

(V_{j}[X_{x}]_{j},V_{j}[X_{x}]_{j};0,...,[X_{x}]_{j},...,0)_{T\mathbb{H}}=[X_{x}]_{j}V_{j}p_{0}+[X_{x}]_{j}\partial_{j},

and spanned by

(3.35)

(V_{j},V_{j};0,...,1,...,0)_{T\mathbb{H}}=V_{j}p_{0}+\partial_{j}.

In particular, as the point
q_{0}
has
\mathbf{V}=0
the oriented line field is directed there by
\partial_{j}
.

Finally, we show that the line fields are
parallel
; that is, taking the intersection of the flag at other points of the horosphere leads to the same oriented line as applying the parabolic translation taking
q_{0}
to that point. Let
\mathbf{W}=W_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}W_{j}i_{j}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and apply the parabolic transformation
P^{(1,0)}_{\mathbf{W}}
to the vector
V_{j}p_{0}+\partial_{j}
directing the line field:

\displaystyle P^{(1,0)}_{\mathbf{W}}.(V_{j}p_{0}+\partial_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}2V_{j}-2\text{Re}(i_{j}\mathbf{W})&i_{j}\\
-i_{j}&0\end{pmatrix},

where
-
Re
(i_{j}\mathbf{W})=W_{j}
. This gives the defining vector
((V_{j}+W_{j}),(V_{j}+W_{j});0,...,1,...,0)_{T\mathbb{H}}
for our oriented line field at the point
q_{\mathbf{V}+\mathbf{W}}
. This is precisely the defining vector (
3.35
) we get from intersecting directly at
q_{\mathbf{V}+\mathbf{W}}
, so the oriented line field is parallel as claimed.
\hfill\square

Here we see the first demonstration of these line fields being parallel! Though this is only an example, it will be proven be true in general in Corollary
3.38
. The line fields of this example are also orthogonal, which will be proven in the general case in Corollary
3.40
.

Proposition 3.36
.

\pi\circ\Phi(1,0)
is the horosphere in
\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
centred at infinity, and of Euclidean height 1. It bears
n
mutually orthogonal parallel oriented line fields, with the
i_{j}
field at
q_{0}=(1;0,...,0)_{\mathbb{U}}
pointing along the
x_{j}
coordinate.

Proof.
Recall that
\pi_{1}
,
\pi_{2}
are our maps between models of hyperbolic space from Section
3.2.1
. From Lemma
3.31
we have a description of
\Phi(1,0)
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
. Under the map
\pi^{\partial}
the centre
p_{0}
goes to
\infty\in\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
; the point
q_{0}
maps to
(0;0,...,0)_{\mathbb{B}}
in the ball model under
\pi_{1}
, but as this isn’t on the boundary we cannot simply feed it into
\pi_{2}^{\partial}
. Bypassing
\pi_{2}
, we instead describe this point as the intersection of the pair of geodesics
A=A(0;-1\to 0;1)
and
B=B(-1;0\to 1;0)
in
\mathbb{B}^{n+2}
.

Following the endpoints under
\pi_{2}^{\partial}
, the geodesic
A
maps to the geodesic
\pi_{2}^{\partial}A(-1\to 1)
, while
B
maps to the geodesic
\pi_{2}^{\partial}B(0\to\infty)
. Both of these geodesics are contained in the (
x_{0}
,
z
)-plane, and the intersection (which is on the horosphere) is at the point
(1;0)_{\mathbb{U}}
; as the horosphere is a plane parallel to the boundary
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, the Euclidean height is 1.

The line fields at
q_{0}
are given in Lemma
3.31
, directed by the vectors
\partial_{j}=(0,0;0,...,1,...,0)_{T\mathbb{H}}
, where the only non-zero entry is the
X_{j}
component for
1\leq j\leq n
. The maps
\pi_{1}
,
\pi_{2}
are isometries, which implies that the parallel property of the line fields passes from
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
to the other models. To find the direction, then, we only need to check the direction of the
i_{j}
fields at a single point.

We use the Jacobian matrix to map the tangent vector
\partial_{j}
to the ball model.

D\pi_{1}:T\mathbb{H}^{n+2}\to T\mathbb{B}^{n+2},\ D\pi_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{Z}{(1+T)^{2}}&\frac{1}{1+T}&0&...\\
-\frac{X_{0}}{(1+T)^{2}}&0&\frac{1}{1+T}&...\\
...&...&...&...\\
-\frac{X_{n}}{(1+T)^{2}}&0&...&\frac{1}{1+T}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

The only non-zero entries in row
i
are the first column and the
(i+1)
th column. Applying this to our
\partial_{j}
vectors we find

D\pi_{1}(\partial_{j})=\left(0;0,...,\frac{1}{1+T},...,0\right)_{T\mathbb{B}},

where the only non-zero entry is in the
y_{j}
column. As
T>0
this defines the same oriented line field as
\partial^{\mathbb{B}}_{j}=(0;0,...,1,...,0)_{T\mathbb{B}}
, so we rescale and take that to be our representative instead.

Next, we compute the Jacobian of
\pi_{2}
using the component form of Proposition
3.26
. Not all of the entries of
D\pi_{2}
are needed to find the action on the
\partial_{j}^{\mathbb{B}}
; we can ignore derivatives with respect to
w
and
y_{0}
. We compute the other partial derivatives:

\frac{\partial(\pi_{2}(W))_{z}}{\partial y_{j}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}\left(\frac{1-\left(\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}y_{\ell}^{2}+w^{2}\right)}{\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}y_{\ell}^{2}+(w-1)^{2}}\right)=\frac{4y_{j}\left(w-1\right)}{\left(\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}y_{\ell}^{2}+(w-1)^{2}\right)^{2}}.

For
j\neq k
,

\frac{\partial(\pi_{2}(W))_{x_{k}}}{\partial y_{j}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}\left(\frac{2y_{k}}{\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}y_{\ell}^{2}+(w-1)^{2}}\right)=-\frac{4y_{k}y_{j}}{\left(\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}y_{\ell}^{2}+(w-1)^{2}\right)^{2}}.

If instead we have
k=j
, we find:

\frac{\partial(\pi_{2}(W))_{x_{j}}}{\partial y_{j}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}\left(\frac{2y_{j}}{\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}y_{\ell}^{2}+(w-1)^{2}}\right)=\frac{2(\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}y_{\ell}^{2}-2y_{j}^{2}+(w-1)^{2})}{\left(\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}y_{\ell}^{2}+(w-1)^{2}\right)^{2}}.

As mentioned above we only need to evaluate these at one point, and the parallel property will define their direction elsewhere. We evaluate the vector
D\pi_{2}(\partial_{j})
at the point
(0;0,0,...,0)_{\mathbb{B}}
.

D\pi_{2}(\partial_{j})|_{(0;0)_{\mathbb{B}}}=(0;0,...,0,2,0,...,0)_{T\mathbb{U}},

where the only non-zero entry is the
x_{j}
component. The
i_{j}
oriented line field thus points in the
x_{j}
direction as claimed.

\hfill\square

3.3.2.
The General Case

The horosphere of Proposition
3.36
is an
(n+1)
-plane; more broadly, the horospheres centred at infinity are simply
(n+1)
-planes parallel to the boundary copy of
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
in the upper half-space. But horospheres centred elsewhere appear as spheres tangent to their centres on
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, which makes it a little more difficult to describe the line fields of the correspondence. We make a standard choice, the
North Pole specification
, which considers the line fields only in the tangent space to the horosphere at the point of maximal
z
value in
\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
(this point is the
north pole
, the point furthest from the boundary
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
in the Euclidean metric on
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
).

So we can describe isometries on line fields by Möbius transformations on the boundary at infinity, we associate to a given line field a tangent geodesic which is similarly oriented. Then, as the endpoints of the geodesic change, we can reconstruct how the geodesic (and thus the tangent oriented line) changes as well, as conformality ensures the tangencies are preserved.

Lemma 3.37
.

Consider a horosphere
H
in
\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
centred at
\infty
or
0
, with a parallel oriented line field specified (respectively, North Pole specified) by the unit paravector
D
. The line field transforms as follows under the generators of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
:

•

A_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbf{V}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
leaves
D
unaffected.

•

A_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
sends
D
to
-\overline{\textbf{D}}
, up to scaling.
32
32

32

Recall line fields are invariant under rescaling the specification vector.

•

A_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{*-1}\end{pmatrix},\ a\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
sends
D
to
a\textbf{D}a^{*}
.

Proof.
Let the Euclidean diameter (or equivalently the height) of
H
be
h
. If
H
is centred at
0
, the circular geodesic segment
\gamma(-h\textbf{D}\to h\textbf{D})
is tangent to the north pole specification vector; if centred at
\infty
, this geodesic will also be tangent to the oriented line field of
H
at
(1;0)_{\mathbb{U}}
. The orientation of the line field also agrees with the orientation of the geodesic.

The direction of this geodesic can be specified by the ‘vector’ (really just an ordered pair of points on the boundary)
\mathbf{X}
pointing from one endpoint of
\gamma
to another. Alternately, it can be thought of as the projection onto the boundary
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
of the geodesic. We write it as a vector in
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
:

\mathbf{X}=h\textbf{D}-(-h\textbf{D})=2h\textbf{D}.

Note that this is a vector in the copy of
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\subset\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
, and not a tangent vector!

The parabolic transformation
A_{1}
sends the endpoints
\pm h\textbf{D}
to
\pm h\textbf{D}+\mathbf{V}
. The action on
\mathbf{X}
is then trivial, as it affects the endpoints equally:

A_{1}.\mathbf{X}=h\textbf{D}+\mathbf{V}-(-h\textbf{D}+\mathbf{V})=2h\textbf{D}=\mathbf{X}.

Inversion (
A_{2}
) sends the endpoints
\pm h\textbf{D}
to
-\left(\pm h\textbf{D}\right)^{-1}=-(\pm h\overline{\textbf{D}})\left|\pm h\right|^{-2}
. Taking the difference and rescaling, the line segment points in the direction
-\overline{\textbf{D}}
. Finally,
A_{3}
sends the endpoints
\pm h\textbf{D}
to
\pm a(h\textbf{D})a^{*}
, and the directed line segment between them points along the paravector
a\textbf{D}a^{*}
, as required.
\hfill\square

Corollary 3.38
.

The
n
oriented line fields of
\Phi(\kappa)
are parallel.

Proof.
From Lemma
3.31
, the line fields are parallel in the case of
\kappa=(1,0)
. The action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
is equivariant through
\Phi_{2}
by Theorem
3.30
, and Lemma
3.37
tells us the action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
on the line fields of
L
Hor
n+2
is via isometries, which preserve parallel lines.
\hfill\square

With these lemmas in place, we can use the action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
to find the relationship between arbitrary Lipschitz spinors and horospheres bearing line fields.

Theorem 3.39
.

The spinor
\kappa=(\xi,\eta)\in S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
maps under
\Phi
to a horosphere decorated with
n
parallel oriented line fields, centred at
\xi\eta^{-1}
in the upper half-space model (taken to be
\infty
if
\eta=0
).

•

If
\eta\neq 0
the horosphere has Euclidean diameter
|\eta|^{-2}
, and the north pole specification for the
i_{j}
direction field is
\eta^{{}^{\prime}}i_{j}\overline{\eta}
.

•

If
\eta=0
then the horosphere has Euclidean height
|\xi|^{2}
and the
i_{j}
-direction field is specified by
\xi i_{j}\xi^{*}
.

Proof.
The map
\phi_{1}
sends
\kappa
to a point
(|\xi|^{2}+|\eta|^{2},|\xi|^{2}-|\eta|^{2};2\xi\overline{\eta})_{\mathbb{R}}=(T,Z;\mathbf{X})_{\mathbb{R}}
on the light-cone.
Expand the paravector component as
\xi\overline{\eta}=[\xi\overline{\eta}]_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}[\xi\overline{\eta}]_{j}i_{j}
. We map the ideal boundary of the hyperboloid first into the ball model:

\pi_{1}^{\partial}(|\xi|^{2}+|\eta|^{2},|\xi|^{2}-|\eta|^{2};2[\xi\overline{\eta}]_{0},2[\xi\overline{\eta}]_{1},...,2[\xi\overline{\eta}]_{n})_{\partial\mathbb{H}}=\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2}+|\eta|^{2}}(|\xi|^{2}-|\eta|^{2};2[\xi\overline{\eta}]_{0},2[\xi\overline{\eta}]_{1},...,2[\xi\overline{\eta}]_{n})_{\partial\mathbb{B}},

and then to the upper half-space model:

\displaystyle\pi_{2}^{\partial}\left[\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2}+|\eta|^{2}}(|\xi|^{2}-|\eta|^{2};2[\xi\overline{\eta}]_{0},2[\xi\overline{\eta}]_{1},...,2[\xi\overline{\eta}]_{n})_{\partial\mathbb{B}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\frac{|\xi|^{2}-|\eta|^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}+|\eta|^{2}}}\frac{2\xi\overline{\eta}}{\left(|\xi|^{2}+|\eta|^{2}\right)}=\xi\eta^{-1}.

To compute the Euclidean diameter (or height), first consider the case where
\eta
=0. Starting at
(1,0)
we apply the transformation

G_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&0\\
0&\xi^{*-1}\end{pmatrix},

to get all Lipschitz spinors of the form
G_{1}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix}^{T}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi&0\end{pmatrix}^{T}
. This transforms the geodesic
\pi_{2}^{\partial}A(-1\to 1)
of Proposition
3.36
into
G_{1}\pi_{2}^{\partial}A(-\xi\xi^{*}\to\xi\xi^{*})
, and the geodesic
\pi_{2}^{\partial}B(\infty\to 0)
goes to
G_{1}\pi_{2}^{\partial}B(\infty\to 0)
, so the intersection of these geodesics will now be at the midpoint of the Euclidean semicircle
G_{1}\pi_{2}^{\partial}A
, and thus a full Euclidean radius away from the boundary. The radius is
|\xi\xi^{*}|=|\xi||\xi^{*}|=|\xi|^{2}
, which is the Euclidean height of the horosphere as all points of the plane are at the same height. Lemma
3.37
then implies the action of
G_{1}
sends the specifying vectors
i_{j}
to
\xi i_{j}\xi^{*}
.

If
\eta\neq 0
, start by applying the inversion

G_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},

which sends
(1,0)\to(0,1)
and sends our geodesics to
G_{2}\pi_{2}^{\partial}A(1\to-1),\ G_{2}\pi_{2}^{\partial}B(0\to\infty)
. That is, the orientations are reversed but the geodesics are otherwise invariant. We then apply the transformation

G_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}\eta^{*-1}&0\\
0&\eta\end{pmatrix},

which sends
(0,1)\to(0,\eta)
. This transforms the geodesics as follows:

\displaystyle G_{2}\pi_{2}^{\partial}A

\displaystyle\to G_{3}G_{2}\pi_{2}^{\partial}A(\eta^{*-1}\eta^{-1}\to-\eta^{*-1}\eta^{-1}),

\displaystyle G_{2}\pi_{2}^{\partial}B

\displaystyle\to G_{3}G_{2}\pi_{2}^{\partial}B(0\to\infty),

and by the same logic as above, the intersection is at Euclidean height
|\eta^{*-1}\eta^{-1}|=|\eta|^{-2}
. As this geodesic goes from the centre of the horosphere to infinity it passes through the north pole, and so this gives the Euclidean diameter.

Finally, we apply the transformation

G_{4}=\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbf{V}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

where
\xi\eta^{-1}=\mathbf{V}
. This sends
(0,\eta)\to(\xi,\eta)
and just translates the geodesics equally, which (by Lemma
3.37
) doesn’t change the point of intersection:

\displaystyle G_{3}G_{2}\pi_{2}^{\partial}A

\displaystyle\to G_{4}G_{3}G_{2}\pi_{2}^{\partial}A(\eta^{*-1}\eta^{-1}+\xi\to-\eta^{*-1}\eta^{-1}+\xi),

\displaystyle G_{3}G_{2}\pi_{2}^{\partial}B

\displaystyle\to G_{4}G_{3}G_{2}\pi_{2}^{\partial}B(\xi\to\infty).

The Euclidean diameter of
\phi(\xi,\eta)
is thus
|\eta|^{-2}
.

Consider acting with these transformations on the oriented line fields directed by the
i_{j}
; the inversion
G_{2}
sends
i_{j}\to-\overline{i_{j}}=i_{j}
, the homothety and rotation
G_{3}
sends
i_{j}\to\eta^{*-1}i_{j}\eta^{-1}
, and
G_{4}
leaves the directing vectors invariant. The final line field is north pole specified by
\eta^{*-1}i_{j}\eta^{-1}
, which we can rescale to the slightly simpler form
\eta^{\prime}i_{j}\overline{\eta}
.
\hfill\square

This theorem implies the line fields come from taking the orthonormal frame for
\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
given by the
i_{j}
, and applying an isometry (plus rescaling)
\sigma(\xi)
or
\sigma(\eta^{\prime})
to the whole frame.

Corollary 3.40
.

The
n
line fields of
\Phi(\xi,\eta)
are mutually orthogonal.

Proof.
From Theorem
3.39
we know the line fields are specified either by
\sigma(\xi)(i_{j})
for every
i_{j}
, or by
\sigma(\eta^{\prime})(i_{j})
for every
i_{j}
. But
\sigma(x)
is a conformal transformation of
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
for every
x\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
(namely, an isometry plus a homothety). Since the
i_{j}
are mutually orthogonal in
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, they remain orthogonal after applying an isometry and a homothety.
\hfill\square

We can now properly restrict the map
\Phi_{2}
to the appropriate codomain.

Definition 3.41
.

An ordered set of
n
oriented mutually orthogonal parallel line fields
(L_{1},...,L_{n})
on a horosphere is termed a
decoration
; the line field
L_{j}
may also be referred to as the
i_{j}

decoration
. A horosphere in
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
bearing a decoration is termed a
decorated horosphere
. Denote the set of decorated horospheres by
D
Hor
n+2
.

\lrcorner

The set of decorated horospheres can be given a manifold structure; in fact, a fibre bundle structure. The space of horospheres Hor
n+2
can be identified with
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times S^{n+1}
, as we can smoothly vary the centre across the boundary of
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
and then pick a ‘radius’ to capture a unique horosphere. A decoration can be equated with the orthonormal frame in the tangent space to the horosphere whose basis vectors point along the parallel line fields (and from any orthonormal frame we can recover a unique decoration), and so we can identify
D
Hor
n+2
with the orthonormal frame bundle over Hor
n+2
.

Because the case of
n=0
is somewhat unique, we define a special decoration to allow a continuous rotation between real spin decorations (defined in the next section).
33
33

33

See
[
22
]
Section 2.1 for more details.

Definition 3.42
.

The points
(z;x_{0},0)_{\mathbb{U}}
of
\mathbb{U}^{3}
can be identified with
\mathbb{U}^{2}
; the boundary at infinity of
\mathbb{U}^{2}
is then given by
\partial\mathbb{U}^{2}=\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}\subset\mathbb{C}\cup\{\infty\}=\partial\mathbb{U}^{3}
. By intersecting
\mathbb{U}^{3}
with
\mathbb{U}^{2}
, we may regard horocycles
H
of
\mathbb{U}^{2}
as arising from horospheres
\widetilde{H}
of
\mathbb{U}^{3}
centred at
\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Given a horocycle
H
in
\mathbb{U}^{2}\subset\mathbb{U}^{3}
, a
planar decoration
on
H
is a decoration on
\widetilde{H}
(north pole) specified by the vector
i_{1}=i
.

\lrcorner

A spinor
(\xi,\eta)
describes a planar decoration on a horocycle if and only if
\xi
and
\eta
are both real; see
[
22
]
, towards the end of Section 2.1.

Theorem 3.43
.

\Phi_{2}
is a diffeomorphism from
\mathcal{MF}^{n}
to
D
Hor
n+2
.

Proof.
First, we note that
\phi_{2}
is a bijection (in fact, a diffeomorphism). Every point
p\in L^{+}
will generate a different hyperplane (implying the map is injective), and both
L^{+}
and Hor
n+2
are topologically
S^{n+1}\times\mathbb{R}
. Smoothly varying
p
leads to smoothly varying hyperplanes, and thus smoothly varying horospheres.

From Corollary
3.40
, the image
\Phi_{2}(\kappa)
for a Lipschitz spinor
\kappa
with
\phi_{1}(\kappa)=p
has orthogonal line fields, and by Corollary
3.38
they are also parallel. So the image of
\Phi_{2}
does lie within
D
Hor
n+2
. At a point
p\in L^{+}
, the set of multiflags is diffeomorphic to
SO(n+1)
. The set of decorations on the horosphere
\phi_{2}(p)
is also diffeomorphic to
SO(n+1)
, and the set of multiflags based at
p
corresponds bijectively to the set of decorations on
\phi_{2}(p)
(as both are expressions of an orthonormal basis in terms of the same family of vectors), so
\Phi_{2}
is a bijection. As the flags vary smoothly, their intersections with the horosphere will also vary smoothly, so the map is a diffeomorphism.

\hfill\square

4.
Spin decorations, Lambda Lengths, and Ptolemy Relations

We turn now to the issue of spin, and lift our various
SO(n)
structures to
Spin(n)
structures. This will turn our various maps from double covers into genuine homeomorphisms and equivalences, as well as setting us up nicely to apply spinor algebra to our hyperbolic objects. The fruit of this will be a generalisation of Penner’s lambda lengths to arbitrary dimensions, and a non-commutative generalisation of the hyperbolic Ptolemy relation in arbitrary dimension.

In this section,
\mathbb{H}^{n}
refers to
n
-dimensional hyperbolic space regardless of model. Assume
\mathbb{H}^{n}
is oriented.

4.1.
Frame Fields and Double Covers

A horosphere
H\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
has two choices of normal direction; following
[
23
]
, call the normal pointing towards the centre of the horosphere the
outward
normal, and the one pointing away the
inward
normal. These form unit normal vector fields
N^{out}
and
N^{in}
on the horosphere, with vectors based at different points related by parallel transport.

A
frame
is shorthand for a positively oriented orthonormal frame at a point
p
in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
; that is, an ordered tuple of
n
orthonormal vectors in
T_{p}\mathbb{H}^{n}
which is positively oriented. In
[
23
]
this orientation was defined in terms of the standard cross product on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, but in arbitrary dimension defining an orientation takes more work. A common way to define orientations is via the Hodge star operator.

Definition 4.1

(Hodge Star for
k
-Vectors)
.

Let
(X,g)
be an oriented,
m
-dimensional smooth manifold
X
with a (pseudo)-Riemannian metric
g
, and let
p\in X
. For
0\leq k\leq m
write
\bigwedge^{k}T_{p}X
for the space of
k
-vectors in the tangent space
T_{p}X
. Let
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
be the bilinear form on
\bigwedge^{k}T_{p}X
induced by
g
, defined on simple
k
-vectors
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\wedge...\wedge\alpha_{k}
,
\beta=\beta_{1}\wedge...\wedge\beta_{k}
by the
Gram determinant

(\alpha,\beta)_{g}=\text{det}\left(g(\alpha_{\ell},\beta_{j})\right)
, and extended to the rest of
\bigwedge^{k}T_{p}X
by linearity. The
Hodge star operator
is the unique linear function

\star:\bigwedge^{k}T_{p}X\to\bigwedge^{m-k}T_{p}X

defined by the identity
\alpha\wedge\star\beta=(\alpha,\beta)_{g}\omega
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\bigwedge^{k}T_{p}X
, where
\omega
is the unit
m
-vector defined locally by
\omega=e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge...\wedge e_{m}
, for an oriented orthonormal basis
\{e_{1},e_{2},...,e_{m}\}
of
T_{p}X
.

\lrcorner

A
positively oriented orthonormal frame
(or just
frame
) at a point
p\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
then consists of
n
orthonormal (under the metric induced by
(\cdot|\cdot)_{1,n}
) vectors
(v_{1},...,v_{n})\in T_{p}\mathbb{H}^{n}
such that
\star(v_{1}\wedge v_{2}...\wedge v_{n-1})=v_{n}
. Because the vectors are orthogonal, the wedge and Clifford products are equivalent and this condition can equivalently be written as
\star(v_{1}v_{2}...v_{n-1})=v_{n}
.

We’ve established that the decorations of the spinor-horosphere correspondence are orthogonal, so they can be equated with a frame. In the Euclidean geometry of the horosphere, vector fields define oriented line fields and oriented line fields define unit vector fields, so we will use the two somewhat interchangeably.

The collection of frames forms a principal
SO(n+2)
bundle over
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
, denoted
\text{Fr}^{n+2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
. However, from Proposition
2.9
we know we should really be looking at spin groups, rather than special orthogonal groups. We denote the associated double cover of Fr
n+2
(which will be a principal
Spin(n+2)
bundle) by

S\text{Fr}^{n+2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
. Points of this double cover are called
spin frames
, and each point in Fr
n+2
has two lifts in
S\text{Fr}^{n+2}
.

How do these frames relate to decorated horospheres?

Definition 4.2

(Horosphere Frame Fields)
.

The outwards frame field of a decorated horosphere
H\subset D\text{Hor}^{n+2}
with unit normals
N^{out}
and
N^{in}
and decorations specified by unit vector fields
\widehat{D}_{1},\widehat{D}_{2},...,\widehat{D}_{n}
is
34
34

34

This is chosen to agree with the convention in
[
23
]
, Definition 4.1. It is slightly different to the convention in
[
21
]
Definition 6.1.1, as there the normal and the ‘real paravector 1’ generators are swapped in the tuple.

F^{out}=\left(N^{out},\widehat{D}_{1},\widehat{D}_{2},...,\widehat{D}_{n},\star(N^{out}\wedge\widehat{D}_{1}\wedge\widehat{D}_{2}\wedge...\wedge\widehat{D}_{n})\right).

Similarly, the inward frame field is given by

F^{in}=\left(N^{in},\widehat{D}_{1},\widehat{D}_{2},...,\widehat{D}_{n},\star(N^{in}\wedge\widehat{D}_{1}\wedge\widehat{D}_{2}\wedge...\wedge\widehat{D}_{n})\right).

For expediency’s sake, we label this final Hodge star component by
1^{out}
and
1^{in}
, respectively. The
frame field
of the decorated horosphere is the pair
(F^{out},F^{in})
.

\lrcorner

Any frame
F
based at a point
p_{F}
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
(that is, any point of Fr
n+2
) will uniquely generate a frame field in a horosphere as follows: call the first component of
F
,
N^{out}
. Choose the horosphere
H_{F}
which passes through
p_{F}
and is centred at the point of
\partial\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
where the oriented line determined by
N^{out}
intersects the boundary, in the direction
N^{out}
points along. Then parallel transport of
F
across the horosphere will generate a frame field, which will be precisely the
F^{out}
of the horosphere
H_{F}
. This gives a surjective map Fr
{}^{n+2}\to D\text{Hor}^{n+2}
, though the map is not injective as all parallel transports across the horosphere
H_{F}
are different points of Fr
n+2
that generate the same decorated horosphere.

4.1.1.
Bijections and Covering Spaces

We know (see Ahlfors
[
1
]
) that the group of orientation-preserving isometries of
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
is isomorphic to
PSL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
, the quotient
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})/\{I,-I\}
identifying elements of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
with their negative. The action of
PSL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
can be extended to frames through derivatives, and because hyperbolic space is homogeneous and isotropic this action is simply transitive on the frame bundle Fr
n+2
. This implies Fr
n+2
is a
PSL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
-torsor.
35
35

35

Roughly, a
G
-torsor is a set acted on by
G
that can be identified with
G
as a group after making an arbitrary choice of identity.

Proposition 4.3
.

\text{Fr}^{n+2}
is a
PSL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
-torsor.

Proof.
We can obtain an explicit identification by choosing a base-point
F_{0}\in\text{Fr}^{n+2}
and making the association
M\to M.F_{0}
for
M\in PSL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
. This turns Fr
n+2
into a group; because the action is simply transitive, the identification is bijective, and because it is obviously a homomorphism we have an isomorphism of groups.
\hfill\square

We can lift this map to an isomorphism
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})\cong S\text{Fr}^{n+2}
, choosing a lifted base-point
\tilde{F}_{0}\in S\text{Fr}^{n+2}
and assigning to
M\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
the image
M.\tilde{F}_{0}
of the basepoint
\tilde{F}_{0}
under the group action.

Theorem 4.4
.

The map
\Phi:S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to D\text{Hor}^{n+2}
is a smooth double cover.

Proof.
In Theorem
2.39
it was shown that
\Phi_{1}
is a smooth double cover, and in Theorem
3.43
it was shown
\Phi_{2}
is a diffeomorphism. Taken together, we see
\Phi_{2}\circ\Phi_{1}=\Phi
is also a smooth double cover.
\hfill\square

Remark
.

We have used the equivariance of
\Phi
to extend calculations from
\Phi(1,0)
to other decorated horospheres; but does this allow us to describe all of
D\text{Hor}^{n+2}
? In fact, it does; we can extend the transitive action on
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
(see Lemma
1.34
) through to
D\text{Hor}^{n+2}
, because transitivity of a group action
G
passes through
G
equivariant surjective maps; and, if the action is transitive on
D
Hor
n+2
, then we can reach every decorated horosphere from
\Phi(1,0)
. This also lifts to transitivity on
S
Hor
n+2
under the equivariance of
\tilde{\Phi}
, terms which which will be defined shortly.

4.2.
Lifting to Double Covers

Now we lift everything to the universal cover. Instead of discussing frames and decorations, we now consider spin frames and spin decorations.

Definition 4.5
.

Consider a horosphere
H\subset\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
. An outward (inward) spin frame on
H
is a continuous lift of an outward (inward) frame on
H
from Fr
n+2
to
S\text{Fr}^{n+2}
.

•

If
W^{out}
is an outward spin frame on
H
lifting an outward frame field, the associated inward spin frame is obtained by rotating
W^{out}
by an angle of
\pi
about the oriented geodesic
n
-plane (with orientation chosen by
N^{out}
) spanned by the decorations. More concretely, it is obtained by rotating in the plane spanned by
N^{out}
and
1^{out}
by
\pi
, from
N^{out}
towards
1^{out}
.

•

If
W^{in}
is an inward spin frame on
H
lifting an inward frame field, the associated outward spin frame is obtained by rotating
W^{in}
by an angle
-\pi
about the oriented geodesic
n
-plane (with orientation chosen by
N^{out}
) spanned by the decorations. Precisely, it is obtained by rotating in the plane spanned by
N^{in}
and
1^{in}
by
\pi
, from
1^{in}
towards
N^{in}
.

\lrcorner

The choice of ‘associated’ spin frame is somewhat arbitrary, but some consistent choice needs to be made. An outward spin frame
W^{out}
now has an associated inward spin frame
W^{in}
, and the inward frame is associated to
W^{out}
as well; they form a pair.

Definition 4.6

(Spin Decorations)
.

A
spin decorated horosphere
is a decorated horosphere
H
equipped with a pair
W=(W^{in},W^{out})
of an inward and outward spin frame lifting decorations on
H
, each associated to the other. The
i_{j}

spin decoration
is then the spin lift of the
i_{j}
decoration. The space of spin decorated horospheres
(H,W)
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
is denoted
S
Hor
n+2
.

\lrcorner

4.2.1.
Null Flag Bundles

The flag bundle
\mathcal{MF}^{n}
is also insensitive to the spinorial nature of
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
. We can resolve this by considering a map into the double cover
\widetilde{\mathcal{MF}}^{n}
; choosing an arbitrary lift of
\Phi_{1}(1,0)
as a base-point in
\widetilde{\mathcal{MF}}^{n}
, we obtain a lifted map

\tilde{\Phi}_{1}:S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{MF}}^{n}.

We denote points of this new space
spin multiflags
. To differentiate our flags
[[p,v_{j}]]
from their lifted counterparts we write
\widetilde{[[p,v_{j}]]}
for the lifted flags. We also define a lifted map
\tilde{\Phi}_{2}:\widetilde{\mathcal{MF}}^{n}\to S\text{Hor}^{n+2}
, again choosing an arbitrary lift of
\Phi(1,0)
as a base-point such that Figure
2
commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\$\Gamma_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{MF}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\text{Hor}^{n+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{MF}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D\text{Hor}^{n+2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Phi}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{2}}

Figure 2.
The various lifts of spaces and maps.

With these lifted functions, we properly have a diffeomorphism
\tilde{\Phi}=\tilde{\Phi}_{2}\circ\tilde{\Phi}_{1}:S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to S\text{Hor}^{n+2}
. Putting together these various correspondences gives us the full Lipschitz spinor-horosphere correspondence.

Theorem 4.7
.

There is an explicit, smooth, bijective,
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
equivariant correspondence between the following:

i)

Lipschitz spinors
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}
,

ii)

spin multiflags
\widetilde{\mathcal{MF}}^{n}
,

iii)

The space
S
Hor
n+2
of spin decorated horospheres in
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
.

Proof.
We prove ii), iii) are equivalent to i).

i)
\Leftrightarrow
ii):
\Phi_{1}
is a smooth double cover (as shown in Theorem
2.39
), which implies it lifts to a diffeomorphism. It is
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
equivariant by Theorem
2.37
, which also lifts to the equivariant map
\tilde{\Phi}_{1}
.

i)
\Leftrightarrow
iii): Theorem
4.4
tells us
\Phi
is a smooth double cover of
D\text{Hor}^{n+2}
, which lifts to a diffeomorphism
S\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to S\text{Hor}^{n+2}
. It is made explicit (up to spin) by Theorem
3.39
, and
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
equivariant by lifting the equivariance of Theorem
3.30
.

\hfill\square

4.2.2.
The Case of 2 Dimensions

Spin decorations in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
are an outlier, because the space of positively oriented orthonormal frames based at a point on a horocycle consists of a single point. The spin ‘double cover’ is then two points and is thus disconnected, which means there is no path from one spin frame to another. We can remedy this by embedding
\mathbb{U}^{2}\subset\mathbb{U}^{3}
, as was set up in Definition
3.42
.

Definition 4.8

(Planar Spin Decorations)
.

Embed
\mathbb{U}^{2}\subset\mathbb{U}^{3}
as in Definition
3.42
. Given a horocycle
H
in
\mathbb{U}^{2}\subset\mathbb{U}^{3}
, a
planar spin decoration
on
H
is a lift of a planar decoration on
H
to the spin double cover. Each horocycle
H
of
\mathbb{U}^{2}
has two planar spin decorations, related by a
2\pi
rotation.

\lrcorner

We can then define frames and spin frames as usual by considering these planar spin decorations as a subset of the spin decorations on
\mathbb{U}^{3}
.

4.3.
Lambda Lengths

In
[
23
]
, complex lambda lengths are defined geometrically and shown to have a nice algebraic interpretation as a symplectic form on the space of complex spinors. This relationship can be generalised; but while we already have a natural generalisation of the symplectic form (the bracket defined in Section
1.5.3
), we need to define the appropriate notion of ‘higher dimensional lambda length’ between decorated horospheres.

Informally, the appropriate geometric picture of a lambda length (or
\lambda
) is given by pushing the inward (resp. outward) spin frame on one horosphere to the outward (resp. inward) spin frame on the other, parallel transporting along the geodesic between their centres. Then, when the frames are based at the same point, there will be some rotation in the (now identified) tangent spaces of the horospheres that aligns the spin decorations. The lambda length is an element of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
representing this combined translation and rotation.

Definition 4.9
.

We define two (closely related) actions of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
on spin decorations, with the difference being essentially spinorial. Because
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
is defined to act on
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, using it to act on the tangent spaces of horospheres requires an identification of the tangent space with
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. In one instance we make this identification using the spin frame
W^{in}
from the spin decoration
W
, choosing
i_{j}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
to point along the
i_{j}
component of
W
, and
1\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
to point along
1^{in}
. In the other, we identify
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
along the components of
W^{out}
.

For
a\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
, the
inwards action
on the spin decorated horosphere
(H,W)
sends it to
a^{in}.(H,W)=(H,a^{in}.W)
. The spin decoration
a^{in}.W=(a^{in}.W^{in},a^{in}.W^{out})
is the result of acting on
W^{in}
and
W^{out}
with
\tilde{\sigma}\left(a/|a|\right)
under the identification of the tangent space along
W^{in}
, where
\tilde{\sigma}(\cdot)
is the lift of
\sigma(\cdot)
from a map
\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to SO(n+1)
to a map
\$\Gamma_{n+1}\to Spin(n+1)
. The
outwards action
is almost the same, but instead identifies the tangent space with
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
along
W^{out}
.

\lrcorner

Definition 4.10

(Lambda Length)
.

Consider a pair of spin decorated horospheres
(H_{1},W_{1})
and
(H_{2},W_{2})
. If the centres of
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
are distinct, the lambda length
\lambda_{12}
from
H_{1}
to
H_{2}
is defined to be the element of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
with magnitude
e^{d/2}
, where
d
is the oriented hyperbolic distance from
H_{1}
to
H_{2}
along their common perpendicular geodesic, and whose inwards action on spin decorations sends
W_{1}^{in}
to
\tilde{\sigma}(\lambda_{12})(W_{1}^{in})=W_{2}^{out}
after parallel transporting
W_{1}
along the common perpendicular to be based at the same point as
W_{2}
, carrying the inwards identification along with it. If
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
share a centre, then
\lambda_{12}=0
.

\lrcorner

Although this is a somewhat intuitive geometric description, it isn’t very explicit. For computations, the following characterisation proves more useful.

Proposition 4.11
.

The lambda length
\lambda_{12}
between two spin decorated horospheres
(H_{1},W_{1})
and
(H_{2},W_{2})
can be written explicitly in the form

\lambda_{12}=e^{d/2}e^{\theta_{k}V_{k}/2}...e^{\theta_{2}V_{2}/2}e^{\theta_{1}V_{1}/2},

where the simple rotations
e^{\theta_{j}V_{j}/2}
are applied right to left. Here,
d
is the signed hyperbolic distance from
H_{1}
to
H_{2}
along the geodesic between their centres (as in the definition above), the
\theta_{j}
are angles in
[0,4\pi)
, and the
V_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
are unit vectors; the
e^{\theta_{j}V_{j}/2}
correspond to an ordered series of rotations by
\theta_{j}
within the
2
-planes spanned by
\{\mathbb{R},V_{1}\},\{\mathbb{R},V_{2}\},...,\{\mathbb{R},V_{k}\}
, from
1
towards
V_{j}
, under the identification of
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with the tangent space using the spin frame
W^{in}
. These rotations compose to the action
\tilde{\sigma}(\lambda_{12})
sending
W_{1}^{in}
to
W_{2}^{out}
.

Proof.

e^{d/2}
is the magnitude of
\lambda_{12}
by definition. The action on
W_{1}^{in}
is by
\tilde{\sigma}(\lambda_{12})
, but by Corollary
1.21
, the action of any
\sigma(\alpha)
for
\alpha\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
can be generated by the product of simple rotations in the 2-planes spanned by
\{\mathbb{R},V_{j}\}
for some
V_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
. This statement lifts, so any
\tilde{\sigma}(\alpha)
can be generated by the product of the lifts of simple rotations.
\hfill\square

Before moving on, a quick lemma.

Lemma 4.12
.

The only paravectors
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
satisfying
\mathbf{V}^{2}=-1
are the subset of unit vectors; that is, the
V\in\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\subset\$\Gamma_{n+1}
satisfying
|V|=1
.

Proof.
Consider expanding
\mathbf{V}^{2}
for a generic paravector
\mathbf{V}=V_{0}+V
(where
V
is the vector component):

\left(V_{0}+V\right)\left(V_{0}+V\right)=V^{2}+2V_{0}V+V_{0}^{2}=-1.

If
V_{0}V
is non-zero the left-hand side has complex components, so this cross-term must be zero. This implies
V_{0}=0
, or
V=0
; but if
V=0
then
V_{0}^{2}=-1
, which is not satisfied for any real
V_{0}
. We conclude
V_{0}=0
and therefore
\mathbf{V}^{2}=V^{2}=-1
, and as
|V|^{2}=-V^{2}
we see
|V|=1
.
\hfill\square

4.3.1.
Computing the Lambda Length

The spin nature of the lambda length can make it difficult to handle signs; to resolve this we compute a simple example, and extend that case to the general one.

Lemma 4.13
.

The lambda length
\lambda_{12}
from
\tilde{\Phi}(1,0)
to
\tilde{\Phi}(0,1)
is
1
.

Proof.
