We give the horospheres and spin decorations names:

\displaystyle\tilde{\Phi}(1,0)

\displaystyle=(H_{1},W_{1}),\ \tilde{\Phi}(0,1)=(H_{2},W_{2}).

Both horospheres go through the point
(1;0)_{\mathbb{U}}
(which looks, in the upper half-space, like a point at Euclidean distance 1 above the origin of the boundary), and the two spin decorations project to the same orthonormal frame
(i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{n})
on
\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
. This implies the spin decorations either align, or differ by a
2\pi
rotation; if they are aligned then
\lambda_{12}=1
, and if they differ by a
2\pi
rotation
\lambda_{12}=-1
.

Consider a smooth path of horospheres from
(1,0)
to
(0,1)
given by
(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)
for
\theta\in[0,\pi/2]
. This is equivalently given by acting on
(1,0)
with

M_{\theta}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1}).

This action is an elliptic isometry, a rotation about a geodesic hyperplane. We can see this by examining the fixed points; consider the set
36
36

36

It is easy to check
\infty
is not fixed.
of
\mathbf{V}\in\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
such that
M_{\theta}.\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{V}
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}

\displaystyle=\left(\cos\theta\mathbf{V}-\sin\theta\right)\left(\sin\theta\mathbf{V}+\cos\theta\right)^{-1}\implies\mathbf{V}^{2}=-1.

From Lemma
4.12
, this implies
\mathbf{V}
is a unit vector in
\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\subset\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, and the set of these unit vectors form the boundary of a geodesic hyperplane. The path
M_{\theta}
fixes the points
i_{1},-i_{1},i_{2},-i_{2}
on the boundary and therefore also fixes the geodesics
\gamma_{1}(-i_{1}\to i_{1})
and
\gamma_{2}(-i_{2}\to i_{2})
. The point
(1;0)_{\mathbb{U}}
is on both geodesics, and must stay on both under the action. As the only point where the geodesics meet is
(1;0)_{\mathbb{U}}
, it must be fixed. Because there is a fixed point in the interior, the action is elliptic.

Geometrically, this action looks like a rotation by
2\theta
about the oriented geodesic hyperplane
\Pi_{\gamma}
bounded by the unit
(n-1)
-sphere of pure vectors
\mathbb{R}^{0,n}\subset\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
. Note that if we restrict to the subspace spanned by the vectors
1
,
i_{j}
, and
i_{n+1}
in
\mathbb{U}^{n+2}\subset\$\mathbb{R}^{n+2}
then this looks like rotation about the oriented geodesic from
(-i_{j}\to i_{j})
, which reduces us to the
n=1
case given in
[
23
]
p.18. The frame
W_{1}^{in}
based at
(1;0)_{\mathbb{U}}
is thus rotated by
\pi
about
\Pi_{\gamma}
to become
W_{2}^{in}
.

To arrive at the associated outward spin frame
W_{2}^{out}
, we then rotate
W_{2}^{in}
by
-\pi
about the oriented hyperplane defined by the decorations (as in Definition
4.5
). But the decorations for
(H_{2},W_{2})
are given by
i_{1},i_{2},...,i_{n}
, so this is a rotation about precisely the same hyperplane
\Pi_{\gamma}
! In particular, as the decorations all point along the
i_{j}
, we get the same orientation as
\Pi_{\gamma}
also. So rotating to the outwards spin frame means rotating by
-\pi
, back to the start, and
W_{1}^{in}=W_{2}^{out}
at
(0;1)_{\mathbb{U}}
. As the spin frames align, the lambda length
(H_{1},W_{1})\to(H_{2},W_{2})
is
1
.
\hfill\square

We can compute the general case via the equivariant action of
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
, starting with a slightly more general example. Unless stated otherwise, in the following lemma when we write elements in the tangent spaces to horospheres as elements of
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
the identification is always taken to be the generic basis inherited from the boundary
\partial\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
, or equivalently the restriction
z=

constant
of
\mathbb{U}^{n+2}
to the horosphere.

Lemma 4.14
.

The lambda length
\lambda_{13}
from the spin decorated horosphere
(H_{1},W_{1})=\tilde{\Phi}(1,0)
to
(H_{3},W_{3})=\tilde{\Phi}(0,\mathbf{U})
,
\mathbf{U}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is
\mathbf{U}
.

Proof.
We can write
\mathbf{U}
as
|\mathbf{U}|e^{\theta V}
for a unit vector
V\in\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
,
|V|^{2}=1
(Proposition
1.12
). We then consider the magnitude and rotational components of the lambda length independently, a rotation by
\mathbf{U}/|\mathbf{U}|
and magnitude
|\mathbf{U}|
.

First we send
(1,0)
to
(0,1)
, which (as the lambda length between them is 1 by Lemma
4.13
) matches the inward spin frame of
\tilde{\Phi}(1,0)
to the outward spin frame of
\tilde{\Phi}(0,1)
. If
\mathbf{U}=|\mathbf{U}|
is real and positive then there is no rotation, and the magnitude is a function of the distance from
1
to
\mathbf{U}^{-2}
. The signed hyperbolic distance along the geodesic from the centre of
H_{1}
(which is
\infty
) to the centre of
H_{3}
(which is 0) is then:

d=\int^{1}_{|\mathbf{U}|^{-2}}\frac{dz}{z}=2\ln|\mathbf{U}|,

and
\lambda=e^{d/2}=|\mathbf{U}|=\mathbf{U}
.

We therefore assume
\mathbf{U}\notin\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and we’ll also assume
|\mathbf{U}|=1
as we can simply rescale afterwards. Acting by

M_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{U}^{-1}&0\\
0&\mathbf{U}\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})

sends
(0,1)
to
(0,\mathbf{U})
and therefore sends
\tilde{\Phi}(0,1)=(H_{2},W_{2})
from Lemma
4.13
to
(H_{3},W_{3})
.
We define a path in
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
from the identity to the action
M_{1}
:

M_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-t\theta V}&0\\
0&e^{t\theta V}\end{pmatrix},

for
t\in[0,1]
. As shorthand, write
\mathbf{U}_{t}=e^{t\theta V}
; note also that, as a unit paravector,
\mathbf{U}^{\prime}_{t}=\overline{\mathbf{U}}_{t}=\mathbf{U}^{-1}_{t}
. As per Lemma
1.20
, the action of
M_{t}
as a Möbius transformation on
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
will be a rotation
\sigma(\mathbf{U}^{-1}_{t})
in the plane spanned by
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{U}^{-1}_{t}
; denote the span by
\{\mathbb{R},\mathbf{U}^{-1}_{t}\}
.

M_{t}.\mathbf{V}=\sigma(\mathbf{U}^{-1}_{t})(\mathbf{V})=e^{-t\theta V}\mathbf{V}e^{-t\theta V}.

But this will always be the plane spanned by
\{\mathbb{R},\mathbf{U}\}
, since

\mathbf{U}^{-1}_{t}=\cos(t\theta)-V\sin(t\theta)\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbf{U}\},

and therefore
\{\mathbb{R},\mathbf{U}^{-1}_{t}\}
spans the same plane as
\{\mathbb{R},\mathbf{U}\}
unless
\mathbf{U}^{-1}_{t}
is real. If
\mathbf{U}^{-1}_{t}
is real (implying
\mathbf{U}^{-1}_{t}=\cos(t\theta)
), it is simple to check that the derivative lies within the correct plane and is orthogonal to
\mathbf{U}^{-1}_{t}
, so the path of isometries
M_{t}
really is just a rotation in the
\{\mathbb{R},\mathbf{U}\}
-plane.

If we act on a vector in the plane
\{\mathbb{R},\mathbf{U}\}
:

\sigma(\mathbf{U}^{-1}_{t})(A+B\mathbf{U})=Ae^{-t\theta V}e^{-t\theta V}+Be^{-t\theta V}e^{\theta V}e^{-t\theta V},

then, applying the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula, we see all the commutators vanish (as all the exponents are multiples of
V
):

\sigma(\mathbf{U}^{-1}_{t})(A+B\mathbf{U})=Ae^{-2t\theta V}+Be^{\theta V-2t\theta V}=(A+B\mathbf{U})e^{-2t\theta V}.

This is precisely a rotation by
-2t\theta
in the plane spanned by
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{U}^{-1}
(Lemma
1.20
), from
\mathbb{R}^{+}
towards
\mathbf{U}
. Thus, the action of
M_{1}
sending the spin frame of
\tilde{\Phi}(0,1)
to
\tilde{\Phi}(0,\mathbf{U})
is a rotation in the
\{\mathbb{R},\mathbf{U}\}
-plane by
-2\theta\mod 4\pi
from
\mathbb{R}^{+}
towards
\mathbf{U}
.

But we aren’t trying to map the spin frame of
\tilde{\Phi}(0,1)
to the spin frame of
\tilde{\Phi}(0,\mathbf{U})
; we want to go from
\tilde{\Phi}(1,0)
to
\tilde{\Phi}(0,\mathbf{U})
, and the lambda length is computed in the
W_{1}^{in}
identification. As shown in Lemma
4.13
, the inwards spin frame of
\tilde{\Phi}(1,0)
is identified with the outwards spin frame of
\tilde{\Phi}(0,1)
. But, in our orientation conventions,
1^{in}
of
W^{in}
will point in the opposite direction to
1^{out}
of
W^{out}
. This makes the rotation by
-2\theta
from
\mathbb{R}^{+}
towards
\mathbf{U}
a rotation by
-2\theta
from
\mathbb{R}^{-}
towards
\mathbf{U}
, or a rotation by
2\theta
from
\mathbb{R}^{+}
towards
\mathbf{U}
by our inwards action, which is the rotation taking the spin frame of
\tilde{\Phi}(1,0)
to
\tilde{\Phi}(0,\mathbf{U})
.

To compute the lambda length
\lambda_{13}
we relax the assumption
|\mathbf{U}|=1
and integrate (as above) to find the hyperbolic distance as
2\ln|\mathbf{U}|
, giving

\lambda_{13}=e^{2\ln|\mathbf{U}|/2}e^{2\theta V/2}=|\mathbf{U}|e^{\theta V}=\mathbf{U}.

It isn’t a huge leap to the next generalisation; because
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
is generated by paravectors, we can factor a general
\eta\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
into paravectors and apply the rotations one after the other.

Lemma 4.15
.

The lambda length between the spin decorated horospheres
(H_{1},W_{1})=\tilde{\Phi}(1,0)
and
(H_{4},W_{4})=\tilde{\Phi}(0,\eta)
is
\eta
.

Proof.
Similar to the previous case, we can find the hyperbolic distance between these by integrating along the
z
axis, giving
d=\int_{|\eta|^{-2}}^{1}\frac{dz}{z}=2\ln|\eta|
and
e^{d/2}=|\eta|
.

Parallel transport the inward frame
W_{1}^{in}
along
\gamma
to be based at the intersection of
\gamma
with
H_{2}
. To be precise about spin frames, we need to act by a path of elements in
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
. We already know the lambda length from
(1,0)
to
(0,1)
involves no distance or rotation, so we consider going from
(0,1)
to
(0,\eta)
. Action by

M_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}\eta^{-1*}&0\\
0&\eta\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})

sends
(0,1)
to
(0,\eta)
and therefore sends
\tilde{\Phi}(0,1)=(H_{2},W_{2})
from Lemma
4.13
to
(H_{4},W_{4})
. We factor
\eta/|\eta|
as a product of paravectors and write them in exponential form, giving
\eta/|\eta|=e^{\theta_{k}V_{k}}...e^{\theta_{1}V_{1}}

where the
V_{j}
are unit vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{0,n}
. We can construct a path through
SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
from the identity to
M_{1}
by defining an appropriately staggered set of parameters
t_{j}
:

t_{j+1}=t_{j+1}(t)=\begin{cases}0&t<\frac{j}{k},\\
kt-j&\frac{j}{k}\leq t\leq\frac{j+1}{k},\\
1&t>\frac{j+1}{k},\end{cases}

for
j\in\{0,...,k-1\}
, which gives us the path of transformations

M_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-t_{k}\theta_{k}V_{k}}...e^{-t_{1}\theta_{1}V_{1}}&0\\
0&e^{t_{k}\theta_{k}V_{k}}...e^{t_{1}\theta_{1}V_{1}}\end{pmatrix}

from the identity matrix
I
to
M_{1}
, for
t\in[0,1]
. The action on
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
will be by
\sigma(\cdot)
; for
\mathbf{V}\in\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,

\displaystyle M_{t}.\mathbf{V}

\displaystyle=e^{-t_{k}\theta_{k}V_{k}}...e^{-t_{1}\theta_{1}V_{1}}\mathbf{V}e^{-t_{1}\theta_{1}V_{1}}...e^{-t_{k}\theta_{k}V_{k}}=\sigma\left(e^{-t_{k}\theta_{k}V_{k}}\right)...\sigma\left(e^{-t_{1}\theta_{1}V_{1}}\right)(\mathbf{V}).

From Lemma
1.20
and Lemma
4.14
we see this is a rotation by
2\theta_{1}\mod 4\pi
in the
\{\mathbb{R},V_{1}\}
plane, followed by a
2\theta_{2}\mod 4\pi
rotation in the
\{\mathbb{R},V_{2}\}
plane, and so on. By the same argument as Lemma
4.14
, this implies a total lambda length of

\lambda=e^{2\ln|\eta|/2}e^{2\theta_{k}V_{k}/2}...e^{2\theta_{1}V_{1}/2}=|\eta|e^{\theta_{k}V_{k}}...e^{\theta_{1}V_{1}}=\eta.

Given a completely general pair of Lipschitz spinors, we can transform them via hyperbolic isometries (which leave the lambda length invariant) to the form of Lemma
4.15
.

Theorem 4.16
.

The lambda length between two spin decorated horospheres with spinor coordinates
\kappa_{1}=(\xi_{1},\eta_{1})
and
\kappa_{2}=(\xi_{2},\eta_{2})
is given by taking their bracket. Properly,
\lambda_{12}=\{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\}
.

Proof.
We can apply an isometry
A\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
to send
\kappa_{1}
to
(1,0)
:

A.\kappa_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\xi_{1}^{-1}&\frac{1}{2}\eta_{1}^{-1}\\
-\eta_{1}^{*}&\xi_{1}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}\\
\eta_{1}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

This isometry affects
\kappa_{2}
as follows:

A.\kappa_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\xi_{1}^{-1}&\frac{1}{2}\eta_{1}^{-1}\\
-\eta_{1}^{*}&\xi_{1}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{2}\\
\eta_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\xi_{1}^{-1}\xi_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{1}^{-1}\eta_{2}\\
-\eta_{1}^{*}\xi_{2}+\xi_{1}^{*}\eta_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

We can then apply the appropriate translation
B\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1})
to send the centre of
A.\kappa_{2}
to 0:

B.A.\kappa_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-(\frac{1}{2}\xi_{1}^{-1}\xi_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{1}^{-1}\eta_{2})(-\eta_{1}^{*}\xi_{2}+\xi_{1}^{*}\eta_{2})^{-1}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}\xi_{1}^{-1}\xi_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{1}^{-1}\eta_{2}\\
-\eta_{1}^{*}\xi_{2}+\xi_{1}^{*}\eta_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\xi_{1}^{*}\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}^{*}\xi_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

This translation leaves
A.\kappa_{1}
invariant. The lambda length can now be computed from Lemma
4.15
as

\lambda_{12}=\xi_{1}^{*}\eta_{2}-\eta_{1}^{*}\xi_{2}=\{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\}.

In its definition, the lambda length picks out a canonical basis for the tangent space (given by the decorations) to allow the action of
\$\Gamma_{n+1}
to be defined. But, as it turns out, the lambda length is independent of the coordinates on the tangent space, up to orientation-preserving and orthogonal changes of basis (meaning it maps positively oriented orthonormal bases to positively oriented orthonormal bases).

Proposition 4.17
.

The lambda length is invariant under orientation-preserving orthonormal changes of basis of the shared tangent space of the horospheres.

Proof.
Take a pair of spin decorated horospheres
(H_{1},W_{1})
and
(H_{2},W_{2})
described by Lipschitz spinors
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
respectively. Consider the lambda length
\lambda_{12}
from
H_{1}
to
H_{2}
, so
W_{1}^{in}
is identified with
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. We change the identification of the tangent space with
\$\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
through the action
\sigma(b)
of some unit element
b\in\$\Gamma_{n+1}
; as
\sigma
gives all orientation-preserving isometries, any orientation-preserving orthogonal change of basis can be written in this form.

Via equivariance, we can describe this rotation on the tangent space by acting on
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
with the element

B=\begin{pmatrix}b&0\\
0&b^{*-1}\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\$\Gamma_{n+1}).

This action on
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
leaves bracket, and thus the lambda length, invariant, as shown in Lemma
1.51
.

4.4.
The Generalised Ptolemy Relations

In the complex
n=1
case described in
[
23
]
, a Ptolemy equation relating the lambda lengths of four horospheres is derived from the Plücker relations of a matrix built from the spinor coordinates of the horospheres. This relation can be generalised, but since we’re now working over noncommutative algebras we cannot use the commutative Plücker relations. We appeal instead to the theory of quasideterminants (see for example
[
14
]
), due to Gelfand and Retakh.

Definition 4.18

(Quasideterminant of a Matrix)
.

Given an
m\times m
matrix
A
with components
A_{jk}
over a (possibly noncommutative) ring, if
A
has a well defined inverse with components
(A^{-1})_{jk}
,
1\leq j,k\leq m
, the
(j,k)
-quasideterminant of
A
is defined as
|A|_{jk}=((A^{-1})_{kj})^{-1}
whenever the element
(A^{-1})_{kj}
is invertible.

\lrcorner

Given an invertible
2\times 2
matrix with possibly noncommutative entries:

A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}

The inverse
A^{-1}
is the following:

A^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}(a-bd^{-1}c)^{-1}&(c-db^{-1}a)^{-1}\\
(b-ac^{-1}d)^{-1}&(d-ca^{-1}b)^{-1}\end{pmatrix},

and then the various quasideterminants are just the inverses of these entries. Specifically, the quasideterminants can be arranged into the following matrix:

|A|=\begin{pmatrix}(a-bd^{-1}c)&(b-ac^{-1}d)\\
(c-db^{-1}a)&(d-ca^{-1}b)\end{pmatrix}.

Definition 4.19
.

Given a matrix
A
, the matrix
A^{(j)}
is
A
with the
j
th column deleted. The left quasi-Plücker coordinates are defined on the
m\times(m+1)
matrix
A
as
q_{jk}^{(s)}(A)=|A^{(k)}|_{sj}^{-1}|A^{(j)}_{sk}|
, with
1\leq j,s\leq m
,
0\leq k\leq m
. The quasi-Plücker coordinates
q_{jk}^{(s)}(A)
are actually independent of the choice of
s
(Proposition 4.2.1 of
[
14
]
).

For a more general
m\times\ell
matrix
B
,
m\leq\ell
, choose a set of indices
1\leq j,k,j_{1},...,j_{m-1}\leq\ell
with
j\not\in I=\{j_{1},j_{2},...,j_{m-1}\}
. Let
C
be the
m\times(m+1)
submatrix of the columns
j,k,j_{1},...,j_{m-1}
drawn from
B
. The left quasi-Plücker coordinates are
q_{jk}^{I}(B)=q_{jk}^{(s)}(C)
.

\lrcorner

To relate this machinery to the bracket and lambda lengths, we recall that a matrix

A_{\ell j}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{\ell}&\xi_{j}\\
\eta_{\ell}&\eta_{j}\end{pmatrix}

has pseudo-determinant
\Delta_{\ell j}=\xi_{\ell}^{*}\eta_{j}-\eta_{\ell}^{*}\xi_{j}
.

Lemma 4.20
.

Consider a matrix whose columns are four Lipschitz spinors:

A=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}&\xi_{2}&\xi_{3}&\xi_{4}\\
\eta_{1}&\eta_{2}&\eta_{3}&\eta_{4}\end{pmatrix}.

The left quasi-Plücker coordinates can be constructed from the pseudo-determinant as
q^{\{k\}}_{\ell j}=\Delta_{k\ell}^{-1}\Delta_{kj}
.

Proof.

Define the submatrices

A_{\ell j}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{\ell}&\xi_{j}\\
\eta_{\ell}&\eta_{j}\end{pmatrix}.

Computing the left quasi-Plücker coordinates (arbitrarily picking
s=1
) gives us

q^{\{k\}}_{\ell j}=|A_{\ell k}|_{1\ell}^{-1}|A_{jk}|_{1j}=(\xi_{\ell}-\xi_{k}\eta_{k}^{-1}\eta_{\ell})^{-1}(\xi_{j}-\xi_{k}\eta_{k}^{-1}\eta_{j}).

Now we compute the right-hand side and compare.

\Delta_{k\ell}^{-1}\Delta_{kj}=(\xi_{k}^{*}\eta_{\ell}-\eta_{k}^{*}\xi_{\ell})^{-1}(\xi_{k}^{*}\eta_{j}-\eta_{k}^{*}\xi_{j}).

We can insert a factor of
1=\eta_{k}^{*}\eta_{k}^{*-1}
between the two factors and rearrange terms to find

\Delta_{k\ell}^{-1}\Delta_{kj}=(\xi_{\ell}-(\xi_{k}\eta_{k}^{-1})^{*}\eta_{\ell})^{-1}(\xi_{j}-(\xi_{k}\eta_{k}^{-1})^{*}\eta_{j})

and because the
(\xi_{k}\eta_{k}^{-1})
terms are paravectors they’re invariant under the
\cdot^{*}
operation.

\Delta_{k\ell}^{-1}\Delta_{kj}=(\xi_{\ell}-\xi_{k}\eta_{k}^{-1}\eta_{\ell})^{-1}(\xi_{j}-\xi_{k}\eta_{k}^{-1}\eta_{j})=q_{\ell j}^{\{k\}}.

With this, we can apply the properties of these coordinates to lambda lengths (as these descend from the pseudo-determinant); one of those properties is a noncommutative Plücker relation.

Theorem 4.21
.

Given four spin decorated horospheres
(H_{j},W_{j})
,
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
in
\mathbb{H}^{n+2}
with lambda lengths
\lambda_{jk}
between
(H_{j},W_{j})
,
(H_{k},W_{k})
, their lambda lengths satisfy

(4.22)

\lambda_{31}^{-1}\lambda_{23}^{*}\lambda_{42}^{-1}\lambda_{14}^{*}+\lambda_{31}^{-1}\lambda_{43}^{*}\lambda_{24}^{-1}\lambda_{12}^{*}=1.

This is the noncommutative counterpart to the Ptolemy equation.

Proof.
The noncommutative (2,4) Plücker relations (
[
14
]
Theorem 4.4.2) are
q^{\{k\}}_{\ell j}q_{j\ell}^{\{l\}}+q_{\ell l}^{\{k\}}q_{l\ell}^{\{j\}}=1
, which we can substitute with pseudo-determinants by Lemma
4.20
.

\Delta_{k\ell}^{-1}\Delta_{kj}\Delta_{lj}^{-1}\Delta_{l\ell}+\Delta_{k\ell}^{-1}\Delta_{kl}\Delta_{jl}^{-1}\Delta_{j\ell}=1.

However, the pseudo-determinant applied to a matrix with two Lipschitz spinors as columns is just the lambda length between them.

\lambda_{k\ell}^{-1}\lambda_{kj}\lambda_{lj}^{-1}\lambda_{l\ell}+\lambda_{k\ell}^{-1}\lambda_{kl}\lambda_{jl}^{-1}\lambda_{j\ell}=1.

By substituting
\ell,j,k,l=1,2,3,4
and applying the identity
\lambda_{\ell j}=-\lambda_{j\ell}^{*}
, we get the claimed result.
\hfill\square

Rearranging the Ptolemy equation as

(4.23)

\lambda_{kj}\lambda_{lj}^{-1}\lambda_{l\ell}+\lambda_{kl}\lambda_{jl}^{-1}\lambda_{j\ell}=\lambda_{k\ell},

we get a kind of holonomy relation, where the lambda length from
k
to
\ell
is the sum of two paths going
k\to j\to l\to\ell
and
k\to l\to j\to\ell
.

Another useful property of these quasi-Plücker coordinates is given in Theorem 4.4.1 of
[
14
]
, the skew-symmetry relation
q^{\{k\}}_{\ell j}q_{jk}^{\{\ell\}}q_{k\ell}^{\{j\}}=-1
. This translates into a relation on lambda lengths.

Proposition 4.24
.

Given the lambda lengths
\lambda_{12}
,
\lambda_{13}
,
\lambda_{23}
between three spin decorated horospheres
(H_{j},W_{j})
for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, the following identity holds:

\lambda_{12}\lambda_{32}^{-1}\lambda_{31}=(\lambda_{12}\lambda_{32}^{-1}\lambda_{31})^{*}.

That is,
\lambda_{12}\lambda_{32}^{-1}\lambda_{31}
is invariant under the reversal map.

Proof.
Using the above skew-symmetry relation, we substitute lambda lengths to find

\lambda_{31}^{-1}\lambda_{32}\lambda_{12}^{-1}\lambda_{13}\lambda_{23}^{-1}\lambda_{21}=-1.

After some rearranging, we find the claimed identity.
\hfill\square

We can also rearrange this skew-symmetry relation and find the following:

\lambda_{13}\lambda_{23}^{-1}\lambda_{21}+\lambda_{12}\lambda_{32}^{-1}\lambda_{31}=0=\lambda_{11}.

This is a special case of the holonomy relation (
4.23
); just set
k=\ell
.
